===Original===
As a final salute to the, on the whole not thrilling landscape, he wetted his forefinger by inserting it in his mouth and held it in the air. It was comfortingly chilly on the exterior, towards his back. Light airs were going right in the other fellows’ faces. It might be only the dawn wind. But if it stiffened a very little or even held, those blessed Württemburgers would never that day get out of their trenches. They couldn’t come without gas. They were probably pretty well weakened, too.… You were not traditionally supposed to think much of Württemburgers. Mild, dull creatures they were supposed to be. With funny hats. Good Lord! Traditions were going by the board!

===Chinese===
算是对这幅总的来说并不动人的风景行个告别礼，他把食指伸进嘴里蘸湿，举到空中。朝着他背后的一侧，凉得让人安心。微微的风，正对着对面那帮家伙的脸吹。也许只是拂晓风。可只要它再硬上那么一点儿，或者哪怕就这么稳住，那帮有福的符腾堡兵（Württemburgers）当天就休想爬出自己的堑壕。没有毒气他们来不了。他们多半也被耗得差不多了。……按老话，你不该把符腾堡兵太当回事。据说他们是种温和、迟钝的畜生。戴着滑稽的帽子。老天爷！老传统全被抛下船啦！

===Original===
He dropped down into the trench. The rather reddish soil with flakes of flint and little, pinkish nodules of pebbles was a friendly thing to face closely.

That sergeant was saying:

“You hadn’t ought to do it, sir. Give me the creeps.” He added rather lachrymosely that they couldn’t do without superior officers *al*together. Odd creatures these Derby N.C.O.’s! They tried to get the tone of the old, timeserving N.C.O. They couldn’t; all the same you couldn’t say they weren’t creditable achievements.

===Chinese===
他跳下堑壕。这微带红色的土壤，夹着燧石片和粉红色的小砾石结核，凑近了面对，是件让人踏实的东西。

那个军士正在说：

「您不该这么干，长官。俺都起鸡皮疙瘩了。」他又带点儿哭腔地补充说，长官们可不能*全*没有。这些德比军士（Derby N.C.O.’s）真是些怪东西！他们学老式混年头的军士那个腔调。学不像；可你也不能说他们不是拿得出手的成就。

===Original===
Yes, it was friendly, the trench face. And singularly unbellicose. When you looked at it you hardly believed that it was part of this affair.… Friendly! You felt at peace looking at its flints and pebbles. Like being in the butts up above Groby on the moor, waiting for the grouse to come over. The soil was not of course like those butts which were built of turfs.…

He asked, not so much for information, as to get the note of this fellow:

Why? What difference did it make whether there were senior officers or not? Anyone above eighteen would do, wouldn’t they? They would keep on going on. It was a young man’s war!

“It hasn’t got that comfortable feeling, sir!” the Sergeant expressed it. The young officers were very well for keeping you going through wire and barrages. But when you looked at them you didn’t feel they knew so well what you were doing it for, if he might put it that way.

===Chinese===
是的，壕壁是亲切的。而且出奇地不带火药味。你看着它，几乎不信它是这场事的一部分。……亲切！看着它的燧石和砾石，你心里太平。像在荒原上格罗比高处的松鸡射击掩体里，等松鸡飞过来。土质当然不像那些草皮垒的掩体。……

他问话，与其说是打听，不如说是要摸摸这家伙的调门：

怎么？有没有高级军官，有什么两样？十八岁往上的谁不行，是不是？他们会照旧干下去。这是年轻人的战争！

「就是差了那股踏实劲儿，长官！」军士这么表达。年轻的军官们领着你钻铁丝网、闯弹幕，挺好。可你看着他们，就是觉得他们不那么明白你们图个什么——恕俺这么说话。

===Original===
Tietjens said:

“Why? What are you doing it for?”

It wanted thirty-two minutes to the crucial moment. He said:

“Where are those bloody bombs?”

A trench cut in gravel wasn’t, for all its friendly reddish-orange coloration, the ideal trench. Particularly against rifle-fire. There were rifts, presumably alongside flakes of flint that a rifle-bullet would get along. Still, the chances against a hit by a rifle-bullet were eighty thousand to one in a deep gravel trench like that. And he had had poor Jimmy Johns killed beside him by a bullet like that. So that gave him, say, 140,000 chances to one against. He wished his mind would not go on and on figuring. It did it whilst you weren’t looking. As a well-trained dog will do when you tell it to stay in one part of a room and it prefers another. It prefers to do figuring. Creeps from the rug by the door to the hearthrug, its eyes on your unconscious face.… That was what your mind was like. Like a dog!

===Chinese===
蒂金斯说：

「哦？你们图个什么？」

离那要命的时刻还差三十二分钟。他说：

「那些该死的手榴弹呢？」

在砾石里挖的堑壕，任凭它那亲热的红橙色，也不是理想的堑壕。尤其防不了步枪火力。壕壁上有裂纹，多半顺着燧石片走，步枪子弹正好顺缝钻。不过话说回来，在这样一条深深的砾石壕里，挨步枪子弹的概率是八万比一。而他偏偏有过可怜的吉米·约翰斯死在他身边，就是这么一颗子弹。这么算，他就有大约 140,000 比 1 的安全把握。他真希望自己的脑子别没完没了地演算。它专在你不知不觉间干这营生。就像一条训练有素的狗，你叫它待在屋子一头，它偏爱另一头。它偏爱演算。从门边地毯蹭到炉边地毯，眼睛盯着你毫无察觉的脸。……你的脑子就是这么个东西。像条狗！

===Original===
The Sergeant said:

“They do say the first consignment of bombs was it not smashed. Hin a gully; well behind the line. Another was coming down.”

“Then you’d better whistle,” Tietjens said. “Whistle for all you’re worth.”

The Sergeant said:

“Fer a wind, sir? Keep the ’Uns’ beck, sir?”

===Chinese===
军士说：

「他们倒是说，头一批手榴弹不是叫炮火给砸烂了嘛。在一条沟子里；离前线老远的后方。另一批正往下来呢。」

「那你最好吹口哨，」蒂金斯说。「可着劲儿吹。」

军士说：

「求风使吗，长官？好把德国佬吹回去，长官？」

===Original===
Looking up at the whitewash cockscomb Tietjens lectured the sergeant on gas. He always *had* said, and he said now, that the Germans had ruined themselves with their gas.…

He went on lecturing that Sergeant on gas.…

He considered his mind: it was alarming him. All through the war he had had one dread—that a wound, the physical shock of a wound, would cause his mind to fail. He was going to be hit behind the collarbone. He could feel the spot; not itching but the blood pulsing just a little warmer. Just as you can become conscious of the end of your nose if you think about it!

The Sergeant said that ’e wished ’e could *feel* the Germans ’ad ruined theirselves: they seemed to be drivin’ us into the Channel. Tietjens gave his reasons. They were driving us. But not fast enough. Not fast enough. It was a race between our disappearance and their endurance. They had been hung up yesterday by the wind: they were as like as not going to be held up today.… They were not going fast enough. They could not keep it up.

The Sergeant said ’e wished, sir, you’d tell the men that. That was what the men ought to be told: not the stuff that was hin Divisional Comic Cuts and the ’ome pipers.…

===Chinese===
蒂金斯仰望着那片白灰鸡冠，给军士上了一课毒气经。他一向*就*说，此刻又说：德国人栽就栽在他们的毒气上。……

他接着给那军士上毒气经。……

他检视自己的脑子：它让他心惊。整个战争期间他只有一桩怕事——怕一处伤口、伤口带来的肉体震荡，会让他的脑子垮掉。他要挨的就在锁骨后面。他能觉出那个点；不发痒，只是血在那儿跳得略热一点点。就像你一想到，就能意识到自己鼻尖的存在！

军士说，他倒*希望*他能觉出德国人栽了：他们这架势，像是要把咱们赶进海峡。蒂金斯摆出他的道理。他们在赶我们。可不够快。不够快。这是一场赛跑：一边是我们的覆灭，一边是他们的耐力。昨天他们被风拖住了；今天他们八成还要被拖。……他们前进得不够快。他们撑不住的。

军士说，他盼着，长官，您把这话讲给弟兄们听。弟兄们该听的就是这个：不是师部滑稽画报（Divisional Comic Cuts）上和国内那几张破报上的货色。……

===Original===
A key-bugle of singular sweetness—at least Tietjens supposed it to be a key-bugle, for he knew the identities of practically no wind-instruments; it was certainly not a cavalry bugle, for there were no cavalry and even no Army Service Corps at all near—a bugle, then, of astounding sweetness made some remarks to the cool, wet dawn. It induced an astonishingly melting mood. He remarked:

“Do you mean to say, then, that your men, Sergeant, are really damned heroes? I suppose they are!”

===Chinese===
一柄音色绝甜的军号（key-bugle）——至少蒂金斯猜它是军号，因为各种管乐器的名目他几乎一概不识；反正不是骑兵号，因为附近既没有骑兵，连陆军辎重队（Army Service Corps）都没有——那么，是一柄甜得惊人的号，向清凉湿润的黎明说了几句话。它引来一种出奇让人心软的情绪。他说：

「那么你是说，军士，你的弟兄们真是些不要命的英雄？我估摸着还真是！」

===Original===
He said “your men,” instead of “our” or even “the” men, because he had been till the day before yesterday merely the second-in-command—and was likely to be tomorrow again merely the perfectly inactive second-in-command of what was called a ragtime collection that was astonishingly a clique and mutely combined to regard him as an outsider. So he really regarded himself as rather a spectator; as if a railway passenger had taken charge of a locomotive whilst the engine-driver had gone to have a drink.

The Sergeant flushed with pleasure. “Hit was,” he said, “good to ’ave prise from Regular officers.” Tietjens said that he was not a Regular. The Sergeant stammered:

“*Hain’t* you, sir, a Ranker. The men all thinks you are a promoted Ranker.”

===Chinese===
他说「你的弟兄们」，而不说「我们的」、甚至不说「这支部队的」，是因为直到前天他还不过是个二把手（second-in-command）——而且明天多半又会变回一个全然无所事事的二把手，管的据称是一支「拉格泰姆」大杂烩，那居然还是个抱团的小圈子，齐心憋着拿他当外人。所以他着实把自己看作半个看客；好比司机下馆子喝酒去了，一个火车乘客被拉来照看机车。

军士喜得红了脸。「能让，」他说，「正规军的长官夸奖，是桩好事。」蒂金斯说他不是正规军的。军士结结巴巴：

「您*不*是，长官，行伍出身？弟兄们都当您是行伍提上来的。」

===Original===
No, Tietjens said, he was not a promoted Ranker. He added, after consideration, that he was a militiaman. The men would have, by the will of chance, to put up with his leadership for at least that day. They might as well feel as good about it as they could—as settled in their stomachs! It certainly made a difference that the men should feel assured about their officers: what exact difference there was no knowing. This crowd was not going to get any satisfaction out of being led by a “gentleman.” They did not know what a gentleman was: a quite un-feudal crowd. Mostly Derby men. Small drapers, rate-collectors’ clerks; gas-inspectors. There were even three music-hall performers, two scene shifters and several milkmen.

It was another tradition that was gone. Still, they desired the companionship of elder, heavier men who had certain knowledges. A militiaman probably filled the bill! Well, he was that, officially!

He glanced aside and upwards at the whitewash cockscomb. He regarded it carefully. And with amusement. He knew what it was that had made his mind take the particular turn it had insisted on taking.… The picks going in the dark under the H.Q. dugout in the Cassenoisette section. The men called it Crackerjack.

===Chinese===
不是，蒂金斯说，他不是行伍提上来的。他想了想，又补了一句：他是民兵。凭着运气的意思，弟兄们至少那一天还得凑合接受他的带领。他们不妨尽量往好里想——心里落定为好！弟兄们对军官心里有底，肯定有差别：什么差别，天知道。这伙人可从「绅士」的带领里得不着什么满足。他们不知道绅士是什么东西：一伙毫无封建气的乌合之众。多半是德比兵。小布店老板、收税员的文员、查煤气表的。居然还有三个杂耍场艺人、两个换布景的，外加好几个送奶的。

又一条老传统完蛋了。不过，他们到底还是愿意有上年岁、有分量、见过些世面的男人做伴。一个民兵多半正合格！好吧，他官面上就算是个民兵！

他斜眼往上瞟那片白灰鸡冠。他细细端详它。还带着玩味。他知道是什么让他脑子拐上了它认准要拐的那个弯。……营部掩蔽部底下黑暗里响着的镐头。在卡斯努阿塞特（Cassenoisette）防区。弟兄们管它叫「胡桃夹子」（Crackerjack）。

===Original===
He had been all his life familiar with the idea of picks going in the dark, underground. There is no North Country man who is not. All through that country, if you awake at night you hear the sound, and always it appears supernatural. You know it is the miners, at the pit-face, hundreds and hundreds of feet down.

But just because it was familiar it was familiarly rather dreadful. Haunting. And the silence had come at a bad moment. After a perfect hell of noise; after so much of noise that he had been forced to ascend the slippery clay stairs of the dugout.… And heaven knew if there was one thing that on account of his heavy-breathing chest he loathed, it was slippery clay… he had been forced to pant up those slippery stairs.… His chest had been much worse, then… two months ago!

Curiosity had forced him up. And no doubt **fear**. The large battle fear; not the constant little, haunting misgivings. God knew! Curiosity or fear. In terrific noise; noise like the rushing up of innumerable noises determined not to be late, whilst the earth rocks or bumps or quakes or protests, you cannot be very coherent about your thoughts. So it might have been cool curiosity or it might have been sheer panic at the thought of being buried alive in that dugout, its mouth sealed up. Anyhow, he had gone up from the dugout where in his capacity of second-in-command, detested as an interloper by his C.O., he had sat ignominiously in that idleness of the second-in-command that it is in the power of the C.O. to inflict. He was to sit there till the C.O. dropped dead: then, however much the C.O. might detest him, to step into his shoes. Nothing the C.O. could do could stop that. In the meantime, as long as the C.O. existed the second-in-command must be idle; he would be given nothing to do. For fear he got kudos!

===Chinese===
他这辈子对黑暗地底响镐这念头太熟悉了。北方人没有不熟的。在那片地方，你夜里一醒就能听见这声音，而且它永远显得超自然。你知道那是矿工，在掌子面上，几百几百英尺的地底。

可正因为熟，它才熟得相当可怕。缠人。而那阵静默来得不是时候。在一场地道的地狱喧嚣之后；喧嚣凶到把他逼上了掩蔽部那黏滑的黏土台阶。……天知道，要是有一件他因为那副喘不匀的胸口而恨之入骨的东西，那就是滑黏土……他不得不喘着爬上那些滑台阶。……那时他的胸口还糟得多……两个月前！

是好奇心把他逼上去的。还有，毫无疑问，**恐惧**。大战场的大恐惧；不是那种缠人的、时时刻刻的小疑惧。天知道！好奇抑或恐惧。在吓死人的喧嚣里；那喧嚣像无数怕迟到的声响一齐往前赶，大地又是摇、又是磕、又是震、又是抗议，你的念头没法条理。所以那也许是冷静的好奇，也许是一想到要被活埋在那掩蔽部里、洞口封死，就纯粹是恐慌。反正，他从掩蔽部上去了——在那底下，他以二把手的身份，被他的指挥官（C.O.）当外来的野种恨着，坐在二把手才享有的那种窝囊清闲里，那清闲是指挥官有权加诸他的。他要一直坐到指挥官倒毙为止：到那时，任凭指挥官多恨他，他也继承衣钵。指挥官无可奈何。眼么前儿，只要指挥官还在，二把手就得闲着；什么都不派给他。就怕他捞着功劳！

===Original===
Tietjens flattered himself that he cared nothing about kudos. He was still Tietjens of Groby: no man could give him anything, no man could take anything from him. He flattered himself that he in no way feared death, pain, dishonour, the after-death, feared very little disease—except for choking sensations!… But his Colonel got in on him.

He had no disagreeable feelings, thinking of the Colonel. A good boy, as boys go: perfectly warranted in hating his second-in-command.… There are positions like that! But the fellow got in on him. He shut him up in that reeling cellar. And, of course, you might lose control of your mind in a reeling cellar where you cannot hear your thoughts. If you cannot hear your thoughts how the hell are you going to tell what your thoughts are doing?

You couldn’t hear. There was an orderly with fever or shell-shock or something—a rather favourite orderly of the orderly room—asleep on a pile of rugs. Earlier in the night Orderly Room had asked permission to dump the boy in there because he was making such a beastly row in his sleep that they could not hear themselves speak and they had a lot of paper work to do. They could not tell what had happened to the boy, whom they liked. The acting Sergeant-Major thought he must have got at some methylated spirits.

===Chinese===
蒂金斯自诩不把功劳放在心上。他照旧是格罗比的蒂金斯：没人能给他什么，也没人能夺走他什么。他自诩绝不怕死、不怕痛、不怕耻辱、不怕身后事，连疾病都很少怕——除了怕堵得慌的感觉！……可他的上校钻了他的空子。

想起上校，他并无恶感。一个不坏的小伙子，就小伙子而论：恨他的二把手恨得完全在理。……世上有这种处境！可那家伙钻了他的空子。把他关在那间天旋地转的地窖里。当然，在一间听不见自己念头的天旋地转的地窖里，你可能会失去对脑子的掌控。你连自己的念头都听不见，又他娘的怎么知道念头在干什么？

你听不见。有个勤务兵发着烧还是震颤症还是怎么——文书室挺喜欢的一个勤务兵——睡在一堆毯子上。头半夜文书室曾求准把这小子塞进来，因为他睡觉闹得实在太凶，他们连自己说话都听不见，还有一大堆文书要做。他们也说不清这小子出了什么事，他们挺喜欢他。代理军士长估摸他准是偷喝了甲基化酒精。

===Original===
Immediately, that strafe had begun. The boy had lain, his face to the light of the lamp, on his pile of rugs—army blankets, that is to say.… A very blond boy’s face, contorted in the strong light, shrieking—positively shrieking obscenities at the flame. But with his eyes shut. And two minutes after that strafe had begun you could see his lips move, that was all.

Well, he, Tietjens had gone up. Curiosity or fear? In the trench you could see nothing and noise rushed like black angels gone mad; solid noise that swept you off your feet.… Swept your brain off its feet. Something else took control of it. You became second-in-command of your own soul. Waiting for its C.O. to be squashed flat by the direct hit of a four point two before you got control again.

There was nothing to see; mad lights whirled over the black heavens. He moved along the mud of the trench. It amazed him to find that it was raining. In torrents. You imagined that the heavenly powers in decency suspended their activities at such moments. But there was positively lightning. They didn’t! A Verey light or something extinguished *that*: not very efficient lightning, really. Just at that moment he fell on his nose at an angle of forty-five degrees against some squashed earth where, as he remembered, the parapet had been revetted. The trench had been squashed in. Level with the outside ground. A pair of boots emerged from the pile of mud. How the deuce did the fellow get into that position?

===Chinese===
紧跟着，那场炮击就开始了。那小子脸朝着灯光，躺在他的毯子堆上——就是说，军毯。……一张极金发的脸，在强光里扭曲着，尖叫——冲着火苗实实在在尖叫着脏话。可眼睛闭着。炮击开始两分钟后，你只能看见他嘴唇动，仅此而已。

于是他，蒂金斯，上去了。好奇还是恐惧？在堑壕里你什么也看不见，喧嚣像发了疯的黑天使横冲直撞；实心的喧嚣，把你整个人卷走。……把你的脑子卷下脚。别的什么东西接管了它。你沦为自己灵魂的二把手。只等它的指挥官被一发四点二直接命中、压成一张饼，你再收回控制权。

没什么可看的；疯狂的火光在黑色的天穹上打旋。他沿着堑壕的泥往前挪。他吃惊地发现天在下雨。瓢泼大雨。你还以为上天的诸神这种时刻总会体面地歇手。可居然还打闪电。他们不歇！一道维利信号弹还是什么把*闪电*灭了：说实在的，那闪电也真没多大道行。恰在那时，他鼻子着地、以四十五度角扑在一片压塌的土上——按他的记忆，那儿原先是对胸墙做过被覆的。堑壕被砸塌了。跟外面的地面齐平。一双靴子从泥堆里戳出来。那家伙到底怎么摆成那个姿势的？

===Original===
Broadside on to the hostilities in progress!… But, naturally, he had been running along the trench when that stuff buried him. Clean buried, anyhow. The obliging Verey light showed to Tietjens, just level with his left hand, a number of small smoking fragments. The white smoke ran level with the ground in a stiff breeze. Other little patches of smoke added themselves quickly. The Verey light went out. Things were coming over. Something hit his foot; the heel of his boot. Not unpleasantly, a smarting feeling as if his sole had been slapped.

It suggested itself to him, under all the noise, that there being no parapet there… He got back into the trench towards the dugout, skating in the sticky mud. The duckboards were completely sunk in it. In the whole affair it was the slippery mud he hated most. Again a Verey light obliged, but the trench being deep there was nothing to see except the backside of a man. Tietjens said:

“If he’s wounded… Even if he’s dead one ought to pull him down.… And get the Victoria Cross!”

===Chinese===
正侧身对着打得正酣的战事！……不过，自然而然，他被埋那会儿正沿着堑壕跑来着。反正埋了个严实。那道殷勤的维利信号弹给蒂金斯照见，恰与他左手齐平，好些冒着烟的小碎片。白烟贴着地面平跑，风头很硬。别的小股烟很快凑了上来。维利信号弹灭了。东西正纷纷过来。什么东西打中了他的脚；靴子后跟。不很难受，一阵火辣辣，像脚底板挨了一巴掌。

在满耳喧嚣底下，他忽然想到：那儿既然没了胸墙……他折回堑壕往掩蔽部去，在黏泥里溜冰似的滑。木排道整个沉在泥里头。这整场事里，他最恨的就是这滑泥。又一发维利信号弹殷勤照来，可壕深，除了一个人的屁股什么也看不见。蒂金斯说：

「他要是伤了……就算死了，也该把他拽下来。……好领一枚维多利亚十字勋章！」

===Original===
The figure slid down into the trench. Speedily, with drill-movements, engrossed, it crammed two clips of cartridges into a rifle correctly held at the loading angle. In a rift of the noise, like a crack in the wall of a house, it remarked:

“Can’t reload lying up there, sir. Mud gets into your magazine.” He became again merely the sitting portion of a man, presenting to view the only part of him that was not caked with mud. The Verey light faded. Another reinforced the blinking effect. From just overhead.

===Chinese===
那个人影出溜进堑壕。迅速地、按操典动作、专注地，往一杆端端正正保持着装填角度的步枪里压了两夹子弹。在喧嚣的一道裂缝里，像房子墙上的一道缝，它说道：

「在上头躺着没法装弹，长官。泥会灌进弹仓。」他又还原成一个人的坐部，向你展示他全身唯一没糊泥的部位。维利信号弹暗下去。又一道从头顶正上方，加强了这眨眼的效果。

===Original===
Round the next traverse after the mouth of their dugout a rapt face of a tiny subaltern, gazing upwards at a Verey illumination, with an elbow on an inequality of the trench and the forearm pointing upwards suggested—the rapt face suggested The Soul’s Awakening!… In another rift in the sound the voice of the tiny subaltern stated that he had to economise the Verey cartridges. The battalion was very short. At the same time it was difficult to time them so as to keep the lights going.… This seemed fantastic! The Huns were just coming over.

With the finger of his upward pointing hand the tiny subaltern pulled the trigger of his upward pointing pistol. A second later more brilliant illumination descended from above. The subaltern pointed the clumsy pistol to the ground in the considerable physical effort—for such a tiny person!—to reload the large implement. A very gallant child—name of Aranjuez. Maltese, or Portuguese, or Levantine—in origin.

The pointing of the pistol downwards revealed that he had practically coiled around his little feet, a collection of tubular, dead, khaki limbs. It didn’t need any rift in the sound to make you understand that his loader had been killed on him.… By signs and removing his pistol from his grasp Tietjens made the subaltern—he was only two days out from England—understand that he had better go and get a drink and some bearers for the man who might not be dead.

===Chinese===
在他们掩蔽部口再过去一个转折部，一张小小的少尉的出神脸孔，仰望着一片维利照明，一肘支在壕壁的凸凹处，小臂指天——那张出神的脸孔让人想起《灵魂的觉醒》！……在声音的又一道裂缝里，小小少尉的嗓音申明，他必须省着用维利信号弹。营里奇缺。可同时，掐着点儿让灯不灭也难。……这听着简直荒诞！德国佬眼看就要过来了。

小小少尉用他那指天的手的手指，扣响了那杆指天的手枪。一秒钟后，更辉煌的照明从天而降。少尉把那杆笨拙的手枪指向地面，费了好一番气力——这么个小个子人！——给那件大家伙重新装弹。一个非常勇的孩子——名叫阿兰胡埃斯。马耳他、葡萄牙、或黎凡特血统。

手枪朝下一指，露出了他脚边几乎盘绕着一堆管状的、死掉的卡其肢体。用不着声音里有什么裂缝，你也明白他的弹药手就死在他身上。……蒂金斯连比划带把枪从他手里卸下来，让这位少尉——他离开英格兰才两天——明白：他最好去弄口喝的，再叫几个担架兵来，抬那个也许还没死的人。

===Original===
He was, however. When they removed him a little to make room for Tietjen’s immensely larger boots his arms just flopped in the mud, the tin hat that covered the face, to the sky. Like a lay figure, but a little less stiff. Not yet cold.

Tietjens became like a solitary statue of the Bard of Avon, the shelf for his elbow being rather low. Noise increased. The orchestra was bringing in *all* the brass, *all* the strings, *all* the woodwind, all the percussion instruments. The performers threw about biscuit tins filled with horseshoes; they emptied sacks of coal on cracked gongs, they threw down forty-storey iron houses. It was comic to the extent that an operatic orchestra’s crescendo is comic. Crescendo!… Crescendo! Crrrrresc.… The Hero *must* be coming! He didn’t!

Still like Shakespeare contemplating the creation of, say, Cordelia, Tietjens leaned against his shelf. From time to time he pulled the trigger of the horse-pistol; from time to time he rested the butt on his ledge and rammed a charge home. When one jammed he took another. He found himself keeping up a fairly steady illumination.

===Chinese===
不过，死了。他们把他稍微挪了挪，给蒂金斯那双大出许多的靴子腾地方，他的两条胳膊就在泥里扑塌了两下，那只扣着脸的钢盔，朝天。像一具活动人体模型，只是没那么僵。还没凉。

蒂金斯变成了一尊埃文河畔诗圣的孤像，搁肘子的壁架低了点。喧嚣升级。乐队把*全体*铜管、*全体*弦乐、*全体*木管、全部打击乐都拉了进来。乐手们四下抛掷装满马蹄铁的饼干筒；往破裂的锣上倒整袋整袋的煤，摔下一座座四十层的铁房子。滑稽到什么程度呢，歌剧乐队渐强到顶的那么个程度。渐强！……渐强！渐——强强强。主角*必定*要登场了！他没来！

蒂金斯照旧像莎士比亚似的倚着他的壁架，琢磨着，比如说，考狄利娅的创造。时不时扣一下那杆大手枪的扳机；时不时把枪托往壁架上一磕，捣实一发装药。一发卡了壳，就再换一杆。他发现自己竟维持着相当稳定的照明。

===Original===
The Hero arrived. Naturally, he was a Hun. He came over, all legs and arms going, like a catamount; struck the face of the parados, fell into the trench on the dead body, with his hands to his eyes, sprang up again and danced. With heavy deliberation Tietjens drew his great trench-knife rather than his revolver? Why? The butcher-instinct? Or trying to think himself with the Exmoor staghounds. The man’s shoulders had come heavily on him as he had rebounded from the parados-face. He felt outraged. Watching that performing Hun he held the knife pointed and tried to think of the German for *Hands Up*. He imagined it to be *Hoch die Haende*! He looked for a nice spot in the Hun’s side.

His excursion into a foreign tongue proved supererogatory. The German threw his arms abroad, his—considerably mashed!—face to the sky.

===Chinese===
主角登场了。自然而然，是个德国佬。他翻过来，四肢全在划拉，像只大山猫；撞上背墙的墙面，一跟头栽进堑壕，正砸在尸首上，双手捂着眼，又蹦起来跳舞。蒂金斯带着沉沉的从容，抽出他那把大战壕刀，而不是左轮枪？为什么？屠夫本能？还是想把自己想成跟埃克斯穆尔猎鹿犬在一块儿。那人从背墙面弹回来时，肩膀重重砸在他身上。他觉得受了冒犯。他盯着那个表演的德国佬，端平了刀尖，努力想「举起手来」的德语怎么说。他想大概是 *Hoch die Haende*（举起手来）！他在德国佬的肋上找一块好下手的地方。

他这次外语远征证明纯属多余。那德国佬双臂朝外一摊，他那——砸得相当烂了！——的脸朝天。

===Original===
Always dramatic, Cousin Fritz! Too dramatic, really.

He fell, crumpling, into his untidy boots. Nasty boots, all crumpled too, up the calves! But he didn’t say *Hoch der Kaiser*, or *Deutschland uber alles*, or anything valedictory.

===Chinese===
天生爱演，弗里茨表弟！实在太爱演了。

他倒下去，缩成一团，倒进他那双邋遢靴子里。糟糕的靴子，也全皱着，一直皱到小腿！可他没说 *Hoch der Kaiser*（德皇万岁），也没唱《德意志高于一切》（*Deutschland uber alles*），或任何诀别的话。

===Original===
Tietjens fired another light upwards and filled in another charge, then, down on his hams in the mud he squatted over the German’s head, the fingers of both hands under the head. He could feel the great groans thrill his fingers. He let go and felt tentatively for his brandy flask.

But there was a muddy group round the traverse end. The noise reduced itself to half. It was bearers for the corpse. And the absurdly wee Aranjuez and a new loader.… In those days they had not been so short of men! Shouts were coming along the trench. No doubt other Huns were in.

Noise reduced itself to a third. A bumpy diminuendo. Bumpy! Sacks of coal continued to fall down the stairs with a regular cadence; more irregularly, Bloody Mary, who was just behind the trench, or seemed like it, shook the whole house as you might say and there were other naval howitzers or something, somewhere.

===Chinese===
蒂金斯朝天又打了一发照明，又捣实了一发装药，随后蹲在泥里，跨在德国佬的头上方，双手手指垫在头底下。他能感到那阵阵大声呻吟从指尖颤过来。他撒开手，试探着去摸他的白兰地酒壶。

可转折部那头围上了一伙泥人。喧嚣减了一半。是来抬尸首的担架兵。还有小得离谱的阿兰胡埃斯和一个新弹药手。……那时候人还没这么缺！喊声顺着堑壕传来。无疑又有别的德国佬进壕了。

喧嚣减到三分之一。一段磕磕绊绊的渐弱。磕磕绊绊！整袋整袋的煤照旧按着匀整的节拍顺着楼梯往下滚；更没个准头的是「血腥玛丽」（Bloody Mary），它就在堑壕正后方，或者像是在那儿，可以说把整栋房子都摇了；还有些别的舰载榴弹炮还是什么玩意儿，在别的什么地方。

===Original===
Tietjens said to the bearers:

“Take the Hun first. He’s alive. Our man’s dead.” He was quite remarkably dead. He hadn’t, Tietjens had observed, when he bent over the German, really got what you might call a head, though there was something in its place. What had done that?

Aranjuez, taking his place beside the trench-face, said:

“Damn cool you were, sir. Damn cool. I never saw a knife drawn so slow!” They had watched the Hun do the *danse du ventre*! The poor beggar had had rifles and the young feller’s revolver turned on him all the time. They would probably have shot him some more but for the fear of hitting Tietjens. Half-a-dozen Germans had jumped into that sector of trenches in various places. As mad as march hares!… That fellow had been shot through both eyes, a fact that seemed to fill the little Aranjuez with singular horror. He said he would go mad if he thought he would be blinded, because there was a girl in the teashop at Bailleul, and a fellow called Spofforth of the Wiltshires would get her if his, Aranjuez’s, beauty was spoiled. He positively whimpered at the thought and then gave the information that this was considered to be a false alarm: he meant a feigned attack to draw off troops from somewhere else where the real attempt was being made. There must be pretty good hell going on somewhere else, then.

It looked like that. For almost immediately all the guns had fallen silent except for one or two that bumped and grumped.… It had all been just for fun, then!

===Chinese===
蒂金斯对担架兵们说：

「先抬德国佬。他还活着。我们的人死了。」他死得相当彻底。蒂金斯俯身看那德国佬的时候留意过，他其实已经没有一颗你所谓的脑袋了，虽说原处还剩点什么。那是什么干的？

阿兰胡埃斯在壕壁旁站定，说：

「您可真够沉得住气的，长官。真沉得住气。我从没见谁拔刀拔得那么慢！」他们眼睁睁看那德国佬跳了一段肚皮舞（*danse du ventre*）！那可怜虫从头到尾被好几杆步枪和小伙子的左轮指着。要不是怕打着蒂金斯，他们八成还会多赏他几枪。有五六个德国佬在各处跳进了这段堑壕。疯得跟三月的野兔似的！……那家伙两眼都被打穿了，这一事实似乎让小阿兰胡埃斯充满了异样的恐怖。他说，一想到自己会瞎，他就会发疯，因为巴约勒的茶馆里有个姑娘，要是他、阿兰胡埃斯的脸蛋毁了，一个叫斯波福思的威尔特郡团的家伙就会把她抢走。他一想到这个就当真呜咽起来，随后又通报消息：这一场据认为是虚惊——他是说，一场佯攻，为了把兵力从真正动手的别处引开。那么，别处八成正闹着挺像样的地狱。

看样子正是如此。因为几乎是立刻，所有的炮都哑了，只剩下一两门还在咕咚咕咚发着牢骚。……这么说，这一切只是为了逗乐子！

---

===Original===
Well, they were damn near Bailleul now. They would be driven past it in a day or two. On the way to the Channel. Aranjuez would have to hurry to see his girl. The little devil! He had overdrawn his confounded little account over his girl, and Tietjens had had to guarantee his overdraft—which he could not afford to do. Now the little wretch would probably overdraw still more—and Tietjens would have to guarantee still more of an overdraft.

But that night, when Tietjens had gone down into the black silence of his own particular branch of a cellar—they really had been in wine-cellars at that date, cellars stretching for hundreds of yards under chalk with strata of clay which made the mud so particularly sticky and offensive—he had found the sound of the pickaxes beneath his flea-bag almost unbearable. They were probably our own men. Obviously they were our own men. But it had not made much difference, for, of course, if they were there they would be an attraction, and the Germans might just as well be below them, countermining.

His nerves had been put in a bad way by that rotten strafe—that had been just for fun. He knew his nerves were in a bad way because he had a ghostly visit from 09 Evans, a fellow whose head had been smashed, as it were, on his, Tietjen’s, own hands, just after Tietjens had refused him home leave to go and get killed by a prizefighter who had taken up with his, 09 Evans’, wife. It was complicated, but Tietjens wished that fellows who wished to fall on him when they were stopping things would choose to stop things with something else than their heads. That wretched Hun dropping on his shoulder, when, by the laws of war, he ought to have been running back to his own lines, had given him, Tietjens, a jar that still shook his whole body. And, of course, a shock. The fellow had looked something positively Apocalyptic, his whitey-grey arms and legs spread abroad.… And it had been an imbecile affair, with no basis of real fighting.…

===Chinese===
唉，他们如今离巴约勒已经近得要命。一两天内，他们就会被打着退过那儿。一路退向海峡。阿兰胡埃斯得抓紧去看他的姑娘了。小鬼头！为了他的姑娘，他把他那笔要命的小账户透支了，蒂金斯只好为他担保透支——这笔账他可担保不起。如今这小混蛋多半还要再透支——蒂金斯就只好再担保更多的透支。

可那天夜里，当蒂金斯下到他独享的那一岔地窖的黑色寂静里时——那时候他们住的还真是酒窖，白垩层里的酒窖，一伸几百码，白垩里夹着黏土层，把泥弄得格外黏、格外讨厌——他发觉睡袋底下洋镐的声音几乎无法忍受。那多半是我们自己人。明摆着是我们自己人。可这也没轻松多少，因为，当然了，他们若在那儿，就是个招炮火的目标，德国佬完全可能就在他们底下，反挖对进。

他那副神经被那场要命的炮击弄糟了——那场纯属逗乐子的炮击。他知道自己的神经糟了，因为 09 号埃文斯（09 Evans）鬼魂似的来看过他——那家伙的脑袋等于是砸在他、蒂金斯自己手上开的瓢，就发生在蒂金斯刚拒绝他回英国休假之后；他要回去，就是要去被一个职业拳击手打死，那拳手勾搭上了他、09 号埃文斯的老婆。事情绕得慌，可蒂金斯只盼着那些存心朝他扑倒的家伙，在「挡住」什么东西的时候，挑一样脑袋以外的什么去挡。那个倒霉德国佬往他肩膀上砸——按战争法，他本该正往自己那边的壕跑——给了他、蒂金斯一下震荡，至今还在摇他整个身子。当然，还有一记惊吓。那家伙当时那副样子，简直就是《启示录》里跑出来的，灰白的胳膊腿四下张开。……况且那是件白痴透顶的事，没有任何真战斗的根基。……

===Original===
That thin surge of whitey-grey objects of whom not more than a dozen had reached the line—Tiejens knew that, because, with a melodramatically drawn revolver and the fellows who would have been really better employed carrying away the unfortunate Hun who had had in consequence to wait half an hour before being attended to—with those fellows loaded up with Mills bombs like people carrying pears, he had dodged, revolver first, round half-a-dozen traverses, and in quite enough of remains of gas to make his lungs unpleasant… Like a child playing a game of “I spy!” Just like that.… But only to come on several lots of Tommies standing round unfortunate objects who were either trembling with fear and wet and sweat, or panting with their nice little run.…

This surge then of whitey-grey objects, sacrificed for fun, was intended… was intended ulti… ultim… then…

A voice, just under his camp-bed, said:

“*Bringt dem Hauptmann eine Kerze.…*” As who should say: “Bring a candle for the Captain.…” Just like that! A dream!

It hadn’t been as considerable of a shock as you might have thought to a man just dozing off. Not really as bad as the falling dream: but quite as awakening.… His mind had resumed that sentence.

===Chinese===
那一小股灰白色的物件，摸到壕跟前的不过十来个——这个蒂金斯知道，因为，手里端着一把戏剧情节十足的左轮枪，还带着一帮其实更该去抬走那个倒霉德国佬的家伙——那德国佬因此多等了半个钟头才有人照管——那帮家伙扛着米尔斯手榴弹，像扛着梨子的人，他领着头，左轮枪在前，绕过五六个转折部闪转腾挪，毒气余味还浓得很，呛得他肺里难受……像个玩「我发现你啦！」的孩子。就是这么回事。……结果只撞见好几堆汤姆米大兵，围着些倒霉的物件站着，那些物件不是吓得发抖、湿透了雨水和汗，就是为刚才那趟漂亮小跑直喘。……

于是，这一小股为逗乐子牺牲掉的灰白物件，本意是……本意是最——最——那么……

一副嗓音，就在他行军床底下，说：

「*Bringt dem Hauptmann eine Kerze.*（给上尉拿一支蜡烛来。……）」就像有人会说：「给上尉拿支蜡烛。……」就这么回事！一个梦！

对一个刚要睡着的人，那算不上你可能以为的那种大惊吓。真论起来不如坠落梦可怕：可一样叫人惊醒。……他的脑子把那句话续上了。

===Original===
The handful of Germans who had reached the trench, had been sacrificed for the stupid sort of fun called Strategy. Probably. Stupid!… It was, of course, just like German spooks to go mining by candlelight. Obsoletely Nibelungen-like. Dwarfs probably!… They had sent over that thin waft of men under a blessed lot of barrage and stuff.… A lot! A *whole* lot! It had been really quite an artillery strafe. Ten thousand shells as like as not. Then, somewhere up the line they had probably made a demonstration in force. *Great* bodies of men, an immense surge. And twenty to thirty thousand shells. Very likely some miles of esplanade, as it were, with the sea battering against it. And only a demonstration in force.…

It could not be real fighting. They had not been ready for their spring advance.

===Chinese===
那几个摸到壕里的德国人，是被牺牲给了那种叫「战略」的愚蠢把戏。多半如此。愚蠢！……当然，德国鬼魂点着蜡烛挖坑道，正对他们的路数。过时的尼伯龙根做派。八成是小矮人！……他们在天大的一顿弹幕和名堂底下，送过来那一小撮人。……一顿！*天大的一顿*！那真算得上一场像模像样的炮击了。一万发炮弹怕是不止。随后，在线上某处，他们多半又来了一次兵力示威。*大大*的几股人，一股洪流。两万到三万发炮弹。很像是有几英里的滨海长堤，可以说，海浪正拍打着它。而只是一次兵力示威。……

那不可能是真进攻。他们的春季攻势还没准备好。

===Original===
It had been meant to impress somebody imbecile.… Somebody imbecile in Wallachia, or Sofia, or Asia Minor. Or Whitehall, very likely. Or the White House!… Perhaps they had killed a lot of Yankees—to make themselves Transatlantically popular. There were no doubt, by then, whole American Army Corps in the line somewhere. By then! Poor devils, coming so late into such an accentuated hell. Damnably accentuated.… The sound of even that little bit of fun had been portentously more awful than even quite a big show say in ’15. It was better to have been in then and got used to it.… If it hadn’t broken you, just by duration.…

Might be to impress anybody.… But who was going to be impressed? Of course, our legislators with the stewed-pear brains running about the ignoble corridors with coke-brize floors and mahogany doors… might be impressed.… You must not rhyme!… Or, of course, our own legislators might have been trying a nice little demonstration in force, equally idiotic somewhere else, to impress someone just as unlikely to be impressed.… This, then, would be the answer! But no one ever would be impressed again. We all had each other’s measures. So it was just wearisome.…

===Chinese===
那是打算吓唬哪个白痴的。……瓦拉几亚的、索菲亚的、小亚细亚的某个白痴。或者白厅，很可能。要不就是白宫！……没准儿他们宰了不少美国佬——好在跨大西洋那边讨个人缘。毫无疑问，到那时，整军整军的美国兵团已经摆在线上某处了。到那时！可怜的家伙们，这么晚才进这么一座变本加厉的地狱。糟蹋人心地变本加厉。……就连那一点逗乐子的动静，都比 '15 年挺大的仗还要骇人得多。还是那时候就进场、熬习惯了的好。……只要没把你熬垮，光凭熬的年头。……

也可能是吓唬随便谁。……可谁还吓得着呢？当然，我们那些炖梨脑子的立法大人们，在煤渣地面、红木大门的寒碜走廊里跑来跑去……没准儿就吓得着。……不许押韵！……再不然，当然，我们自己的立法大人们也可能在别处搞了场漂亮的小规模兵力示威，一样地蠢，想吓唬一个一样吓不着的什么人。……那么，这就是答案了！可再也没有谁吓得着谁了。彼此的斤两，大家都摸清了。所以就只剩腻烦。……

===Original===
It was remarkably quiet in that thick darkness. Down below, the picks continued their sinister confidences in each other’s ears.… It was really like that. Like children in the corner of a schoolroom whispering nasty comments about their masters, one to the other.… Girls, for choice.… Chop, chop, chop, a pick whispered. Chop? another asked in an undertone. The first said Chopchopchop. Then *Chup*.… And a silence of irregular duration.… Like what happens when you listen to typewriting and the young woman has to stop to put in another page.…

Nice young women with typewriters in Whitehall had very likely taken from dictation, on hot-pressed, square sheets with embossed royal arms, the plan for that very strafe.… Because, obviously it might have been dictated from Whitehall almost as directly as from Unter den Linden. We might have been making a demonstration in force on the Dwolologda in order to get the Huns to make a counter-demonstration in Flanders. Hoping poor old Puffles would get it in the neck. For they were trying still to smash poor old General Puffles and stop the single command.… They might very well be hoping that our losses through the counter-demonstration would be so heavy that the Country would cry out for the evacuation of the Western Front.… If they could get half-a-million of us killed perhaps the Country might.… They, no doubt, thought it worth trying. But it was wearisome: those fellows in Whitehall never learned. Any more than Brother Boshe.…

Nice to be in poor old Puffles’ army. Nice but wearisome.… Nice girls with typewriters in well-ventilated offices. Did they still put paper cuffs on to keep their sleeves from ink? He would ask Valen… Valen.… It was warm and still.… On such a night.…

===Chinese===
浓浓的黑暗里静得出奇。底下，洋镐还在彼此耳边交换阴森的体己话。……真就是那个样子。像教室角落里的孩子们交头接耳，互相嘀咕着讲老师坏话。……要挑就挑女孩。……嚓，嚓，嚓，一把镐低声说。嚓？另一把压着嗓子问。头一把说：嚓嚓嚓。然后是*嚓噗*。……然后是一段没个准长的静默。……像你听人打字，那年轻姑娘不得不停下来换一页纸。……

白厅里那些摆着打字机的漂亮年轻女人，很可能就口授记录过那场炮击的方案，记在烫压精整、四四方方、压印着王家纹章的纸上。……因为，明摆着，那方案没准儿就是白厅口授的，跟菩提树下大街（Unter den Linden）口授的差不多直接。说不定我们在德沃洛格达河（Dwolologda）上搞了一场兵力示威，就为了引德国佬在弗兰德斯来一场反示威。巴望可怜的老帕夫尔斯挨一闷棍。因为他们至今还想搞垮可怜的帕夫尔斯老将军，搅黄统一指挥。……他们完全可能巴望着，我们在反示威里的损失惨重到全国哭着喊着要撤出西线。……要是他们能叫我们死上五十万，没准儿全国还真能。……他们无疑觉得值得一试。可这真叫人腻烦：白厅那帮家伙从来不长记性。跟德国佬兄弟一个样。……

在可怜的老帕夫尔斯的军队里，真好。好，可是累人。……打字的漂亮姑娘们，坐在通风良好的办公室里。她们还戴纸袖套防墨水吗？他要问问瓦伦……瓦伦。……天气暖，四下静。……这样的夜里。……

===Original===
“*Bringt dem Hauptmann eine Kerze!*” A voice from under his camp bed! He imagined that the Hauptmann spook must be myopic: short-sightedly examining a tamping fuse.… If they used tamping fuses or if that was what they called them in the army!

He could not see the face or the spectacles of the Hauptmann any more than he could see the faces of his men. Not through his flea-bag and shins! They were packed in the tunnel; whitish-grey, tubular agglomerations.… Large! Like the maggots that are eaten by Australian natives.… Fear possessed him!

He sat up in his flea-bag, dripping with icy sweat.

“By Jove, I’m for it!” he said. He imagined that his brain was going: he was mad and seeing himself go mad. He cast about in his mind for some subject about which to think so that he could prove to himself that he had not gone mad.

===Chinese===
「*Bringt dem Hauptmann eine Kerze.*（给上尉拿一支蜡烛来！）」行军床底下一副嗓音！他想，那位上尉鬼魂准是个近视眼：正眯着眼细看一根填塞导火索（tamping fuse）……要是军队里管那玩意儿叫这名字、他们也真用这玩意儿的话！

上尉的脸和眼镜他看不见，就像他看不见自己弟兄们的脸一样。隔着睡袋隔着小腿，是看不见的！他们挤满了坑道；灰白色的、管状的堆团。……大个儿！像澳洲土著拿来吃的那种蛆。……恐惧攫住了他！

他在睡袋里坐起来，淌着一身冷汗。

「天哪，我要完啦！」他说。他想自己的脑子正在垮：他疯了，而且眼看自己疯下去。他在脑子里搜罗一个可以想一想的东西，好向自己证明自己还没疯。

### II / 第二章

===Original===
The key-bugle remarked with singular distinctness to the dawn:

A sudden waft of pleasure at the seventeenth century air that the tones gave to the landscape went all over Tietjens.… Herrick and Purcell!… Or it was perhaps a modern imitation. Good enough. He asked:

“What the devil’s that row, Sergeant?”

The Sergeant disappeared behind the muddied sacking curtain. There was a guardroom in there. The key-bugle said:

It might be two hundred yards off along the trenches. Astonishing pleasure came to him from that seventeenth century air and the remembrance of those exact, quiet words.… Or perhaps he had not got them right. Nevertheless, they were exact and quiet. As efficient working beneath the soul as the picks of miners in the dark.

The Sergeant returned with the obvious information that it was 09 Griffiths practising on the cornet. Captain Mckechnie ’ad promised to ’ear ’im after breakfast ’n’ recommend ’im to the Divisional Follies to play at the concert tonight, if ’e likes ’im.

===Chinese===
军号异乎寻常地清晰，向黎明奏道：

那曲调给这片风景平添了一股十七世纪的气息，一阵突如其来的愉悦漫过蒂金斯全身。……赫里克和珀塞尔！……兴许不过是现代人的仿作。也够好的了。他问道：

「那吵吵嚷嚷的是什么动静，中士？」

中士消失在沾满泥污的麻袋布帘子后面。那里面是一间卫兵室。军号说道：

号声也许来自沿战壕两百码开外的地方。那股十七世纪的调子，连同对那些精确而安静的词句的回忆，给他带来一种讶异的愉悦。……兴许是他记错了词。然而它们确实精确而安静。它们在灵魂底下悄然作业，效率一如黑暗中掘进的矿工的镐头。

中士回来了，带回一条明摆着的消息：是 09 号兵格里菲斯在练短号。麦克凯克尼上尉答应过早饭后去「听听」他，要是瞧得上眼，就把他举荐给师部滑稽剧团，去今晚的音乐会上露一手。

===Original===
Tietjens said:

“Well, I hope Captain Mckechnie likes him!”

He hoped Mckechnie, with his mad eyes and his pestilential accent, would like that fellow. That fellow spread seventeenth century atmosphere across the landscape over which the sun’s rays were beginning to flood a yellow wash. Then, might the seventeenth century save the fellow’s life, for his good taste! For his life would probably be saved. He, Tietjens, would give him a pass back to Division to get ready for the concert. So he would be out of the strafe.… Probably none of them would be alive after the strafe that Brigade reported to be coming in.… Twenty-seven minutes, by now! Three hundred and twenty-eight fighting men against.… Say a Division. Any preposterous number.… Well, the seventeenth century might as well save one man!

What had become of the seventeenth century? And Herbert and Donne and Crashaw and Vaughan, the silurist?… Sweet day so cool, so calm, so bright, the bridal of the earth and sky!… By Jove, it was that!… Old Campion, flashing like a popinjay in the scarlet and gilt of the Major-General, had quoted that in the base camp, years ago. Or was it months? Or wasn’t it: “But at my back I always hear Time’s winged chariots hurrying near,” that he had quoted?

Anyhow, not bad for an old General!

===Chinese===
蒂金斯说：

「好啊，我希望麦克凯克尼上尉瞧得上他！」

他还真希望麦克凯克尼——带着他那双疯眼睛和那口要命的苏格兰腔——能瞧得上那家伙。那家伙把十七世纪的气氛铺展在这片风景上，而太阳的光线正开始往风景上漫过一层黄色的水彩。那么，就让十七世纪救那家伙一命吧，看在他好品味的分上！他的命多半还真有救。他蒂金斯会给他签一张回师部的通行证，让他好生准备音乐会。这样他就躲开了那场炮击。……而旅部通报即将压过来的那场炮击过后，他们这伙人多半没几个还活着。……到这会儿，已经二十七分钟了！三百二十八名战斗兵员，对阵……就算一个师吧。随便什么荒唐数字都成。……得，让十七世纪救下一个人也不算亏！

十七世纪都到哪儿去了？还有赫伯特、多恩、克拉肖，还有「西留尔人」沃恩？……「甜美的一天，如此清凉，如此宁静，如此明丽，正是天与地的婚期！」……天哪，正是这一句！……老坎皮恩——披着少将的猩红金饰军服，闪亮得像只花孔雀——多年以前在后方基地营盘里念的就是这句。还是几个月以前？要不，他念的其实是：「可我总听得见背后时间的带翼马车匆匆逼近」？

不管怎么说，对一位老将军而言，不算坏了！

===Original===
He wondered what had become of that elegant collection of light yellow, scarlet and gilt.… Somehow he always thought of Campion as in light yellow, rather than khaki, so much did he radiate light.… Campion and his, Tietjen’s, wife, radiating light together—she in a golden gown!

Campion was about due in these latitudes. It was astonishing that he had not turned up before. But poor old Puffles with his abominably weakened Army had done too jolly well to be replaced. Even at the request of the Minister who hated him. Good for him!

It occurred to him that if he… call it “stopped one” that day, Campion would probably marry his, Tietjen’s, widow.… Sylvia in crêpe. With perhaps a little white about it!

The cornet—obviously it was not a key-bugle—remarked:

===Chinese===
他纳闷，那一身淡黄、猩红与金饰的雅致行头，后来都落到哪儿去了。……不知怎么，他想起坎皮恩时想到的总是淡黄，而非卡其，因为他周身放光放得太厉害了。……坎皮恩和他——蒂金斯的——妻子，双双放光——她穿着一身金色的礼服！

坎皮恩差不多该到这一带纬度来了。他竟还没露面，倒真是桩奇事。可可怜的老帕夫尔斯带着他那支削弱得不成样子的部队，干得实在太漂亮了，漂亮得没法把他换下来。哪怕恨他的那位大臣开口要求也不行。他好样的！

他忽然想到，倘若他……就说「挨了一下」吧——就在今天，坎皮恩多半会娶他的——蒂金斯的——遗孀。……西尔维娅一身黑绉纱。周身上下，或许再点缀一点白！

短号——明摆着不是军号——奏道：

===Original===
and then stopped to reflect. After a moment it added meditatively:

That would scarcely refer to Sylvia.… Still, perhaps in crêpe, with a touch of white, passing by, very tall.… Say, in a seventeenth century street.…

The only satisfactory age in England!… Yet what chance had it today? Or, still more, tomorrow? In the sense that the age of, say, Shakespeare had a chance. Or Pericles! or Augustus!

Heaven knew, we did not want a preposterous drum-beating such as the Elizabethans produced—and received. Like lions at a fair.… But what chance had quiet fields, Anglican sainthood, accuracy of thought, heavy-leaved, timbered hedgerows, slowly creeping plough-lands moving up the slopes?… Still, the land remains.…

The land remains.… It remains!… At that same moment the dawn was wetly revealing; over there in George Herbert’s parish.… What was it called?… What the devil was its name? Oh, Hell!… Between Salisbury and Wilton.… The tiny church.… But he refused to consider the plough-lands, the heavy groves, the slow high road above the church that the dawn was at that moment wetly revealing—until he could remember that name.… He refused to consider that, probably even today, that land ran to… produced the stock of… Anglican sainthood. The quiet thing!

But until he could remember the name he would consider nothing.…

===Chinese===
随即停住，似在回味。片刻之后，又冥想似的补了一句：

那可不太像是在说西尔维娅。……不过，兴许也穿着绉纱，带一点白，袅袅走过，身量极高。……就说，走在一条十七世纪的街上。……

英格兰唯一让人称心的时代！……可它今天还有什么机会？或者说，明天更有什么机会？这里说的机会，是像莎士比亚的时代所拥有的那一种。或者伯里克利的时代！或者奥古斯都的时代！

天知道，我们并不想要伊丽莎白时代的人搞出的那种擂鼓呐喊的荒唐排场——他们既是炮制者，也是承受者。像集市上的狮子。……可是，安静的田野、英国国教的圣洁、思想的精确、叶片肥厚、杂木成行的树篱、缓缓爬上坡去的耕地——它们又有什么机会？……然而，土地依旧长存。……

土地依旧长存。……它长存！……就在这一刻，黎明正湿漉漉地显露出那片天地；就在那边，乔治·赫伯特的教区里。……那地方叫什么来着？……那鬼名字到底是什么？哦，见鬼！……在索尔兹伯里和威尔顿之间。……那座小小的教堂。……可他偏不肯去想那些耕地、那些厚重的树丛、教堂上方那条徐缓的大路——黎明此刻正湿漉漉地把它们显露出来——直到想起那个名字，他才肯想。……他也偏不肯去想：大概直到今天，那片土地还在源源生出……还在养育……英国国教圣徒的苗裔。那安静的东西！

可在想不起那个名字之前，他什么都不打算想。……

===Original===
He said:

“Are those damned Mills bombs coming?”

The Sergeant said:

“In ten minutes they’ll be ere, sir. **Hay** Cumpny had just telephoned that they were coming in now.”

It was almost a disappointment: in an hour or so, without bombs, they might all have been done with. As quiet as the seventeenth century: in heaven.… The beastly bombs would have to explode before that, now! They might, in consequence, survive.… Then what was he, Tietjens, going to do! Take orders! It was thinkable.…

===Chinese===
他说：

「那些该死的米尔斯手榴弹到底来不来？」

中士说：

「十分钟里头就到，长官。**阿连（A 连）**刚打来电话，说这就在送来的道儿上了。」

这几乎叫人失望：再过一个来钟头，倘若没有手榴弹，他们兴许就统统了结了。像十七世纪一样安静：在天国里。……可如今，那些畜生手榴弹非得在那之前炸响不可了！这么着，他们兴许还能活下来。……那么他蒂金斯到时候可怎么办！听人号令！这倒不是不能想。……

===Original===
He said:

“Those bloody imbeciles of Huns are coming over in an hour’s time, brigade says. Get the beastly bombs served out, but keep enough in store to serve as an emergency ration if we should want to advance.… Say a third. For C and D Companies.… Tell the Adjutant I’m going along all the trenches and I want the Assistant-Adjutant, Mr. Aranjuez, and Orderly-Corporal Colley to come with me.… As soon as the bombs come for certain!… I don’t want the men to think they’ve got to stop a Hun rush without bombs.… They’re due to begin their barrage in fourteen minutes, but they won’t really come over without a hell of a lot of preparation.… I don’t know how brigade knows all this!”

The name *Bemerton* suddenly came on to his tongue. Yes, Bemerton, Bemerton, Bemerton was George Herbert’s parsonage. Bemerton, outside Salisbury.… The cradle of the race as far as our race was worth thinking about. He imagined himself standing up on a little hill, a lean contemplative parson, looking at the land sloping down to Salisbury spire. A large, clumsily bound seventeenth century testament, Greek, beneath his elbow.… Imagine standing up on a hill! It was the unthinkable thing there!

The Sergeant was lamenting, a little wearily, that the Huns were coming.

“Hi did think them bleeding ’uns, ’xcuse me, sir, wasn’ per’aps coming this morning.… Giv us a rest an’ a chance to clear up a bit.…” He had the tone of a resigned schoolboy saying that the Head *might* have given the school a holiday on the Queen’s birthday. But what the devil did that man think about his approaching dissolution?

===Chinese===
他说：

「旅部说，那帮该死的傻瓜德国佬一个钟头后就冲过来。把那些混账手榴弹发下去，但要存够一份，万一咱们要往前推进，就当应急口粮使。……就存三分之一吧。给 C 连和 D 连。……告诉副官，我要沿全部战壕走一趟，让助理副官阿兰胡埃斯先生和文书下士科利跟我一起来。……等手榴弹确实到了就走！……我不想让弟兄们以为，要赤手空拳去挡德国佬的冲锋。……他们定在十四分钟后开弹幕，可不下足一番大功夫准备，他们是不会真冲过来的。……我真不知道旅部这些都是从哪儿知道的！」

「贝默顿」（Bemerton）这个名字忽然跳上他的舌尖。对了，贝默顿，贝默顿，贝默顿就是乔治·赫伯特的牧师宅。贝默顿，在索尔兹伯里城外。……就我们这个种族还值得计较的那部分而言，那里便是它的摇篮。他想象自己站在一座小山上，一个清瘦而好沉思的牧师，望着土地缓缓倾泻而下，直落到索尔兹伯里的尖塔。肘弯下垫着一部装帧粗笨的十七世纪希腊文《新约》。……试想，站在小山上！在这儿，这可是想都不敢想的事！

中士正有点蔫蔫地抱怨着德国佬要来了这件事。

「俺本来寻思着，这帮挨刀的德国佬——长官您担待俺说话粗——今儿早上兴许就不来了呢。……也该让俺们歇口气，归置归置拾掇拾掇。……」他那一副听天由命的小学生的腔调，就像在说校长*原本满可以*在女王生日那天放全校的假。可这个人对自己正在逼近的寂灭，究竟是怎么想的呢？

===Original===
That was the unanswerable question. He, Tietjens, had been asked several times what death was like.… Once, in a cattle-truck under a bridge, near a Red-Cross Clearing Station, by a miserable fellow called Perowne. In the presence of the troublesome lunatic called Mckechnie. You would have thought that even a Movement Order Officer would have managed to send up the line that triangle differently arranged. Perowne was known to have been his wife’s lover; he, Tietjens, against his will, had been given the job, as second-in-command of the battalion, that Mckechnie wanted madly. And indeed he had a right to it. They *ought* not to have been sent up together.

===Chinese===
那是个没法回答的问题。他蒂金斯被人问过好几回，死是什么滋味。……有一回，在一座桥下一节牲畜车厢里，在一处红十字伤兵清理站附近，问话的是一个愁眉苦脸的家伙，名叫佩罗恩。当时那个惹是生非的疯子麦克凯克尼也在场。你会以为，哪怕是个管开拔令的军官，也总该懂得把这么一个三角，错开来了再送上前线。佩罗恩是出了名的、他妻子的旧情人；而他蒂金斯，违背本意，领了营里副职的位子——那正是麦克凯克尼发疯想要的位置。论理，这位置也确实该归他。他们仨*本就不该*被一起送上前线。

===Original===
But there they had been—Perowne broken down, principally at the thought that he was not going to see his, Tietjens’ wife ever again in a golden gown.… Unless, perhaps, with a golden harp on a cloud, for he looked at things like that.… And, positively, as soon as that baggage-car—it had been a baggage-car, not a cattle-truck!—had discharged the deserter with escort and the three wounded Cochin-Chinese platelayers whom the French authorities had palmed off on them.… And where the devil had they all been going? Obviously up into the line, and already pretty near it: near Division Headquarters. But where?… God knew? Or when? God knew too!… A fine-ish day with a scanty remains of not quite melted snow in the cutting and the robins singing in the coppice above. Say February.… Say St. Valentine’s Day: which, of course, would agitate Perowne some more.… Well, positively as soon as the baggage-car had discharged the wounded who had groaned, and the sheepish escort who did not know whether they ought to be civil to the deserter in the presence of the orfcers, and the deserter who kept on defiantly—or if you like broken-heartedly, for there was no telling the difference—asking the escort questions as to the nature of their girls, or volunteering information as to the intimate behaviour of *his*.… The deserter a gipsyfied, black-eyed fellow with an immense jeering mouth; the escort a Corporal and two Tommies, blond and blushing East Kents, remarkably polished about the buttons and brass numerals, with beautifully neatly put on puttees: obviously Regulars, coming from behind the lines: the Cochin-Chinese, with indistinguishable broad yellow faces, brown poetic eyes, furred top-boots and blue furred hoods over their bandaged heads and swathed faces. Seated, leaning back against the side of the box-truck and groaning now and then and shivering all the time.…

===Chinese===
可他们偏偏就都凑在了那儿——佩罗恩垮掉了，主要是一想到再也见不着他蒂金斯的妻子穿金色礼服的模样，除非，也许，她抱着金竖琴立在云端，因为他看事情就是那副眼光。……而且，千真万确，那节行李车厢——那是节行李车，不是牲畜车！——刚把由押送兵看管的逃兵，连同法国当局硬塞过来的三个受伤的交趾支那养路工卸下车。……他们那一整车人到底是要去哪儿？明摆着是上前线，而且已经不远了：快到师部了。可到底是哪儿？……天知道？又是几时？也是天知道！……一个还算晴和的日子，路堑里残着没化尽的点点积雪，上方的小树林里有知更鸟在啼唱。就算二月吧。……就算圣瓦伦丁节吧：这当然会让佩罗恩心里再翻腾几分。……总之，千真万确，那节行李车刚把呻吟的伤兵、把讪讪的押送兵——他们拿不准当着长官的面该不该对逃兵客气——把那逃兵卸完车；那逃兵一路上不停嘴地——挑衅地，或者你爱说，心碎地，反正两者无从分辨——盘问押送兵他们各自的相好都是什么路数，又自告奋勇交代他自己那位的闺房私密。……那逃兵是个吉普赛模样的黑眼睛家伙，一张好讥诮的大嘴；押送兵是一个下士带两名大兵，金头发、红脸膛的肯特东团人，纽扣和铜番号擦得锃亮，绑腿打得漂漂亮亮、整整齐齐：一望便知是正规军，从后方开上来；那几个交趾支那人，一张张宽脸黄得分不清彼此，一双双褐色的诗人眼睛，脚蹬毛皮长靴，缠着绷带的头颅和裹住的脸孔外面罩着蓝色毛皮兜帽。他们坐着，后背靠在货车厢壁上，时而呻吟一声，一直抖个不停。……

===Original===
Well, the moment they had been cleared out at the Deputy Sub. R.T.O.’s tin shed by the railway bridge, the fellow Perowne with his well-padded presence and his dark babu-Hinduish aspect had bubbled out with questions as to the hereafter according to Tietjens and as to the nature of Death; the immediate process of dissolution: dying.… And in between Perowne’s questions Mckechnie, with his unspeakable intonation and his dark eyes as mad as a cat’s, had asked Tietjens how he dared get himself appointed second-in-command of his, Mckechnie’s, own battalion.… “You’re no soldier,” he would burst out, “Do you think you are a b——y infantryman? You’re a meal-sack, and what the devil’s to become of *my* battalion.… Mine.… My battalion! *Our* battalion of pals!”

That had been in, presumably, February, and, presumably, it was now April. The way the dawn came up looked like April.… What did it matter?… That damned truck had stayed under that bridge for two hours and a half… in the process of the eternal waiting that is War. You hung about and you hung about, and you kicked your heels and you kicked your heels: waiting for Mills bombs to come, or for jam, or for generals, or for the tanks, or transport, or the clearance of the road ahead. You waited in offices under the eyes of somnolent orderlies, under fire on the banks of canals, you waited in hotels, dugouts, tin sheds, ruined houses. There will be no man who survives of His Majesty’s Armed Forces that shall not remember those eternal hours when Time itself stayed still as the true image of bloody War!…

===Chinese===
好了，等他们在铁路桥旁副铁路运输官（Deputy Sub. R.T.O.）的铁皮棚子那儿办完移交，那位养尊处优、周身衬垫得虚胖、一副黑黢黢的巴布式印度人相貌的佩罗恩，就咕嘟咕嘟冒出一连串问题：按蒂金斯的说法，来世究竟如何；死亡又是什么性质；寂灭的即时过程：咽气。……而在佩罗恩提问的空当里，麦克凯克尼——带着他那没法形容的腔调和那双疯得像猫一样的黑眼睛——质问蒂金斯，怎么敢谋到他麦克凯克尼自家营里的副职。……「你根本不是军人，」他动不动就发作，「你当自己是他妈的步兵？你就是条装面粉的口袋，我的营往后可怎么办。……我的。……我的营！*咱们*的友伴营！」

那想必是二月里的事，而如今，想必已是四月。破晓的模样瞧着像四月。……这又有什么要紧？……那节倒霉的车厢在那座桥底下停了两个半钟头……置身于那永恒的等待之中——等待，便是战争。你闲耗着，闲耗着，你干跺脚，干跺脚：等米尔斯手榴弹运到，等果酱，等将军们，等坦克，等辎重，等前头的路开通。你在办公室里等，在打瞌睡的勤务兵的眼皮底下等；你在运河岸边顶着炮火等；你在旅馆里等，在掩蔽部里等，在铁皮棚子里、破房子里等。陛下武装部队里但凡活下来的人，没有一个会忘记那些永恒的钟点——那时序本身都凝然不动，恰是血腥战争的真影！……

===Original===
Well, in that case Providence seemed to have decreed a waiting just long enough to allow Tietjens to persuade the unhappy mortal called Perowne that death was not a very dreadful affair.… He had enough intellectual authority to persuade the fellow with his glued-down black hair that Death supplied His own anaesthetics. That was the argument. On the approach of Death all the faculties are so numbed that you feel neither pain nor apprehension.… He could still hear the heavy, authoritative words that, on that occasion, he had used.

The Providence of Perowne! For, when he was dug out after, next night having been buried in going up into the trenches, they said, he had a smile like a young baby’s on his face. He didn’t have long to wait and died with a smile on his face… nothing having so much become him during the life as… Well, a becoming smile! During life he had seemed a worried, fussing sort of chap.

Bully for Perowne.… But what about him, Tietjens? Was that the sort of thing that Providence ought to do to one?… That’s **tempting God**!

===Chinese===
好了，在那档子事里，天意仿佛特意安排了一段不长不短的等待，刚好够蒂金斯去说服佩罗恩这个倒霉凡人：死并不是桩太可怕的事。……他的学识威望足够说服那个黑头发梳得服服帖帖的家伙：死亡自备麻药。论据就是这一句。死亡临头时，全身官能都麻痹了，你既不觉得痛，也不觉得怕。……他还听得见自己当时用的那些沉甸甸、带权威气的话。

佩罗恩的天意！因为，据他们说，第二天夜里往战壕里开进时他被活埋了，后来人们把他刨出来，他脸上挂着一个婴孩般的微笑。他没等多久，含着微笑死了……生前没有什么比这更衬他得了，除了……嗯，一个衬他的微笑！活着的时候，他一直像个愁眉苦脸、忙忙叨叨的家伙。

佩罗恩好样的。……可他蒂金斯自己呢？天意就该这么待人吗？……这叫**试探上帝**！

===Original===
The Sergeant beside him said:

“Then a man could stand hup on an ill.… You really mean to say, sir, that you think a man will be able to stand up on a bleedin’ ill.…”

Presumably Tietjens had been putting heart into that acting temporary Sergeant-Major. He could not remember what he had been saying to the N.C.O. because his mind had been so deeply occupied with the image of Perowne.… He said:

“You’re a Lincolnshire man, aren’t you? You come from a Fen country. What do you want to stand up on a hill for?”

The man said:

“Ah, but you *do*, sir!”

===Chinese===
身旁的中士说：

「这么着，人就能在山包儿上站起来喽。……长官，您是当真说，您觉着人能在个要命的山包儿上站起来。……」

想必蒂金斯方才一直在给那位代理临时军士长打气。他想不起方才对这位军士说过什么了，因为他的心思深深陷在佩罗恩的影子里。……他说：

「你是林肯郡人，对吧？你是沼泽地来的人。还想站到山上去干什么？」

那人说：

「哎，可人就是想呐，长官！」

===Original===
He added:

“You want to stand up! Take a look around.…” He struggled for expression: “Like as if you wanted to breathe deep after bein’ in a stoopin’ posture for a long time!”

Tietjens said:

“Well, you can do that here. With discretion. I did it just now.…”

The man said:

“You, sir.… You’re a law hunto yourself!”

===Chinese===
他又补了一句：

「人就是想站起来！四下里望一望。……」他费劲地找词儿：「就跟弯腰弓背待久了、想痛痛快快深吸一口气似的！」

蒂金斯说：

「这个嘛，在这儿也行。只是得留神分寸。我方才就这么干来着。……」

那人说：

「您呐，长官……您自个儿就是自个儿的法度！」

===Original===
It was the most considerable shock that Tietjens received in the course of his military career. And the most considerable reward.

There were all these inscrutable beings: the Other Ranks, a brownish mass, spreading underground, like clay strata in the gravel, beneath all this waving country that the sun would soon be warming: they were in holes, in tunnels, behind sackcloth curtains, carrying on… carrying on some sort of life: conversing, breathing, desiring. But completely mysterious, in the mass. Now and then you got a glimpse of a passionate desire: “A man could stand up on a bleedin’ ill!”; now and then you got—though you knew that they watched you eternally and knew the minutest gestures of your sleep—you got some sort of indication as to how they regarded you: “You are a law unto yourself!”

That must be hero-worship: an acting temporary regimental Sergeant-Major, without any real knowledge of his job, extemporising, not so long ago a carrier in an eastern county of remarkable flatness does not tell his Acting Commanding Officer that he is a law unto himself without meaning it to be a flattering testimony: a certificate, as far as it went, of trustworthiness.…

===Chinese===
这是蒂金斯整个军旅生涯中所受的最重的一记震动。也是最重的一份报偿。

这些不可解说的生灵全在这儿：士兵阶层，一团褐色的群体，在地下蔓延伸展，像砾石层间的泥灰土层，铺在这片很快将被太阳晒暖的起伏国土之下：他们在坑洞里，在坑道里，在麻袋布帘子后面，撑持着……撑持着某种生活：交谈，呼吸，欲求。但作为群体，全然是个谜。时不时地，你会瞥见一星炽热的渴望：「人就能在一个要命的山包儿上站起来！」时不时地，你也会得到——虽说你知道他们永恒地注视着你，连你睡梦里最细小的手势都看在眼里——你会得到某种迹象，表明他们怎么看你：「您自个儿就是自个儿的法度！」

那准是英雄崇拜了：一个代理临时团级军士长，对本行的门道谈不上真正精通，一路即兴发挥——不久前还是东部一个平坦得出奇的郡里赶车送货的脚夫——若不是存心把这当作一句奉承的证言，是不会对自己的代理营长说「您自个儿就是自个儿的法度」的：这是一纸证书，就其分量而言，写的是可靠。……

===Original===
They were now crawling out into the light of day;… from behind the sacking: six files that he had last night transferred from C to D Coy., D having been reduced to forty-three rank and file. They shuffled out, an extraordinary Falstaff’s battalion of muddy odd-come shorts; fell into some sort of alignment in the trench; shuffled an inch further this way, an inch further that; pushed up their chin-straps and pulled them down; humped up their packs by hunching their shoulders and jerking; adjusted their water bottles and fell into some sort of immobility, their rifles, more or less aligned, poked out before them. In that small company they were men of all sorts of sizes, of all sorts of disparities and grotesquenesses of physique. Two of them were music-hall comedians and the whole lot looked as if they made up a knockabout turn.… The Rag Time Army: at its vocation: living and breathing.

The Sergeant called them to attention and they wavered back and forward. The Sergeant said:

“The Commadin’ Officer’s lookin’ at you. **Fix**… B’ts!”

And, positively, a dwarf concealed under a pudding basin shuffled a foot-length and a half forward in the mud, protruded his rifle-muzzle between his bent knees, jerked his head swiftly to strain his sight along the minute line.… It was like a blurred fairytale! Why did that dwarf behave in a smart and soldierly manner? Through despair? It wasn’t likely!

The men wavered like the edge of a field of tall grass with the wind running along it; they felt round themselves for their bayonet-handles, like women attempting difficult feats with their skirts.… The dwarf cut his hand smartly away to his side, as the saying is; the men pulled their rifles up into line. Tietjens exclaimed:

“Stand at ease: stand easy,” negligently enough, then he burst out in uncontrollable irritation: “For *God’s* sake, put your beastly hats straight!” The men shuffled uneasily, this being no order known to them, and Tietjens explained: “No, this isn’t drill. It’s only that your hats all at sixes and sevens give me the pip!” And the whispers of the men went down the little line:

===Chinese===
他们这会儿正朝天光里爬出来；……从麻袋布帘子后面：六个兵，是他昨儿夜里从 C 连调到 D 连的，D 连打得只剩四十三名列兵。他们拖拖沓沓地挪出来，活脱一支奇特的福斯塔夫麾下之营，人人泥乎乎、七拼八凑；在战壕里勉强排出个大概的队列；又往这边蹭一寸，往那边蹭一寸；把盔带往上推推，又往下拉拉；耸肩一颠一颠地把背囊颠上肩；正一正水壶，勉强立定不动，步枪大致成一线，戳在身前。小小一支队伍里，高矮胖瘦，各种身量的都有，体格上种种参差、种种怪相。里头有两个是杂耍剧场的滑稽戏子，整伙人瞧上去就像凑成了一出打闹滑稽戏。……拉格泰姆军队：干着它的本行：活着，喘着气。

中士喊了立正，他们前后晃了晃。中士说：

「指挥官在瞧着你们呢。**上**……刺刀！」

而且，千真万确，一个藏在布丁盆底下的小矮子，在泥里往前蹭了一步半，枪口从弯曲的膝盖间探出去，脑袋猛地一甩，顺着那细细的一线瞄准。……这简直像一则洇糊了的童话！那矮子干吗这么利落、这么一副军人的做派？是出于绝望吗？不太像！

士兵们摇摇晃晃，像风扫过高草地时草地的边；他们在身上摸索刺刀柄，像女人提着裙子做高难动作。……那矮子把手利落地劈回身侧——照操典口令的说法；士兵们把步枪提到线上。蒂金斯喊道：

「稍息：——放松些，」说得漫不经心，随即又按捺不住地发作起来：「看在*上帝*分上，把你们那些混账帽子戴正了！」士兵们不安地蹭来蹭去——这道命令他们闻所未闻——蒂金斯解释道：「不，这不是操练。只是你们那歪七竖八的帽子，瞧着叫我心里犯膈应！」士兵们的窃窃私语顺着短短的行列传下去：

===Original===
“You ’eer the orfcer.… Gives ’im the pip, we do!… Goin’ for a wawk in the pawk wiv our gels, we are.…” They glanced nevertheless aside and upwards at each other’s tin-hat rims and said: “Shove ’im a shade forward, ’Orace.… You tighten your martingale, Erb!” They were gaily rueful and impenitently profane: they had had thirty-six hours of let-off. A fellow louder-than-hummed:

> “‘As I wawk erlong ther Bor dee Berlong
 

 Wiv an indipendent air… …
 

 W’ere’s me swegger-kine, you fellers!’”

===Chinese===
「听见长官说的没。……咱们叫他膈应了，咱们！……这不，带着咱家妞儿上园子里遛弯儿来着，俺们。……」话虽如此，他们还是斜着眼往上瞟彼此的钢盔檐，说：「往前推它一丁点儿，奥勒斯。……你把你的马缰绳勒紧点儿，厄布！」他们嘻嘻哈哈地懊恼着，一派满不在乎的粗话：他们刚捞着三十六个钟头的假。一个家伙哼出声来，比哼哼响一些：

> 「『俺顺着布洛涅林子遛达呀
 

 一副独立不羁的派头…… …
 

 俺那耍派头的手杖棍儿哪儿去啦，伙计们！』」

===Original===
Tietjens addressed him:

“Did you ever hear Coborn sing that, Runt?” and Runt replied:

“Yes, sir. I was the hind legs of the elephant when he sung it in the Old Drury panto!”… A little, dark, beady-eyed Cockney, his enormous mouth moved lip on lip as if he were chewing a pebble in pride at the reminiscence. The men’s voices went on: “’Ind legs ’f the elephink!… good ol’ Helefink.… I’ll go ’n’ see ’n elephink first thing I do in Blighty!”

Tietjens said:

“I’ll give every man of you a ticket for Drury Lane next Boxing Day. We’ll all be in London for the next Boxing Day. Or Berlin!”

===Chinese===
蒂金斯冲他说：

「你听过科伯恩唱这支歌没有，伦特？」伦特答道：

「听过，长官。他在老德鲁里那出圣诞哑剧里唱这支歌的时候，俺就是那大象的后腿来着！」……一个小个子、黑不溜秋、眼睛滴溜圆的伦敦东区佬，那张大嘴一片嘴唇磨着一片嘴唇，仿佛嚼着一颗石子，为这段往事得意非凡。士兵们的声音还在往下传：「后腿儿，大象儿的后腿儿！……好样儿的大象儿。……俺回了英国老家，头一件事就去瞧大象儿！」

蒂金斯说：

「明年节礼日，我给你们每个人弄一张德鲁里巷的票。下个节礼日，咱们全都会在伦敦。要不就在柏林！」

===Original===
They exclaimed polyphonically and low:

“Oo-er! Djee ’eer ’im? Di’djee ’eer the orfcer? The noo C.O.?”

A hidden man said:

“Mike it the old Shoreditch Empire, sir, ’n’ we’ll thenk you!”

Another:

“I never keered fer the Lane meself! Give me the old Balham for Boxing Day.” The Sergeant made the sounds for them to move off.

===Chinese===
他们七嘴八舌地低低惊叹：

「嗬哎！听见他说没？听见这位长官的话没？新来的指挥官？」

一个看不见的人说：

「换成老肖尔迪奇帝国剧场吧，长官，那俺们就承您的情了！」

又一个说：

「德鲁里巷那地界儿，俺自个儿从来就不稀罕！节礼日还是给俺老巴拉姆那家吧。」中士弄出声响，让他们开拔。

===Original===
They shuffled off up the trench. An unseen man said:

“Better’n a bleedin’ dipso!” Lips said “*S.h.h.h!*”

The Sergeant shouted—with an astonishing, brutal panic:

“You shut your bleedin’ mouth, you man, or I’ll shove you in the b——y clink!” He looked nevertheless at Tietjens with calm satisfaction a second later.

“A good lot of chaps, sir,” he said. “The best!” He was anxious to wipe out the remembrance of the last spoken word. “Give ’em the right sort of officers ’n’ they’ll beat the world!”

“Do you think it makes any difference to them what officers they have?” Tietjens asked. “Wouldn’t it be all the same if they had just anyone?”

===Chinese===
他们拖拖沓沓地顺着战壕走远了。一个看不见的人说：

「可比那个挨刀的酒鬼强多啦！」一片嘴唇出了声：「*嘘——嘘！*」

中士吼了起来——带着一种骇人的、兽性的惊慌：

「你给我闭上你的臭嘴，你这家伙，不然老子把你塞进那该——死的禁闭室！」可一秒钟后，他望着蒂金斯，神情却平静而称意。

「都是好小伙子，长官，」他说。「顶顶好的！」他急着要把方才那句话的痕迹抹掉。「给他们配上对路的军官，他们能打遍天下！」

「你觉得军官是谁，对他们真有那么大分别吗？」蒂金斯问。「随便换个人来带，不也一个样？」

===Original===
The Sergeant said:

“No, sir. They bin frightened these last few days. Now they’re better.”

This was just exactly what Tietjens did not want to hear. He hardly knew why. Or he did.… He said:

“I should have thought these men knew their job so well—for this sort of thing—that they hardly needed orders. It cannot make much difference whether they receive orders or not.”

The Sergeant said:

“It *does* make a difference, sir,” in a tone as near that of cold obstinacy as he dare attain to; the feeling of the approaching strafe was growing on them. It hung over them.

===Chinese===
中士说：

「不一样，长官。这几天他们一直心里发怯。这会儿好些了。」

这恰恰是蒂金斯最不想听的话。他几乎说不上来为什么。或者说，他说得上来。……他说：

「我原以为这些弟兄们对本行当熟得很——对付眼下这种局面——根本用不着什么命令。有没有命令，想来不会差太多。」

中士说：

「*就是*有分别，长官，」用的腔调，是他敢摆出的最接近冷硬执拗的一种；那场迫近的炮击的感觉在他们心头越压越重。它悬在他们头顶。

===Original===
Mckechnie stuck his head out from behind the sacking. The sacking had the lettering P X L in red and the word *Minn* in black. Mckechnie’s Minn eyes were blazing maniacally. Jumping maniacally in his head. They always were jumping maniacally in his head. He was a tiring fellow. He was wearing not a tin hat, but an officer’s helmet. The gilt dragon on it glittered. The sun was practically up, somewhere. As soon as its disc cleared the horizon, the Huns, according to Brigade, were to begin sending over their wearisome stuff. In thirteen and a half minutes.

Mckechnie gripped Tietjens by the arm, a familiarity that Tietjens detested. He hissed—he really hissed because he was trying to speak under his breath:

“Come past the next traverse. I want to speak to you.”

===Chinese===
麦克凯克尼从麻袋布帘子后面探出头来。那麻袋布上有红色的 P X L 字样，还有一个黑字「*Minn*」。麦克凯克尼那双「Minn」眼睛正狂躁地喷火。在他脑袋里狂躁地蹦跳。它们一向就在他脑袋里狂躁地蹦跳。他是个累人的家伙。他头上戴的不是钢盔，而是一顶军官盔。盔上的镀金蟠龙闪闪发亮。太阳差不多快升起来了，在某处。按旅部的说法，太阳的圆盘一冒出地平线，德国佬就要开始把他们那些腻烦人的玩意儿送过来。还有十三分半钟。

麦克凯克尼一把攥住蒂金斯的手臂——这份亲昵是蒂金斯深恶的。他咝咝出声——他是真在咝咝出声，因为他竭力要压着嗓子说话：

「到下一个横隔（traverse）那头去。我有话跟你说。」

===Original===
In correctly prepared trenches, made according to order as these had been to receive them in retreat, by a regular battalion acting under the orders of the Royal Engineers, you go along a straight ditch of trench for some yards, then you find a square block of earth protruding inwards from the parapet round which you must walk; then you come to another straight piece, then to another traverse, and so on to the end of the line, the lengths and dimensions varying to suit the nature of the terrain or the character of the soil. These outjuttings were designed to prevent the lateral spreading of fragments of shell bursting in the trench which would otherwise serve as a funnel, like the barrel of a gun to direct those parts of missiles into men’s bodies. It was also exciting—as Tietjens expected to be doing before the setting of the not quite risen sun—to crouch rapidly along past one of them, the heart moving very disagreeably, the revolver protruded well in advance, with half a dozen careless fellows with grenades of sorts just behind you. And you not knowing whether, crouching against the side that was just round the corner you would or would not find a whitish, pallid, dangerous object that you would have no time to scrutinise closely.

Past the nearest of these Mckechnie led Tietjens. He was portentous and agitated.

===Chinese===
在按操典规规矩矩构筑的战壕里——这些战壕便是如此，原是为撤退时接应他们、由一支在皇家工兵指挥下作业的正规营修成的——你沿一条笔直的壕沟走上若干码，便会撞见一方从胸墙向内凸出的土块，得绕着它走过去；接着又是一段直壕，又是一个横隔，如此直至防线尽头，长短尺寸因地制宜、因土质而变。这些凸出物是为了防止炮弹在壕内爆炸时破片横向飞散——否则整条壕就成了漏斗，像炮膛一样，把弹片径直引进人的身体。这还挺让人心头发热——蒂金斯料想，在尚未完全升起的太阳落下去之前，自己多半要干上这么一遭——猫着腰迅疾地贴着一个横隔跑过去，心脏翻搅得极不舒服，左轮枪远远探在前面，身后跟着六七个吊儿郎当、揣着各色手榴弹的家伙。而你无从知道，猫着腰贴到刚拐过弯的那一面上时，等着的会不会是一个发白的、惨白的、危险的东西——你根本来不及细看它一眼。

麦克凯克尼领着蒂金斯绕过了最近的一个横隔。他一副煞有介事、心浮气躁的样子。

===Original===
At the end of the next stretch of trench, leaning as it were against a buttress in an attitude of intense fatigue was a mud-coloured, very thin, tall fellow; squatting dozing on his heels in the mud just beside that one’s foot was another, a proper Glamorganshire man of whom not many more than ten were left in the battalion. The standing man was leaning like that to look through a loophole that had been placed very close to the buttress of raw earth. He grunted something to his companion and continued looking intently. The other man grunted too.

Mckechnie withdrew precipitately into the recessed pathway. The column of earth in their faces gave a sense of oppression. He said:

“Did you put that fellow up to saying that damnable thing?…” He repeated: “That perfectly damnable thing! Damnable!” Besides hating Tietjens he was shocked, pained, femininely lachrymose. He gazed into Tietjens’ eyes like a forsaken mistress fit to do a murder, with a sort of wistful incredulity of despair.

===Chinese===
在下一段直壕的尽头，一个泥颜色的、精瘦高挑的家伙倚着土垛，一副疲惫欲绝的架势；在他脚边的泥里，另一个蹲踞着脚跟打盹的，是个地道的格拉摩根郡人——全营里这种人只剩十来个出头了。站着的那位所以那样倚着，是为了凑近一个紧挨生土垛挖出的射击孔朝外望。他朝同伴咕哝了句什么，又继续凝神望出去。蹲着的那位也咕哝了一声。

麦克凯克尼仓促地缩回凹陷的通道里。当面那根土柱子给人一种压迫感。他说：

「是你在背后教那家伙说出那句混账话的吗？……」他又重复：「那句彻头彻尾的混账话！混账！」他除了恨蒂金斯，还震惊、痛心，带着一股女人似的泪汪汪。他直勾勾盯进蒂金斯的眼里，像个被弃的情妇、眼看着就要行凶，带着一种哀恳而不肯相信的绝望。

===Original===
To that Tietjens was accustomed. For the last two months Mckechnie whispering in the ear of the C.O. wherever Battalion Headquarters might happen to be—Mckechnie, with his arms spread abroad on the table and his chin nearly on the cloth that they had always managed to retain in spite of three precipitate moves, Mckechnie, with his mad eyes every now and then moving in the direction of Tietjens, had been almost the most familiar object of Tietjens’ night landscapes. They wanted him gone so that Mckechnie might once again become Second in Command of that body of pals.… That indeed was what they were… with the addition of a great deal too much of what they called Ooch.

Tietjens obviously could not go. There was no way of managing it: he had been put there by old Campion and there he must remain. So that by the agreeable irony of Providence there was Tietjens who had wanted above all Mckechnie’s present relatively bucolic job hated to hell by half a dozen quite decent if trying young squits—the pals—because Tietjens was in his, Mckechnie’s, desired position. It seemed to make it all the worse that they were all, with the exception of the Commanding Officer himself, of the little, dark, Cockney type and had the Cockney’s voice, gesture and intonation, so that Tietjens felt himself like a blond Gulliver with hair very silver in patches, rising up amongst a lot of Lilliputian brown creatures.… Portentous and unreasonably noticeable.

===Chinese===
这个蒂金斯倒习惯了。这两个月来，无论营部落脚在哪儿，麦克凯克尼总在营长耳边嘀咕——麦克凯克尼，双臂摊在桌上，下巴几乎贴着那块桌布——三次仓皇转移他们都死保住了这块桌布——麦克凯克尼，那双疯眼睛时不时朝蒂金斯的方向瞟一瞟，几乎成了蒂金斯夜间景象里最熟悉的一件东西。他们巴不得他走，好让麦克凯克尼重新当上那个友伴营的副营长。……他们确实是那么一号人……只是掺了太多太多他们称之为「乌奇」（Ooch）的东西。

蒂金斯显然走不了。没法子安排：是老坎皮恩把他搁在那儿的，他就得待在那儿。于是，托天意那宜人的反讽，蒂金斯——这个最最想要麦克凯克尼眼下这份还算田园牧歌差事的人——被六七个挺不坏、只是够呛的愣头青恨到了地狱里——就是那帮友伴——因为蒂金斯占的是他麦克凯克尼朝思暮想的位置。更糟的是，除营长本人之外，他们清一色都是小个子、黑皮肤的伦敦东区佬类型，一开口一抬手全是东区佬的嗓音、手势和腔调，弄得蒂金斯自觉像一头金发的格列佛、头发东一块西一块地花白，矗立在一群小人国的褐色生灵中间。……煞有介事，又显眼得没有道理。

===Original===
A large cannon, nearer than the one that had lately spoken, but as it were with a larger but softer voice, remarked: “*Phoh.h.h.h.h.h.h.h.h.*,” the sound wandering round the landscape for a long while. After a time about four coupled railway-trains hurtled jovially amongst the clouds and went a long way away.—Four in one. They were probably trying to impress the North Sea.

It might of course be the signal for the German barrage to begin. Tietjens’ heart stopped; his skin on the nape of the neck began to prickle: his hands were cold. That was fear: the **battle fear**, experienced in strafes. He might not again be able to hear himself think. Not ever. What did he want of life?… Well, just not to lose his reason. One would pray: Not that.… Otherwise, perhaps a nice parsonage might do. It was just thinkable. A place in which forever to work at the theory of waves.… But of course it was not thinkable.…

===Chinese===
一门大口径炮——比方才发言的那门近些，嗓门像是更宽厚也更绵软——说道：「*噗·嗬·嗬·嗬·嗬·嗬·嗬·嗬·嗬。*」声音在四野里游荡了好一阵子。过了一会儿，约有四列挂了车厢的火车欢天喜地地呼啸着钻进云堆，跑出去老远。——四发一体。它们大概是想给北海留个深刻的印象。

这当然可能是德军弹幕开始的信号。蒂金斯的心脏停了一拍；后颈的皮肤开始发麻发刺：两手冰凉。那是恐惧：**战斗恐惧**，挨炮击时尝过的滋味。他也许再也听不见自己思考了。永远听不见了。他对人生还有什么想头？……唉，只求别失掉理性。换个人会这样祈祷：别的都可以，唯独别求那个。……除此之外，或许一座舒心的牧师宅也算将就。这并非全然不可想。一个可以永远钻研波的理论的地方。……可这当然不可想。……

===Original===
He was saying to Mckechnie:

“You ought not to be here without a tin hat. You will have to put a tin hat on if you mean to stop here. I can give your four minutes if that is not the strafe beginning. Who’s been saying what?”

Mckechnie said:

“I’m not stopping here. I’m going back, after I’ve given you a piece of my mind, to the beastly job you have got me defiled with.”

Tietjens said:

“Well, you’ll put on a tin hat to go there, please. And don’t ride your horse, if you’ve got it here, till after you’re a hundred yards at least down a communication trench.”

===Chinese===
他正对麦克凯克尼说：

「你不该不戴钢盔就到这儿来。你要是想在这儿待着，就得戴上钢盔。倘若那不是炮击的开始，我能给你四分钟。是谁说了些什么？」

麦克凯克尼说：

「我不是要在这儿待着。等我把心里话给你数落完，我就回去——回到你害我沾了一身腥的那份混账差事上去。」

蒂金斯说：

「那好，请你回去的路上也戴上钢盔。还有，你的马若是牵到这儿来了，至少要下了交通壕走出一百码，再骑。」

===Original===
Mckechnie asked how Tietjens dared give him orders and Tietjens said: Fine he would look with Divisional Transport dead in his lines at five in the morning in a parade hat. Mckechnie with objurgations said that the Transport Officer had the right to consult the C.O. of a battalion he supplied. Tietjens said:

“I’m commanding here. You’ve not consulted me!”

It appeared to him queer that they should be behaving like that when you could hear… oh, say: the wings of the angel of death.… You can “almost hear the very rustling of his wings” was the quotation. Good enough rhetoric.… But of course that was how armed men would behave.… At all times!

He had been trying the old trick of the military, clipped voice on the half-dotty subject. It had before then reduced Mckechnie to some sort of military behaviour.

It reduced him in this case to a maudlin state. He exclaimed with a sort of lachrymose agony:

“This is what it has come to with the old battalion… the b——y; b——y, b——y old battalion of z——rs!” Each imprecation was a sob. “How we worked at it.… And now… *you*’ve got it!”

===Chinese===
麦克凯克尼质问蒂金斯怎么敢对他下命令，蒂金斯答道：要是清晨五点，师部运输队的牲口死在他的防区里，而他还顶着顶阅兵帽，那他可就真够体面的了。麦克凯克尼骂骂咧咧地回说，运输官有权跟他所供应的营的营长接洽。蒂金斯说：

「这儿是我指挥。你并没有跟我接洽过！」

事情显得怪诞：明明听得见……哦，就算听得见死亡天使的翅膀吧……他们却还有心思这么闹。「你几乎能听见他翅膀的真切窸窣」——引文是这么说的。修辞得不坏。……不过，拿枪的人行事当然就是这个样子。……任何时候都是！

他一直在那半个疯子身上试验那套老法子：军人的、斩钉截铁的腔调。从前这一手总能让麦克凯克尼收敛出几分军人的行止。

这一回却把他弄成了个伤感的醉态。他带着一种涕泪交零的痛楚喊道：

「咱们那个老营如今竟落到这步田地……这个挨——炮的；挨——炮、挨——炮的老废——物营！」每一声咒骂都是一声抽噎。「咱们在那上头下了多大的功夫。……到头来……落在*你*手里了！」

===Original===
Tietjens said:

“Well, you were Vice-Chancellor’s Latin Prizeman once. It’s what we get reduced to.” He added: “*Vos mellificatis apes!*”

Mckechnie said with gloomy contempt:

“You.… You’re no Latinist!”

By now Tietjens had counted two hundred and eighty since the big cannon had said “*Pho.o.o.o.h.*” Perhaps then it was not the signal for the barrage to begin.… Had it been it would have begun before now; it would have come thumping along on the heels of the “*Pho.o.o.h.*” His hands and the nape of his neck were preparing to become normal.

Perhaps the strafe would not come at all that day. There was the wind. If anything it was strengthening. Yesterday he had suspected that the Germans hadn’t got any tanks handy. Perhaps the ugly, senseless armadillos—and incapable at that! Under-engined!—had all got stuck in the marshes in front of G section. Perhaps the heavy artillery fire of ours that had gone on most of yesterday had been meant to pound the beastly things to pieces. Moving, they looked like slow rats, their noses to the ground, snouting crumbs of garbage. When they were still they looked merely pensive!

===Chinese===
蒂金斯说：

「想当年，你还是拿过大学副校长拉丁文奖的人。咱们如今都沦落到这步了。」他又补了一句：「*Vos mellificatis apes!*（你们这些酿蜜的蜂！）」

麦克凯克尼阴郁而轻蔑地说：

「你。……你算哪门子拉丁文家！」

到这会儿，自那门大炮说了那声「*噗·嗬·嗬·嗬·嗬。*」以来，蒂金斯已数到二百八十。这么说，那兴许不是弹幕开始的信号。……若是，现在早该开始了；它会踩着那声「*噗·嗬·嗬·嗬·嗬*」的脚后跟，砰砰地一路碾过来。他的双手和后颈正准备恢复正常。

兴许那天根本不会有炮击。风就是个变数。非但没歇，反倒越刮越紧。昨天他就疑心德国人手头并没有坦克。兴许那些丑陋、没头脑的犰狳——而且还不顶用！发动机不行！——全都陷在 G 地段前面的沼泽里了。兴许我方昨天大半天的重炮轰击，本就是要把这些畜生砸个稀烂。它们动起来，像一只只慢吞吞的老鼠，鼻子贴着地，拱着垃圾碎屑。停下来不动时，看上去倒只是若有所思！

===Original===
Perhaps the strafe would not come. He hoped it would not. He did not want a strafe with himself in command of the battalion. He did not know what to do: what he ought to do by the book. He knew what he would do. He would stroll about along those deep trenches. Stroll. With his hands in his pockets. Like General Gordon in pictures. He would say contemplative things as the time dragged on.… A rather abominable sort of Time really.… But that would introduce into the Battalion a spirit of calm that it had lately lacked… The night before last the C.O., with a bottle in each hand had hurled them both at Huns who did not materialise for an hour and a half. Even the Pals had omitted to laugh. After that he, Tietjens, had taken command. With lots of the Orderly Room papers under both arms. They had had to be in a hurry. At night. With men suggesting pale grey Canadian trappers coming out of holes!

He did not want to command in a strafe: or at any other time! He hoped the unfortunate C.O. would get over his trouble by the evening.… But he supposed that he, Tietjens, would get through it all right if he had to. Like the man who had never tried playing the violin!

===Chinese===
兴许炮击不会来了。他盼着它别来。他不想在自己统带全营的时候赶上炮击。他不知道该怎么做：按操典该怎么做。他知道的是自己会怎么做。他会在那些深壕里溜达。溜达。两手插在口袋里。像画片上的戈登将军。时间磨磨蹭蹭地挨过去，他便说些沉思冥想的话。……那种时间其实相当可憎。……可这么一来，营里就会注入一种近来一直欠缺的镇定……前天夜里，营长两手各攥一只酒瓶，朝着一伙过一个半钟头都不肯现形的德国佬，把两只瓶子全掷了出去。连友伴营的弟兄都忘了笑。打那以后，他蒂金斯接了指挥。两边胳肢窝底下夹着一大摞文书室的文件。事出仓促，只得连夜办。人人都活像一只只从洞里钻出来的灰白脸膛的加拿大捕兽人！

他不想在炮击里指挥：旁的时候也不想！他盼着那位倒霉的营长到傍晚就能缓过来。……不过他估摸着，真到了那一步，他蒂金斯也能撑得过去。就像那个从没试过拉小提琴的人！

===Original===
Mckechnie had suddenly become lachrymosely feminine: like a woman pleading, large-eyed, for her lover, his eyes explored Tietjens’ face for signs of treachery: for signs that what he said was not what he meant in his heart. He said:

“What are you going to do about Bill? Poor old Bill that has sweated for his Battalion as you never.…” He began again:

“Think of poor old Bill! You can’t be *thinking* of doing the dirty on him.… *No* man could be such a swine!”

===Chinese===
麦克凯克尼忽然变得涕泪涟涟、一副女人腔：像一个大眼睛的女人为自己的情郎哀恳，他的眼睛在蒂金斯脸上搜寻背叛的痕迹：搜寻他嘴上说的与心里想的并不一致的痕迹。他说：

「你打算怎么处置比尔？可怜的老比尔，他为自己的营流过的汗，你从来不曾……」他又从头说起：

「想想可怜的老比尔吧！你总不至于*当真*想在背后捅他一刀。……*天下*没有男人能这么猪狗！」

===Original===
It was curious how those circumstances brought out the feminine that was in man. What was that ass of a German Professor’s theory… formula? My plus Wx equals Man?… Well, if God hadn’t invented woman men would have had to do so. In that sort of place. You grew sentimental. He, Tietjens was growing sentimental. He said:

“What does Terence say about him this morning?”

The nice thing to have said would have been:

“Of course, old man, I’ll do all I can to keep it dark!” Terence was the M.O.—the man who had chucked his cap at the Hun orderly.

Mckechnie said:

“That’s the damnable thing! Terence is ratty with him. He won’t take a pill!”

===Chinese===
奇怪的是，这种境遇总能把男人身上蛰伏的女人气逼出来。那个德国傻教授的理论是什么来着……公式？「M 加 Wx 等于 Man」？……唉，若不是上帝早已发明了女人，男人到头来也只好自己动手发明。在这种地方。人就多愁善感起来。他，蒂金斯，正在多愁善感起来。他说：

「特伦斯今儿早上怎么说他的？」

得体的话本该这么说：

「放心，老伙计，我一定尽力把这事捂住！」特伦斯是营里的军医官——就是朝德国佬勤务兵摔帽子的那位。

麦克凯克尼说：

「糟就糟在这儿！特伦斯正跟他置气。他不肯吃药丸！」

===Original===
Tietjens said:

“What’s that? What’s that?”

Mckechnie wavered: his desire for comfort became overpowering.

He said:

“Look here! *Do* the decent thing! You know how poor Bill has worked for us! Get Terence not to report him to Brigade!”

This was wearisome: but it had to be faced.

===Chinese===
蒂金斯说：

「什么？你说什么？」

麦克凯克尼支吾起来：他渴求安慰的欲望压倒了一切。

他说：

「听我说！做件*体面*事吧！你知道可怜的比尔为咱们下了多少苦功！去让特伦斯别把他上报到旅部！」

这事真磨人：可总得应付。

===Original===
A very minute subaltern—Aranjuez—in a perfectly impossible tin hat peered round the side of the bank. Tietjens sent him away for a moment.… These tin hats were probably all right: but they were the curse of the army. They bred distrust! How could you trust a man whose incapable hat tumbled forward on his nose? Or another, with his hat on the back of his head, giving him the air of a ruined gambler! Or a fellow who had put on a soap-dish. To amuse the children: not a serious proceeding.… The German things were better—coming down over the nape of the neck and rising over the brows. When you saw a Hun sideways he looked something: a serious proposition. Full of ferocity. A Hun up against a Tommie looked like a Holbein *lansknecht* fighting a music-hall turn. It made you feel that you were indeed a ragtime army. Rubbed it in!

Mckechnie was reporting that the C.O. had refused to take a pill ordered him by the M.O. Unfortunately the M.O. was ratty that morning—too much hooch overnight! So he said he should report the C.O. to Brigade. Not as being unfit for further service, for he wasn’t. But for refusing to take the pill. It was damnable. Because if Bill wouldn’t take a pill he wouldn’t… The M.O. said that if he took a pill, and stayed in bed that day—without hooch of course!—he would be perfectly fit on the morrow. He had been like that often enough before. The C.O. had always been given the dose before as a drench. He swore he would not take it as a ball. Sheer contrariety!

===Chinese===
一个身量极小的少尉——阿兰胡埃斯——扣着一顶怎么看都不像话的钢盔，从土垛边上探出头来张望。蒂金斯让他先回避片刻。……这些钢盔本身也许没毛病：可它们是军队的祸害。它们滋生不信任！一顶窝囊帽子老往鼻梁上出溜，你怎么信任戴它的人？或者另一个，帽子扣在后脑勺上，一副输得精光的赌棍气派！又或者哪位仁兄，头顶一只肥皂碟。逗孩子玩的：算不得正经行头。……德国货更好——后片垂到后颈，前檐挑过眉骨。侧脸看一个德国佬，他倒真像个人物：一桩正经的难题。一身煞气。一个德国佬对上一个英国大兵，瞧着就像霍尔拜因画里的一名*兰斯克内雇佣兵*（lansknecht）在跟一出杂耍滑稽戏开打。这叫你真切地觉得，自己这一边确实是支拉格泰姆军队。简直往人心上戳！

麦克凯克尼正报告说，营长拒绝了军医官给他开的一粒药丸。不巧军医官那天早上火气正旺——头天夜里黄汤灌多了！所以他扬言要把营长上报旅部。倒不是说他不胜任继续服役——他胜任。就为他不肯吃药丸。这可太混账了。因为比尔要是不肯吃药丸，他就不肯……军医官说，他只要吃一粒药丸，当天卧床——自然，别再沾黄汤！——第二天保管生龙活虎。他从前这样的次数多了去了。以前给营长用这剂药，都是当灌牲口的汤药（drench）强灌下去的。他发誓绝不肯把它当药丸子吞。纯粹是拧着来！

===Original===
Tietjens was accustomed to think of the C.O. as a lad—a good lad, but young. They were, all the same, much of an age, and, for the matter of that, because of his deeply-lined forehead the Colonel looked the older often enough. But when he was fit he was fine. He had a hooked nose, a forcible, grey moustache, like two badger-haired paintbrushes joined beneath the nose, pink skin as polished as the surface of a billiard ball, a noticeably narrow but high forehead, an extremely piercing glance from rather colourless eyes; his hair was black and most polished in slight waves. He was a soldier.

He was, that is to say, the ranker. Of soldiering in the English sense—the real soldiering of peacetime, parades, social events, spit and polish, hard worked summers, leisurely winters, India, the Bahamas, Cairo seasons and the rest he only knew the outside, having looked at it from the barrack windows, the parade ground and, luckily for him, from his Colonel’s house.
