A dawn-land road, with some old thorn trees. They only grew really in Kent. And the sky coming down on all sides. The flat top of a down!

===Chinese===
其实，他是把瓦伦丁·万诺普留给阿兰胡埃斯了！他明白了，自己干的就是这么回事。那小子达克特*就是*瓦伦丁·万诺普。干净，金发，个子小：一张平平常常的脸，一双有胆气的眼睛，一只倔强的、微微鹰钩的小鼻子。……那就仿佛是，瓦伦丁·万诺普已经归他所有，两人正走在一条路上，看见什么人遭了难。而他，蒂金斯，说了：

「我得往前赶。你留下来，看看能帮上什么！」

而，不可思议地，他正走在一条乡间路上，身旁是瓦伦丁·万诺普，一言不发，带着「拥有」之后才有的那种安静的亲密。她属于他了。……不是山路：不是约克郡。不是谷中的路：不是贝默顿。乡村牧师住宅跟他无缘。所以他不去领受圣职！

一条黎明的原野路，几棵老荆棘树。那样的树，其实只在肯特才真长得好。四面天穹低低压下来。一道白垩高地的平顶！

===Original===
Amazing! He had not thought of that girl for over a fortnight now, except in moments of great strafes, when he had hoped she would not be too worried if she knew where he was. Because he had the sense that, all the time she knew where he was.

He had thought of her less and less. At longer intervals.… As with his nightmare of the mining Germans who desired that a candle should be brought to the Captain. At first, every night, three or four times every night, it had visited him.… Now it came only once every night.…

The physical semblance of that boy had brought the girl back to his mind. That was accidental, so it was not part of any psychological rhythm. It did not show him, that is to say, whether, in the natural course of events and without accidents she was ceasing to obsess him.

She was certainly now obsessing him! Beyond bearing or belief. His whole being was overwhelmed by her… by her mentality really. For of course the physical resemblance of the Lance-Corporal was mere subterfuge. Lance-Corporals do not resemble young ladies.… And, as a matter of fact, he did not remember exactly what Valentine Wannop looked like. Not vividly. He had not that sort of mind. It was words that his mind found that let him know that she was fair, snub-nosed, rather broad-faced and square on her feet. As if he had made a note of it and referred to it when he wanted to think of her. His mind didn’t make any mental picture: it brought up a sort of blur of sunlight.

===Chinese===
不可思议！至今半个月还多，他没想起过那姑娘，除非在炮击最凶的分分秒秒里，那时他只盼着，她要是知道他在哪儿，别太挂心。因为他有一种感觉：她无时无刻不知道他在哪儿。

他想她的时候越来越少。间隔越来越长。……就像他那个梦魇——采矿的德国人一心要给上尉拿一支蜡烛去。起初夜夜来，一夜来三回四回。……如今一夜只来一回。……

那小子的形貌把姑娘带回了他的脑海。这是偶然，所以算不得任何心理节律的一部分。这就是说，它并不能告诉他：顺其自然、没有偶然的情况下，她是不是正在淡出对他的纠缠。

此刻她无疑正纠缠着他！超出能忍受、能相信的限度。他的整个存在被她淹没……说真的，是被她的心智淹没。因为下士那点形貌上的相似，当然纯属托词。下士哪会像年轻小姐。……况且，事实上，他也记不清瓦伦丁·万诺普到底长什么样。记不真切。他不是那一路脑子。是他的脑子找到的那些字眼告诉他：她金发，狮子鼻，脸盘偏宽，脚下站得四平八稳。仿佛他记过一笔账，想她的时候就翻出来对一对。他的脑子不生成画面：它端上来的，是一团模糊的阳光。

===Original===
It was the mentality that obsessed him: the exact mind, the impatience of solecisms and facile generalisations!… A queer catalogue of the charms of one’s lady love!… But he wanted to hear her say: “Oh, chuck it, Edith Ethel!” when Edith Ethel Duchemin, now of course Lady Macmaster, quoted some of the opinions expressed in Macmaster’s critical monograph about the late Mr. Rossetti.… How *very* late now!

It would rest him to hear that. She was, in effect, the only person in the world that he wanted to hear speak. Certainly the only person in the world that he wanted to talk to. The only clear intelligence!… The repose that his mind needed from the crackling of thorns under all the pots of the world.… From the eternal, imbecile “*Pampamperipam Pam Pamperi Pam Pam!*” of the German guns that all the while continued.…

Why couldn’t they chuck that? What good did it do them to keep that mad drummer incessantly thundering on his stupid instrument?… Possibly they might bring down some of our planes, but they generally didn’t. You saw the black ball of their shells exploding and slowly expand like pocket-handkerchiefs about the unconcerned planes, like black peas aimed at dragonflies, against the blue: the illuminated, pinkish, pretty things!… But his dislike of those guns was just dislike—a Tory prejudice. They were probably worth while. Just.…

===Chinese===
纠缠他的，是那副心智：那副精确的心智，对语病和信口一锅烩的概括零容忍！……这样一份意中人的可爱清单，可真别致！……可他就是想听她说：「噢，收起来吧，伊迪丝·埃塞尔！」——当伊迪丝·埃塞尔·迪谢曼，如今当然已是麦克马斯特夫人，引用麦克马斯特论已故罗塞蒂先生的那本批评专著里的高见时。……如今可真是「已故」到家了！

听见那一句，他就能歇过来。实际上，这世上他想听其说话的，只有她一个人。这世上他想与之说话的，无疑也只有她一个人。唯一清明的一副智力！……他的心智需要的那份安歇，好躲开普天下锅底下荆枝的爆响。……躲开那永远敲个不停、蠢得没治的「*砰砰砰佩里砰 砰 佩里砰 砰！*」——德国人的炮。……

他们就不能歇手吗？由着那个疯鼓手在他那件蠢乐器上没完没了地轰，对他们有什么好处？……没准儿能敲下来咱们几架飞机，可多半敲不下来。你看见他们炮弹炸开的黑球，慢吞吞地涨开，像一方方手帕，围住那些满不在乎的飞机——像朝着蜻蜓扔黑豌豆，衬在蓝天上：被照亮的、粉红的、漂亮玩意儿！……可他讨厌那些炮，就只是讨厌——一种托利党人的偏见。它们多半还是值的。就这么回事。……

===Original===
You naturally tried every argument in the unseen contest of wills that went on across the firmament.

“Ho!” says our Staff, “they are going to attack in force at such an hour ackemma,” because naturally the staff thought in terms of ackemma years after the twenty-four-hour day had been established. “Well, we’ll send out a million machine-gun planes to wipe out any men they’ve got moving up into support!”

It was of course unusual to move bodies of men by daylight. But this game had only two resources: you used the usual. Or the unusual. *Usually* you didn’t begin your barrage after dawn and launch your attack at ten-thirty or so. So you might do it—the Huns might be trying it on—as a surprise measure.

On the other hand, our people might be sending over the planes, whose immense droning was then making your very bones vibrate, in order to tell the Huns that we were ready to be surprised: that the time had now about come round when we might be expecting the Hun brain to think out a surprise. So we sent out those deathly, dreadful things to run along just over the tops of the hedgerows, in spite of all the guns! For there was nothing more terrifying in the whole war than that span of lightness, swaying, approaching a few feet above the heads of your column of men: instinct with wrath: dispensing the dreadful rain! So we had sent them. In a moment they would be tearing down.…

===Chinese===
在这场隔着苍穹进行的、看不见的意志较量里，你自然什么招数都要试。

「嗬！」咱们的参谋部说，「他们要在某某钟点『上午』（ackemma——他们把 a.m. 就念成这么个老腔）大举进攻」——因为自然，二十四小时制确立都好些年了，参谋部想问题还满脑子「上午」。「好，那咱们放一百万架机枪飞机出去，他们往前挪、去当预备队的兵，有一个算一个，全抹掉！」

大白天成建制地挪兵，这当然不寻常。可这盘棋只有两样招数：你用寻常的。或者不寻常的。*寻常*你不会拖到拂晓之后才开弹幕、十点半上下才放兵冲锋。所以你兴许就偏偏这么干——德国佬没准儿正要来这一手——当个奇招。

另一方面，咱们的人把那些飞机放过去——它们那震天的嗡嗡声，这会儿正震得你骨头缝里发麻——没准儿就是为了告诉德国佬：我们准备好受惊了；咱们估摸着德国佬的脑子要琢磨出一次奇袭，这日子差不多绕到那一圈了。所以我们放出了那些要命的、可怕的家伙，贴着树篱顶子跑，顶着他们全部的炮！因为整场战争里，再没有比那一叶轻飘飘的东西更吓人的了：摇摇晃晃，逼近到你头顶上方几英尺：满载着怒火：播撒着可怕的弹雨！所以我们放了。转眼它们就要俯冲下来了。……

===Original===
Of course if this were merely a demonstration: if, say, there were no reinforcements moving, no troops detraining at the distant railhead, the correct Hun answer would be to hammer some of our trenches to hell with all the heavy stuff they could put on to them. That was like saying sardonically:

“God, if you interfere with our peace and quiet on a fine day we’ll interfere with yours!” And… Kerumph… the wagons of coal would fly over until we recalled our planes and all went to sleep again over the chessboard.… You would probably be just as well off if you refrained from either demonstration or counter-demonstration. But Great General Staff liked to exchange these witticisms in iron. And a little blood!

A Sergeant of sorts approached him from Bn.H.2 way, shepherding a man with a head wound. His tin hat, that is to say, was perched jauntily forward over a bandage. He was Jewish-nosed, appeared not to have shaved, though he had, and appeared as if he ought to have worn pince-nez to complete his style of Oriental manhood. Private Smith. Tietjens said:

===Chinese===
当然，倘若这只不过是一次示威：倘若，比方说，没有增援在移动，没有部队在远处的铁路端头下车，那么德国佬正确的应对，就是把所有能压上去的重家伙全压上去，把咱们的几段壕砸进地狱。那就像冷笑着说：

「老天，你大晴天搅我们的清净，我们就搅你的！」然后……喀噜——姆夫……一车车煤块就飞过来，直到咱们收回飞机，各自伏在棋盘上接着睡。……你要是示威也好、反示威也好，一概忍住不干，多半落得同样太平。可大总参谋部就爱用钢铁互敬这类俏皮话。还搭上一点儿血！

一个说不上来路的军士从营部二号（Bn.H.2）那头迎上来，押着一个头部挂彩的兵。就是说，他的钢盔俏皮地朝前扣在绷带外头。他长着犹太式的鼻子，看着像没刮脸——其实刮了——又像该夹一副夹鼻眼镜，才配齐他那一路东方男子汉的做派。列兵史密斯。蒂金斯说：

===Original===
“Look here, what was your confounded occupation before the war?”

The man replied with an agreeable, cultured throaty intonation:

“I was a journalist, sir. On a Socialist paper. Extreme Left!”

“And what,” Tietjens asked, “was your agreeable name?… I’m obliged to ask you that question. I don’t want to insult you.”

===Chinese===
「我问你，你战前干的到底是哪门子行当？」

那人回话，嗓音圆润带痰，一副有教养的腔调：

「我是个新闻记者，长官。社会主义报纸。极左！」

「那么，」蒂金斯问，「你那位讨人喜欢的名字是？……这个问题我不得不问。我不想侮辱你。」

===Original===
In the old regular army it was an insult to ask a private if he was not going under his real name. Most men enlisted under false names.

The man said:

“Eisenstein, sir!”

Tietjens asked if the man were a Derby recruit or compulsorily enlisted. He said he had enlisted voluntarily. Tietjens said: “Why?” If the fellow was a capable journalist and on the right side he would be more useful outside the army. The man said he had been foreign correspondent of a Left paper. Being correspondent of a Left paper with a name like Eisenstein deprived one of one’s chance of usefulness. Besides he wanted to have a whack at the Prussians. He was of Polish extraction. Tietjens asked the Sergeant if the man had a good record. The Sergeant said: “First-class man. First-class soldier.” He had been recommended for the D.C.M., Tietjens said:

===Chinese===
在老正规军里，问一个列兵是不是没拿真名当兵，本身就是侮辱。当兵的多半都用假名字入伍。

那人说：

「艾森斯坦，长官！」

蒂金斯问这人是德比方案征来的，还是征兵制征的。他说是自愿入伍的。蒂金斯说：「为什么？」这人要真是个有本事的记者，又站在正道一边，那在军队外头用处大得多。那人说，他原是一家左派报纸的驻外记者。顶着艾森斯坦这么个名字当左派报纸的记者，就别指望有什么用武之地了。再说，他想揍普鲁士人一顿。他是波兰血统。蒂金斯问军士，这人记录怎么样。军士说：「一流的兵。一流的士兵。」他得过杰出操行勋章（D.C.M.）推荐。蒂金斯说：

===Original===
“I shall apply to have you transferred to the Jewish regiment. In the meantime you can go back to the First Line Transport. You shouldn’t have been a Left journalist and have a name like Eisenstein. One or the other. Not both.” The man said the name had been inflicted on his ancestry in the Middle Ages. He would prefer to be called Esau, as a son of that tribe. He pleaded not to be sent to the Jewish regiment, which was believed to be in Mesopotamia, just when the fighting there was at its most interesting.

“You’re probably thinking of writing a book,” Tietjens said. “Well, there are all Abanar and Pharpar to write about. I’m sorry. But you’re intelligent enough to see that I can’t take…” He stopped, fearing that if the Sergeant heard any more the men might make it hot for the fellow as a suspect. He was annoyed at having asked his name before the Sergeant. He appeared to be a good man. Jews could fight.… And hunt!… But he wasn’t going to take any risks. The man, dark-eyed and erect, flinched a little, gazing into Tietjens’ eyes.

“I suppose you can’t, sir,” he said. “It’s a disappointment. I’m not writing anything. I want to go on in the Army. I like the life.”

===Chinese===
「我会打报告，把你转去犹太团。眼下你可以先回一线辎重队去。你不该既当左派记者，又顶着艾森斯坦这么个名字。二选一。别兼着。」那人说，这名字是中世纪强加给他祖宗的。作为那一支族的子孙，他倒宁愿被叫作以扫。他恳求别把他送进犹太团——据说那团在美索不达米亚，那边的战事正当最热闹的当口。

「你八成在打算写本书，」蒂金斯说。「唉，亚巴拿与法珥法（Abanar and Pharpar，《列王纪》里大马士革的两条河）的景致，尽够你写的。对不住。可你够聪明，明白我不能接……」他停住了，怕军士再听下去，弟兄们会把这家伙当嫌疑犯，叫他日子难熬。他恼恨自己当着军士的面问了名字。这人看着是个好人。犹太人能打仗。……还能打猎！……可他不想冒任何险。那人黑眼睛，腰板笔直，微微一缩，直直望进蒂金斯的眼睛。

「我想您是不能，长官，」他说。「很失望。我没在写任何东西。我想留在军队里干下去。我喜欢这日子。」

===Original===
Tietjens said:

“I’m sorry, Smith. I can’t help it. Fall out!” He was sorry. He believed the fellow. But responsibility hardens the heart. It must. A very short time ago he would have taken trouble over that fellow. A great deal of trouble, very likely. Now he wasn’t going to.…

A large capital A in whitewash decorated the piece of corrugated iron that was derelictly propped against a channel at right angles to the trench. To Tietjens’ astonishment a strong impulse like a wave of passion influenced his being towards the left—up that channel. It wasn’t funk: it wasn’t any sort of funk. He had been rather irritatedly wrapped up in the case of Private Smith-Eisenstein. It had undeniably irritated him to have to break the chances of a Jew and Red Socialist. It was the sort of thing one did not do if one were omnipotent—as he was. Then… this strong impulse?… It was a passionate desire to go where you could find exact intellect: rest.

He thought he suddenly understood. For the Lincolnshire Sergeant-Major the word Peace meant that a man could stand up on a hill. For him it meant someone to talk to.

===Chinese===
蒂金斯说：

「对不住，史密斯。我没办法。你退下吧！」他是真对不住。他信得过这个人。可责任心会把心磨硬。它非这样不可。要是不久以前，他会为这个人费心张罗。多半还会大费周章。如今他不打算了。……

一块波纹铁皮被遗弃似的斜支在与堑壕成直角的一条沟口上，上头拿白灰刷着一个大写的 A。叫蒂金斯吃惊的是，一股强烈的冲动，像一阵激情的浪，把他的整个存在推向左边——推上那条沟去。这不是怯阵：不是任何一种怯阵。他方才正为列兵史密斯-艾森斯坦的案子憋着火。不得不毁掉一个犹太人、一个赤色社会主义者的前程，这实实在在叫他冒火。一个人若无所不能——他如今便是——就不干这种事。那么……这股强烈的冲动是？……那是一种火热的渴望，渴望走到能找到精确智力的地方去：歇一歇。

他觉得自己一下子明白了。对那位林肯郡的军士长，「和平」这个词的意思是：人能站上山头，挺直身杆。对他，它的意思是：有个可说话的人。

### V / 第五章

===Original===
The Colonel said:

“Look here, Tietjens, lend me two hundred and fifty quid. They say you’re a damn beastly rich fellow. My accounts are all out. I’ve got a loathsome complaint. My friends have all gone back on me. I shall have to face a Court of Enquiry if I go home. But my nerve’s gone. I’ve got to go home.”

He added:

“I daresay you knew all that.”

From the sudden fierce hatred that he felt at the thought of giving money to this man, Tietjens knew that his inner mind based all its calculations on the idea of living with Valentine Wannop… when men could stand up on hills.

===Chinese===
上校说：

「听我说，蒂金斯，借我二百五十镑。他们说你是他妈的顶顶有钱的家伙。我的账目全乱了套。我得了见不得人的恶疾。我的朋友们全都不认我了。我要是回国，就得面对一个调查庭。可我的神经已经垮了。我不得不回国去。」

他补了一句：

「这些你八成都已经知道了。」

一想到要给这个人钱，他便涌起一阵强烈凶猛的憎恨——从这憎恨里，蒂金斯明白了：他内心深处的一切盘算，都建立在同瓦伦丁·万诺普一起过日子的念头上……等到人总算能在山岗上站起来的那一天。

===Original===
He had found the Colonel in his cellar—it really, actually was a cellar, the remains of a farm—sitting on the edge of his camp-bed, in his shorts, his khaki shirt very open at the neck. His eyes were a little bloodshot, but his cropped, silver-grey hair was accurately waved, his grey moustache beautifully pointed. His silver-backed hairbrushes and a small mirror were indeed on the table in front of him. By the rays of the lamp that, hung overhead, rendered that damp stone place faintly nauseating, he looked keen, clean and resolute. Tietjens wondered how he would look by daylight. He had remarkably seldom seen the fellow by daylight. Beside the mirror and the brushes lay, limply, an unfilled pipe, a red pencil and the white buff papers from Whitehall that Tietjens had already read.

===Chinese===
他是在上校的地窖里找到他的——那确确实实是个地窖，一座农庄的残骸——他坐在行军床沿上，穿着短裤，卡其衬衫的领口敞得很开。他眼里略带血丝，但剪短的银灰色头发烫得纹丝不乱，灰色的小胡子两撇修得漂漂亮亮。他那银背发刷和一面小镜子果然就摆在他面前的桌上。头顶吊着一盏灯，灯光把那潮湿的石屋照得隐隐令人作呕；就在这灯光里，他看上去精悍、干净、果决。蒂金斯心想，此人白天里该是什么模样。他难得见到这家伙白天里的样子。镜子和发刷旁边，蔫蔫地躺着一只没装烟丝的烟斗、一支红铅笔，还有蒂金斯已经读过的那几页来自白厅的米黄色公文。

===Original===
He had begun by looking at Tietjens with a keen, hard, bloodshot glance. He had said:

“You think you can command this battalion? Have you had any experience? It appears you suggest that I take two months’ leave.”

Tietjens had expected a violent outbreak. Threats even. None had come. The Colonel had continued to regard him with intentness, nothing more. He sat motionless, his long arms, bare to the elbow, dependent over each of his knees which were far apart. He said that if he decided to go he didn’t want to leave his battalion to a man that would knock it about. He continued staring hard at Tietjens. The phrase was singular in that place and at that hour, but Tietjens understood it to mean that he did not want his battalion discipline to go to pieces.

Tietjens answered that he did not think he would let the discipline go to pieces. The Colonel had said:

“How do you know? You’re no soldier, are you?”

===Chinese===
他一上来就用精悍、生硬、带着血丝的目光打量蒂金斯。他说过：

「你以为你能指挥这个营？你有过经验吗？听说你建议我休两个月假。」

蒂金斯原以为会有一场狂风暴雨。甚至是威胁。都没有来。上校只是继续目不转睛地端详他，仅此而已。他一动不动地坐着，两条长胳膊裸到肘部，分垂在撇得很开的两膝上。他说，倘若他决定走，他不想把这个营交给一个会糟蹋它的人。他继续狠狠盯着蒂金斯。这个字眼在那个地方、那个时辰听来颇为古怪，但蒂金斯明白，他的意思是不能让全营的军纪垮掉。

蒂金斯回答，他不觉得自己会让军纪垮掉。上校说过：

「你怎么知道？你不是正经军人，对吧？」

===Original===
Tietjens said he had commanded in the line a Company at full strength—nearly as large as the battalion and, out of it, a unit of exactly eight times its present strength. He did not think any complaints had been made of him. The Colonel said, frostily:

“Well! I know nothing about you.” He had added:

“You seem to have moved the battalion all right the night before last. I wasn’t in a condition to do it myself. I’m not well. I’m obliged to you. The men appear to like you. They’re tired of me.”

===Chinese===
蒂金斯说，他在前线指挥过一个满员的连——差不多有这个营那么大——还指挥过一个恰好八倍于现有人数的单位。他不觉得有谁告过他的状。上校冷冰冰地说：

「哼！你的底细我一无所知。」他又补了一句：

「前天夜里你倒像把这个营带得妥妥帖帖。我自己那会儿没法带。我身子不行。多谢你了。弟兄们好像喜欢你。他们受够我了。」

===Original===
Tietjens felt himself on tenterhooks. He had, now, a passionate desire to command that battalion. It was the last thing he would have expected of himself. He said:

“If it becomes a question of a war of motion, sir, I don’t know that I should have much experience.”

The Colonel answered:

“It won’t become a war of motion before I come back. If I ever do come back.”

===Chinese===
蒂金斯觉得自己如坐针毡。此刻，他怀着一种热切的渴望，想指挥这个营。这是他最想不到会在自己身上发现的念头。他说：

「长官，倘若打起运动战来，我不知道自己算不算有多少经验。」

上校答道：

「在我回来之前打不成运动战。如果我还回得来的话。」

===Original===
Tietjens said:

“Isn’t it rather like a war of motion now, sir?” It was perhaps the first time in his life he had ever asked for information from a superior in rank—with an implicit belief that he would get an exact answer. The Colonel said:

“No. This is only falling back on prepared positions. There will be positions prepared for us right back to the sea. If the Staff has done its work properly. If it hasn’t, the war’s over. We’re done, finished, smashed, annihilated, nonexistent.”

===Chinese===
蒂金斯说：

「长官，现在这架势不是已经很像运动战了吗？」这或许是他生平第一次向上级讨教——并且隐隐相信会得到一个确切的回答。上校说：

「不。这不过是退守既设阵地。一路退到海边，都会有替我们备好的阵地。只要参谋部把差事办像了样。若是没办像样，战争就完了。我们就完了、垮了、砸了、灰飞烟灭了、不存在了。」

===Original===
Tietjens said:

“But if the great strafe that, according to Division, is due now…”

The Colonel said: “What?” Tietjens repeated his words and added:

“We might get pushed beyond the next prepared position.”

===Chinese===
蒂金斯说：

「可要是师部说的那场眼下就该到的大攻势——」

上校说：「什么？」蒂金斯重复了一遍，又补上一句：

「我们可能会被推出下一道既设阵地之外。」

===Original===
The Colonel appeared to withdraw his thoughts from a great distance.

“There isn’t going to be any great strafe,” he said. He was beginning to add: “Division has got.…” A considerable thump shook the hill behind their backs. The Colonel sat listening without much attention. His eyes gloomily rested on the papers before him. He said, without looking up:

“Yes: I don’t want my battalion knocked about!” He went on reading again—the communication from Whitehall. He said: “You’ve read this?” and then:

“Falling back on prepared positions isn’t the same as moving in the open. You don’t have to do more than you do in a trench-to-trench attack. I suppose you can get your direction by compass all right. Or get someone to, for you.”

===Chinese===
上校仿佛把思绪从极远处收了回来。

「不会有什么大攻势。」他说。他正要往下说：「师部已经拿到……」一记沉重的闷响震得他们背后的山丘发颤。上校坐着听了听，没怎么在意。他的眼睛阴沉地落在面前的公文上。他没抬头，说：

「对了：我不要我的营挨揍！」他又低头读下去——白厅的那份公文。他说：「这个你读过了？」接着说：

「退守既设阵地跟在开阔地里机动不是一回事。你要做的不比壕对壕的进攻多。我料想你用罗盘辨向是没问题的。要不就找个人替你辨。」

===Original===
Another considerable *Crump* of sound shook the earth but from a little further away. The Colonel turned the sheet of paper over. Pinned to the back of it was the private note of the Brigadier. He perused this also with gloomy and unsurprised eyes.

“Pretty stiff, all this,” he said, “you’ve read it? I shall have to go back and see about this.”

He exclaimed:

“It’s rough luck. I should have liked to leave my battalion to someone that knew it. I don’t suppose you do. Perhaps you do, though.”

===Chinese===
又一声沉闷的*砰隆*震得大地发抖，只是离得稍远了些。上校把那页纸翻了过来。纸背面别着旅长的私人便条。他也用阴沉而不意外的眼睛读了一遍。

「这些个都相当棘手，」他说，「你读过了？我只好回国去料理这个了。」

他感叹道：

「运气真糟。我本想把这个营交给一个了解它的人。我看你不了解。不过，兴许也了解。」

===Original===
An immense collection of fire-irons: all the fire-irons in the world fell just above their heads. The sound seemed to prolong itself in echoes, though of course it could not have. It was repeated.

The Colonel looked upwards negligently. Tietjens proposed to go to see. The Colonel said:

“No, don’t. Notting will tell us if anything’s wanted.… Though nothing can be wanted!” Notting was the beady-eyed Adjutant in the adjoining cellar. “How could they expect us to keep accounts straight in August 1914? How can they expect me to remember what happened? At the Depot. Then!” He appeared listless, but without resentment. “Rotten luck…” he said. “In the battalion and… with this!” He rapped the back of his hand on the papers. He looked up at Tietjens.

===Chinese===
一大捆炉火铁器：全世界的炉火铁器都砸在他们头顶正上方。那声音仿佛在回声里绵延不去——当然其实不可能。又是一下。

上校漫不经心地朝上看了看。蒂金斯提议出去看看。上校说：

「不，别去。真有什么事，诺丁会来告诉我们。……反正也不会有什么事！」诺丁就是隔壁地窖里那位珠子眼副官。「他们怎么能指望我们把 1914 年 8 月的账目理清？他们怎么能指望我记得住当时的事？在补充营。那时候！」他显得无精打采，却并无怨怼。「倒霉透了……」他说，「营里这一摊……外加……这个！」他用手背敲了敲那几页公文。他抬眼看蒂金斯。

===Original===
“I suppose I could get rid of you; with a bad report,” he said. “Or perhaps I couldn’t… General Campion put you here. You’re said to be his bastard.”

“He’s my godfather,” Tietjens said. “If you put in a bad report of me I should not protest. That is, if it were on the grounds of lack of experience. I should go to the Brigadier over anything else.”

“It’s the same thing,” the Colonel said. “I mean a godson. If I had thought you were General Campion’s bastard, I should not have said it.… No; I don’t want to put in a bad report of you. It’s my own fault if you don’t know the battalion. I’ve kept you out of it. I didn’t want you to see what a rotten state the papers are in. They say you’re the devil of a paper soldier. You used to be in a Government office, didn’t you?”

Heavy blows were being delivered to the earth with some regularity on each side of the cellar. It was as if a boxer of the size of a mountain were delivering rights and lefts in heavy alternation. And it made hearing rather difficult.

===Chinese===
「我想我能把你撵走；写份坏报告就是了，」他说，「要不，兴许撵不走……是坎皮恩将军把你安插到这儿来的。据说你是他的私生子。」

「他是我教父，」蒂金斯说，「你若写我的坏报告，我不会抗议。也就是说，倘若理由是缺乏经验。换了别的理由，我就会去找旅长。」

「一回事，」上校说，「我是说教子。我早想到你是坎皮恩将军的私生子，就不会那么说了。……不；我不想写你的坏报告。你不了解这个营，是我自己的过错。是我一直把你关在外面。我不想让你看见文书烂成了什么样子。他们说你是玩文书的行家里手。你从前在政府部门干过，对吧？」

地窖两侧正以某种规律向大地发起重重打击。仿佛一座山那么大的拳师在沉重地左右开弓。这使得听清话音颇为费劲。

===Original===
“Rotten luck,” the Colonel said. “And Mckechnie’s dotty. Clean dotty.” Tietjens missed some words. He said that he would probably be able to get the paper work of the battalion straight before the Colonel came back.

The noise rolled down hill like a heavy cloud. The Colonel continued talking and Tietjens, not being very accustomed to his voice, lost a good deal of what he said but, as if in a rift, he did hear:

“I’m not going to burn my fingers with a bad report on you that may bring a General on my back—to get back Mckechnie who’s dotty.… Not fit to.…”

===Chinese===
「倒霉透了，」上校说，「还有麦克凯克尼疯了。彻底疯了。」蒂金斯漏掉了几个词。他说，多半能赶在上校回来之前把全营的文书理顺。

轰隆声像一团沉重的乌云滚下山去。上校继续说着，蒂金斯不很习惯他的嗓音，漏掉了大半，但仿佛在云缝里，他确实听见了：

「我才不为你去引火烧身，写份坏报告，招个将军压到我头上来——就为了把疯掉的麦克凯克尼弄回来。……不配。……」

===Original===
The noise rolled in again. Once the Colonel listened to it, turning his head on one side and looking upwards. But he appeared satisfied with what he heard and recommenced his perusal of the Horse Guards letter. He took the pencil, underlined words and then sat idly stabbing the paper with the point.

With every minute Tietjen’s respect for him increased. This man at least knew his job—as an engine-dresser does, or the captain of a steam tramp. His nerves might have gone to pieces. They probably had; probably he could not go very far without stimulants: he was probably under bromides now.

And, all things considered, his treatment of Tietjens had been admirable and Tietjens had to revise his view of it. He realised that it was Mckechnie who had given him the idea that the Colonel hated him: but he would not have said anything. He was too old a hand in the Army to give Tietjens a handle by saying anything definite.… And he had always treated Tietjens with the sort of monumental deference that, in a Mess, the Colonel should bestow on his chief assistant. Going through a door at mealtimes, for instance, if they happened to be side by side, he would motion with his hand for Tietjens to go first, naturally though, taking his proper precedence when Tietjens halted. And here he was, perfectly calm. And quite ready to be instructive.

===Chinese===
轰隆声又滚了回来。有一回上校侧过头、抬眼听了听。但他似乎对听到的动静还算满意，重新读起近卫骑兵部（Horse Guards）的公函来。他拿起铅笔，在字句下画线，然后闲闲地坐着，用笔尖一下一下戳那页纸。

蒂金斯对他的敬意一分钟深过一分钟。这个人至少精通本行——像机车整备工，或者蒸汽货船的船长那样精通。他的神经也许已经垮了。多半是垮了；多半他离了兴奋剂就走不了多远：这会儿多半正服着溴剂。

而通盘算来，他对蒂金斯的处置一直是无可挑剔的，蒂金斯不得不修正自己对此的看法。他意识到，让他以为上校恨他的念头是麦克凯克尼种下的：但那人也不会说破。他在军中是太老的油子了，绝不会说出什么确切的话来给蒂金斯留把柄。……况且他也始终以那种纪念碑式的恭敬对待蒂金斯——在军官食堂里，营长对首席助手正该如此。比方说吃饭时过门，两人恰好并了肩，他会抬手示意蒂金斯先行，不过做得自然而然，待蒂金斯站定，自己再按该有的位次走。而现在，他就在这里，镇定自若。而且相当乐意指点一二。

===Original===
Tietjens was not calm: he was too much bothered by Valentine Wannop and by the thought that, if the strafe was on, he ought to be seeing about his battalion. And of course by the bombardment. But the Colonel said, when Tietjens with the aid of signs again made proposals to take a look around:

“No. Stop where you are. This isn’t the strafe. There is not going to be a strafe. This is only a little extra Morning Hate. You can tell by the noise. That’s only four point twos. There’s nothing really heavy. The really heavies don’t come so fast. They’ll be turning on to the Worcesters now and only giving us one every half minute.… That’s their game. If you don’t know that, what are you doing here?” He added! “You hear?” pointing his forefinger to the roof. The noise shifted. It went away to the right as a slow coal-wagon might. He went on:

“This is your place. Not doing things up above. They’ll come and tell you if they want things. And you’ve got a first-rate Adjutant in Notting and Dunne’s a good man.… The men are all under cover: that’s an advantage in having your strength down to three hundred. There’s dugouts for all and to spare.… All the same, this is no place for you. Nor for me. This is a young man’s war. We’re old uns. Three and a half years of it have done for me. Three and a half months will do for you.”

===Chinese===
蒂金斯并不镇定：他被瓦伦丁·万诺普搅得心神不宁，还惦记着倘若炮击真的开始，他该去照管全营。当然，还有这场轰炸本身。但蒂金斯又打着手势提议出去看看时，上校说：

「不。待在原地。这不是那场大炮击。不会有什么大炮击。这不过是加了点料的晨间仇恨（Morning Hate，德军每晨例行的开锣炮击）。听声音就听得出来。那不过是四点二的玩意儿。没有真正的大家伙。真正的大家伙来不了这么快。这会儿他们该转到伍斯特郡团那边去了，只给我们每半分钟一发。……他们玩的就是这一手。连这个都不知道，你在这儿干什么？」他补了一句！「听见没有？」食指点着屋顶。轰响挪了窝。像一列慢吞吞的运煤车似的朝右去远了。他接着说：

「这里才是你的位置。不是到上头去张罗。他们有事要办，自会来告诉你。你有诺丁这么一流的副官，邓恩也是把好手。……弟兄们全在掩蔽之下：兵力降到三百人，倒有这么个好处。掩蔽部人人有，还有富余。……话虽如此，这地方不属于你。也不属于我。这是年轻人的战争。我们是老骨头了。三年半的仗熬垮了我。你呢，三个月半就熬垮你。」

===Original===
He looked gloomily at his reflection in the mirror that stood before him.

“You’re a gone coon!” he said to it. Then he took it and, holding it for a moment poised at the end of a bare white arm, flung it violently at the rough stones of the wall behind Tietjens. The fragments tinkled to the ground.

“There’s seven years’ bad luck,” he said. “God take ’em, if they can give me seven years worse than this last I’d find it instructive!”

He looked at Tietjens with infuriated eyes.

===Chinese===
他阴郁地望着立在面前的那面镜子里自己的影像。

「你这家伙算是没救了！」他对它说。然后他拿起镜子，在一条裸露的白胳膊尽头悬了一瞬，猛地朝蒂金斯身后粗粝的石墙掷去。碎片叮叮当当落了地。

「这可是七年霉运，」他说，「随它们去吧，它们要是能给我比刚熬过去的这些年份更糟的七年，我倒要受教了！」

他用狂怒的眼睛瞪着蒂金斯。

===Original===
“Look here you!” he said, “You’re an educated man… What’s the worst thing about this war? What’s the *worst* thing? Tell me that!” His chest began to heave. “It’s that they won’t let us alone. Never! Not one of us! If they’d let us alone we could fight. But never.… No one! It’s not only the beastly papers of the battalion, though I’m no good with papers. Never was and never shall be.… But it’s the people at home. One’s own people. God help us, you’d think that when a poor devil was in the trenches they’d let him alone.… Damn it: I’ve had solicitors’ letters about family quarrels when I was in hospital. Imagine that!… Imagine it! I don’t mean tradesmen’s dunnings. But one’s own people. I haven’t even got a bad wife as Mckechnie has and they say you have. My wife’s a bit extravagant and the children are expensive. That’s worry enough.… But my father died eighteen months ago. He was in partnership with my uncle. A builder. And they tried to do his estate out of his share of the business and leave my old mother with nothing. And my brother and sister threw the estate into Chancery in order to get back the little bit my father spent on my wife and children. My wife and children lived with my father whilst I was in India.… And out here.… My solicitor says they can get it out of my share: the cost of their keep. He calls it the doctrine of ademption.… Ademption… Doctrine of… I was better off as a Sergeant,” he added gloomily. “But Sergeants don’t get let alone. They’ve always got women after them. Or their wives take up with Belgians and they get written to about it. Sergeant Cutts of D Company gets an anonymous letter every week about his wife. How’s he to do his duty! But he does. So have I till now.…” He added with renewed violence:

“Look here. You’re an educated man, aren’t you? The sort of man that could write a book. You write a book about that. You write to the papers about it. You’d be more use to the Army doing that than being here. I daresay you’re a good enough officer. Old Campion is too keen a commander to stick a rotten officer into this job, godson or no godson.… Besides, I don’t believe the whole story about you. If a General wanted to give a soft godson’s job to a fellow, it would be a soft job and a fat one. He wouldn’t send him here. So take the battalion with my blessing. You won’t worry over it more than I have: the poor bloody Glamorgan’s.”

===Chinese===
「你听着！」他说，「你是个受过教育的人……这场战争最坏的是什么？最*坏*的是什么？你说给我听！」他的胸膛开始起伏。「就是他们不放过我们。从来不！一个都不放过！他们要是放过我们，我们还能打仗。可从来没有。……谁都不放过！还不光是营里那些该死的文书，虽说我搞不来文书。从来搞不来，往后也搞不来。……是家里的人。自己家里人。老天爷在上，你总以为一个可怜虫蹲在战壕里的时候，他们总该放过他吧。……见鬼：我住院的时候还收到过律师的信，谈家里的纠纷。你想想！……想想看！我不是说商家催账那种信。是自家人。我甚至连麦克凯克尼那样的浑老婆都没有——虽说人家说你倒有一个。我老婆有点儿挥霍，孩子们开销大。这已经够烦的了。……可我父亲十八个月前死了。他和我叔合伙。开营造行。他们想把他名下的生意份额从遗产里抠掉，让我老母亲一个子儿不剩。我弟弟妹妹又把遗产官司拖进大法官法院（Chancery），为的是追回我父亲贴补我老婆孩子的那一点点。我驻印度的时候，我老婆孩子住在我父亲家。……如今我在这边。……我的律师说，他们能从我那份里抠：扣他们娘儿几个的赡养费。他管这叫「赠与失效原则」（doctrine of ademption）……失效……原则……我当军士那会儿日子倒好过些，」他阴郁地补了一句，「可军士们也没人放过。总归有小娘们儿追着他们。再不然就是老婆跟比利时人好上了，还有人为这个写信来告状。D连的卡茨中士每礼拜收到一封匿名信，说的都是他老婆。你让他怎么尽职责！可他照尽。我呢，到今天为止也一样。……”他带着重新涌起的火气补充道：

「你听着。你是个受过教育的人，不是吗？是能写书的那路人。你就写一本书，写这个。你给报纸写信去说这个。你干这个比蹲在这儿对军队更有用。我估摸你这军官当得还算够格。老坎皮恩眼力太毒，绝不会把一个烂军官塞进这个位子，教子也罢、不是教子也罢。……再说，你的那些传闻我一个字也不信。一个将军要是想给哪个家伙安个照顾教子的闲差，那差事得又轻闲又肥。他不会把人派到这儿来。所以，带上我的祝福把这个营收下吧。你为它操的心不会比我多：这个可怜的、该死的格拉摩根郡团。」

===Original===
So he had his battalion! He drew an immense breath. The bumps began to come back along the line. He figured those shells as being like sparrow-hawks beating along a hedge. They were probably pretty accurate. The Germans were pretty accurate. The trenches were probably being knocked about a good deal, the pretty, pinkish gravel falling about in heaps as it would lie in a park, ready to be spread on paths. He remembered how he had been up on the Montagne Noire, still, thank God, behind where they were now. Why did he thank God? Did he really care where the Army was. Probably! But enough to say “thank God” about? Probably too.… But as long as they kept on at the job did anything matter? Anything else? It was keeping on that mattered. From the Montagne Noir he had seen our shells bursting on a thinnish line in the distance, in shining weather. Each shell existing in a white puff, beautifully. Forward and backward along the line.… Under Messines village. He had felt exhilaration to think that our gunners were making such good practice. Now some Hun on a hill was feeling exhilaration over puffs of smoke in our line!… But he, Tietjens was… Damn it, he was going to make two hundred and fifty quid towards living with Valentine Wannop—when you really *could* stand up on a hill… anywhere!

===Chinese===
这么说他有营了！他深深吸了一大口气。闷响开始重新沿着防线滚来。他把那些炮弹想象成贴着树篱掠行的雀鹰。它们多半打得相当准。德国人打得相当准。战壕多半正被砸得够呛，那漂亮的淡红色砾石成堆成堆地坍落，就像码在公园里等着铺上小径的模样。他记起自己曾登上蒙塔涅努瓦山（Montagne Noire，法语「黑山」）——谢天谢地，那地方还在他们如今防线的后面。他为什么谢天谢地？他真在乎大军在哪儿吗？多半在乎！可在乎到要说「谢天谢地」的地步吗？多半也。……可只要他们还在把差事撑下去，别的还重要吗？还有什么别的事？要紧的就是撑下去。从蒙塔涅努瓦山上，他望见过我们的炮弹在远处一条细细的战线上炸开，那天日光耀眼。每一发都在一蓬白烟里存在，漂漂亮亮。沿战线往前、又往后。……就在梅西讷（Messines）村底下。他当时心里一阵振奋，想到我们的炮手打得这么准。此刻某个山头上的德国佬正为我方战线上的一蓬蓬烟振奋呢！……而他，蒂金斯，他……见鬼，他要掏出二百五十镑来，贴补同瓦伦丁·万诺普过日子的开销——等到人真*能*在一座山岗上站起来的时候……随便哪儿都行！

===Original===
The Adjutant, Notting, looked in and said:

“Brigade wants to know if we’re suffering any, sir?”

The Colonel surveyed Tietjens with irony:

“Well, what are you going to report?” he asked.… “This officer is taking over from me,” he said to Notting. Notting’s beady eyes and red-varnished cheeks expressed no emotions.

“Oh, tell Brigade,” the Colonel said, “that we’re all as happy as sand-boys. We could stand this till Kingdom come.” He asked: “We *aren’t* suffering any, are we?”

===Chinese===
副官诺丁探进头来，说：

「旅部问我们有没有吃亏，长官？」

上校揶揄地打量蒂金斯：

「那好，你打算怎么呈报？」他问。……「这位军官正在接我的手，」他对诺丁说。诺丁那双珠子眼和涂了红漆似的脸颊不露一丝表情。

「哦，告诉旅部，」上校说，「我们个个快活似神仙。这种炮我们能一直挨到世界末日。」他问：「我们*没*吃亏，对吧？」

===Original===
Notting said: “No, not in particular. C Company was grumbling that all its beautiful revetments had been knocked to pieces. The sentry near their own dugout complained that the pebbles in the gravel were nearly as bad as shrapnel.”

“Well, tell Brigade what I said. With Major Tietjen’s compliments, not mine. He’s in command.”

“… You may as well make a cheerful impression to begin with,” he added to Tietjens.

===Chinese===
诺丁说：「没有，没什么特别的。C连抱怨他们那些漂亮的壕壁加固全给砸烂了。他们掩蔽部旁边的哨兵抱怨说，砾石里的卵石砸下来差不多跟榴霰弹一样厉害。」

「那好，把我说的告诉旅部。以蒂金斯少校之敬意向旅部致意，不是以我的名义。他现在是指挥官。」

「……你上任伊始，不妨先留个喜气的印象，」他对蒂金斯补了一句。

===Original===
It was then that, suddenly, he burst out with:

“Look here! Lend me two hundred and fifty quid!”

He remained staring fixedly at Tietjens with an odd air of a man who has just asked a teasing, jocular conundrum.…

Tietjens had recoiled—really half an inch. The man said he was suffering from a loathsome disease: it was being near something dirty. You don’t contract loathsome diseases except from the cheapest kind of women or through being untidy-minded.… The man’s pals had gone back on him. That sort of man’s pals do go back on him! His accounts were all out.… He was in short the sort of swindling, unclean scoundrel to whom one lent money.… Irresistibly!

===Chinese===
正是在那时，他突然脱口喊道：

「听着！借我二百五十镑！」

他继续死死盯着蒂金斯，神情古怪，像一个刚抛出逗趣谜语的人。……

蒂金斯向后缩了一下——真的缩了半英寸。这人自己说过，他害着见不得人的恶疾：借钱给他，就是贴近一件脏东西。见不得人的恶疾，不是从最下等的女人身上、就是因为自己心思腌臜才招得上。……这家伙的哥儿们全撇了他。这种人种的哥儿们就偏会撇了他！他的账目一团糟。……总而言之，他正是那种骗人加腌臜的恶棍——人们肯借钱给的那种。……身不由主地肯！

===Original===
A crash of the sort that you couldn’t ignore, as is the case with certain claps in thunderstorms, sent a good deal of gravel down their cellar steps. It crashed against their shaky door. They heard Notting come out of his cellar and tell someone to shovel the beastly stuff back again where it had come from.

The Colonel looked up at the roof. He said that had knocked their parapet about a bit. Then he resumed his fixed gaze at Tietjens.

===Chinese===
一声没法装作没听见的巨响——正如雷雨里某几记炸雷那样——把不少砾石震下了他们的地窖台阶。石子哗啦啦砸在他们那扇摇摇晃晃的门上。他们听见诺丁从自己的地窖里出来，吩咐谁把那讨厌玩意儿从哪儿来的铲回哪儿去。

上校抬眼看了看屋顶。他说，那一下把他们的胸墙砸得不轻。然后他重新死死盯住蒂金斯。

===Original===
Tietjens said to himself:

“I’m losing my nerve.… It’s the damned news that Campion is coming… I’m becoming a wretched, irresolute Johnny.”

The Colonel said:

“I’m not a beastly sponger. I never borrowed before!” His chest heaved.… It really expanded and then got smaller again, the orifice in the khaki at his throat contracting.… Perhaps he never had borrowed before.…

===Chinese===
蒂金斯对自己说：

「我的胆气在一点点漏掉。……是坎皮恩要来的那条该死的消息……我正变成一个可怜巴巴、拿不定主意的家伙。」

上校说：

「我不是那种恶心的揩油虫。我从前从来没借过钱！」他的胸膛起伏。……真的鼓起来，又缩回去，喉头敞开的卡其领口也跟着收紧。……或许他真的从来没借过钱。……

===Original===
After all, it didn’t matter what kind of man this was, it was a question of what sort of a man Tietjens was becoming. He said:

“I can’t lend you the money. I’ll guarantee an overdraft to your agents. For two hundred and fifty.”

Well, then, he remained the sort of man who automatically lent money. He was glad.

===Chinese===
说到底，这个人是什么货色并不要紧，要紧的是蒂金斯正变成什么样的人。他说：

「现金我借不了。我可以向你的银行代理担保一笔透支。二百五十镑。」

好啊，这么说，他还是那种身不由主就借钱给人的角色。他很高兴。

===Original===
The Colonel’s face fell. His martially erect shoulders indeed collapsed. He exclaimed ruefully:

“Oh, I say, I thought you were the sort one could go to.”

Tietjens said:

“It’s the same thing. You can draw a cheque on your bank exactly as if I paid the money in.”

===Chinese===
上校的脸垮了下来。他那军人般挺直的双肩当真塌了下去。他懊恼地喊道：

「哦，我说，我还当你是那种可以上门开口的人呢。」

蒂金斯说：

「这是一回事。你照样可以开支票动你银行里的钱，跟我把现款存进去一模一样。」

===Original===
The Colonel said:

“I *can*? It’s the same thing? You’re *sure*?” His questions were like the pleas of a young woman asking you not to murder her.

… He obviously was not a sponger. He was a financial virgin. There could not be a subaltern of eighteen in the whole army who did not know what it meant to have an overdraft guaranteed after a fortnight’s leave.… Tietjens only wished they didn’t. He said:

===Chinese===
上校说：

「我*可以*？这是一回事？你*确定*？」他这一连串问话，活像一个年轻姑娘求你别杀她时的哀告。

……他显然不是揩油虫。他是个财务上的处子。全军上下，找不出一个十八岁的少尉不懂「休两个星期假回来便有人担保透支」是什么滋味。……蒂金斯只巴不得他们不懂。他说：

===Original===
“You’ve practically got the money in your hand as you sit there. I’ve only to write the letter. It’s impossible your agents should refuse my guarantee. If they do, I’ll raise the money and send it you.”

He wondered why he didn’t do that last in any case. A year or so ago he would have had no hesitation about overdrawing his account to any extent. Now he had an insupportable objection. Like a hatred!

He said:

“You’d better let me have your address.” He added, for his mind was really wandering a little. There was too much talk! “I suppose you’ll go to No. IX Red Cross at Rouen for a bit.”

===Chinese===
「你这么坐在那儿，钱实际上已经攥在手里了。我只消写一封信。你的银行代理不可能拒绝我的担保。他们要是拒了，我就筹出钱来寄给你。」

他纳闷，自己为何无论如何不肯走最后那一步。一年多以前，把账户透支到什么程度他都会毫不迟疑。如今他却有一种忍无可忍的抵触。像一种憎恨！

他说：

「你最好把地址留给我。」他又补了一句——他的脑筋真的有点儿散了。话说得太多了！「我估摸你要去鲁昂的第九红十字站（No. IX Red Cross）待上一阵。」

===Original===
The Colonel sprang to his feet:

“My God, what’s that?” he cried out. “Me… to No. IX.”

===Chinese===
上校霍地跳起来：

「我的天，那算什么？」他喊道，「我……去第九站。」

===Original===
Tietjens exclaimed:

“I don’t know the procedure. You said you had.…”

The other cried out:

“I’ve got cancer. A big swelling under the armpit.” He passed his hand over his bare flesh through the opening of his shirt, the long arm disappearing to the elbow. “Good God… I suppose when I said my pals had gone back on me you thought I’d asked them for help and been refused. I haven’t.… They’re all killed. That’s the worst way you can go back on a pal, isn’t it? Don’t you understand men’s language?”

===Chinese===
蒂金斯分辩道：

「我不清楚那些章程。你方才说你清楚……」

对面那位喊道：

「我得的是癌。腋下有个大肿块。」他的手从衬衫开衩处伸进去，抚过赤裸的皮肉，长胳膊一直没到肘部。「老天爷……我猜，我说哥儿们都撇下我的时候，你准以为我求过他们、被拒了。我没有。……他们全死了。撇下哥儿们，还有比这更绝的撇法吗，是不是？你听不懂男人的话？」

===Original===
He sat heavily down on his bed again.

He said:

“By jove: if you hadn’t promised to let me have the money there would have been nothing for me but to make a hole in the water.”

===Chinese===
他一屁股沉重地坐回床上。

他说：

「老天作证：要不是你答应借钱给我，我就只剩到水里去凿个窟窿这一条路了。」

===Original===
Tietjens said:

“Well, don’t contemplate it now. Get yourself well looked after. What does Derry say?”

The Colonel again started violently:

“Derry! The M.O.… Do you think I’d tell him! Or little squits of subalterns? Or any man! You understand now why I wouldn’t take Derry’s beastly pill. How do I know what it mightn’t do to.…”

===Chinese===
蒂金斯说：

「好了，如今别再打这个主意了。好好让人照顾你。德里怎么说？」

上校又猛地一激灵：

「德里！军医。……你以为我会告诉他！还是告诉那帮小毛头少尉？还是告诉任何人！你现在明白我为什么不肯吃德里那颗讨厌的药丸了吧。我怎么知道它保不齐会把那个……」

===Original===
Again he passed his hand under his armpit, his eyes taking on a yearning and calculating expression. He added:

“I thought it a duty to tell you as I was asking you for a loan. You might not get repaid. I suppose your offer still holds good?”

===Chinese===
他又把手伸到腋下，眼里泛起一种既渴切又盘算的神色。他补了一句：

「既然向你开口借钱，我觉得有义务告诉你。你的钱兴许收不回来。你那句话还算数吧？」

===Original===
Drops of moisture had hitherto made beads on his forehead; it now shone, uniformly wet.

“If you haven’t consulted anybody,” Tietjens said, “you mayn’t have got it. I should have yourself seen to right away. My offer still holds good!”

“Oh, I’ve got it, all right,” the Colonel answered with an air of infinite sapience. “My old man—my governor—had it. Just like that. And he never told a soul till three days before his death. Neither shall I.”

===Chinese===
此前，汗珠一颗颗挂在他额头上；此刻整片额头亮晶晶的，湿成一色。

「你要是还没找任何人看过，」蒂金斯说，「你也许并没得上。我劝你立刻去让人查一查。我的话仍然算数！」

「哦，我是得上了，错不了，」上校带着一股无限的先知先觉的神气答道，「我老子——我家老爷子——就得过。一模一样。他到死前三天才向一个人吐露。我也一样。」

===Original===
“I should get it seen to,” Tietjens maintained. “It’s a duty to your children. And the King. You’re too damn good a soldier for the Army to lose.”

“Nice of you to say so,” the Colonel said. “But I’ve stood too much. I couldn’t face waiting for the verdict.”

… It was no good saying he had faced worse things. He very likely hadn’t, being the man he was.

===Chinese===
「你该去把它治了，」蒂金斯坚持道，「这是对你儿女的义务。也是对国王的。你这样的好军人，军队损失不起。」

「多谢你这么说，」上校道，「可我已经承受得太多。等着听宣判，我受不了。」

……跟他说他连更糟的都熬过来了，无济于事。以他这个人而论，他多半的确没熬过更糟的。

===Original===
The Colonel said:

“Now if I could be any good!”

Tietjens said:

“I suppose I may go along the trenches now. There’s a wet place.…”

===Chinese===
上校说：

「唉，要是我还能顶些用场该多好！」

蒂金斯说：

「我想我现在可以沿战壕走一趟了。有一段泥水地……」

===Original===
He was determined to go along the trenches. He had to… what was it… “find a place to be alone with Heaven.” He maintained also his conviction that he must show the men his meal-sack of a body, mooning along; but attentive.

A problem worried him. He did not like putting it since it might seem to question the Colonel’s military efficiency. He wrapped it up: Had the Colonel any special advice as to keeping in touch with units on the right and left? And as to passing messages.

… That was a mania with Tietjens. If he had had his way he would keep the battalion day and night at communication drill. He had not been able to discover that any precautions of that sort were taken in that unit at all. Or in the others alongside.…

He had hit on the Colonel’s heel of Achilles.

===Chinese===
他是铁了心要走一趟战壕。他必须……那句话怎么说来着……「寻一处可与上苍独对的地方」。他也仍抱着那个信条：必须让弟兄们看看他那条面粉口袋似的身躯，一路梦游般踱过去；可神情是留神的。

有个难题困扰着他。他不愿意提，因为提了似乎就是在质疑上校的军事才干。他把话裹了一层说：上校对于同左右翼友邻单位保持联络，可有什么特别的高见？还有传令递信方面。

……那是蒂金斯的一桩痴念。他若能做主，会叫全营日夜操练通讯联络。他始终没能发现这个营里采取过任何这类预防措施。左右两翼的营里也没有。……

他正好撞在了上校的阿喀琉斯之踵上。

---

===Original===
In the open it became evident: more and more and more and always more evident! The news that General Campion was taking over that command had changed Tietjens’ whole view of the world.

The trenches were much as he had expected. They conformed indeed exactly to the image he had had in the cellar. They resembled heaps of reddish gravel laid out ready to distribute over the roads of parks. Getting out of the dugout had been like climbing into a trolley that had just been inverted for the purpose of discharging its load. It was a nasty job for the men, cleaving a passage and keeping under cover. Naturally the German sharpshooters were on the lookout. Our problem was to get as much of the trench as you could set up by daylight. The German problem was to get as many of our men as possible. Tietjens would see that our men stayed under cover until nightfall; the commander of the unit opposite would attend to the sniping of as many men as he could. Tietjens himself had three first-class snipers left: they would attempt to get as many of the German snipers as they could. That was self-defence.

===Chinese===
到了露天里，这事变得明明白白：越来越、越来越、越来越、永远越来越明明白白！坎皮恩将军即将接掌那个司令部的消息，改变了蒂金斯对整个世界的看法。

战壕跟他的料想相差无几。确确实实就是他在地窖里形成的那幅图景。像一堆堆摊开的淡红色砾石，等着分撒到公园的路上去。从掩蔽部里爬出来，就像往一辆刚翻倒过来卸货的翻斗车里爬。对弟兄们来说，一面劈开通路一面藏身掩体，是桩险差。德国狙击手自然都睁着眼守着。我们的难题是趁天亮把尽量多的战壕支立起来。德国人的难题是尽量多地撂倒我们的人。蒂金斯会看着我方的人守在掩体里直到天黑；对面部队的指挥官则自会照料，把能打中的人尽量打中。蒂金斯自己还剩三名一流的狙击手：他们会设法把德国狙击手尽量多地干掉。那是自卫。

===Original===
In addition a great many Enemy attentions would direct themselves to Tietjen’s stretch of the line. The artillery would continue to plunk in a shell or so from time to time. They would not do this very often because it would invite the attention of our artillery and that might prove too costly. More or less heavy masses of High Explosives would be thrown on to the line: what the Germans called *Minenwerfer* might project what our people called sausages. These being visible coming through the air you posted lookouts who gave you warning in time to get under cover. So the Germans had rather abandoned the use of these, probably as being costly in explosives and not so very effective. They made, that is to say, good holes but accounted for few men.

Airplanes with their beastly bullet-distributing hoppers—that is what they seemed like—would now and then duck along the trench, but not very often. The proceeding was, again, too costly: they would limit themselves as a rule to circling leisurely overhead and dropping things whilst the shrapnel burst round them—and spattered bullets over the trench. Flying pigs, aerial torpedoes, and other floating missiles, pretty, shining, silvery things with fins, would come through the air and would explode on striking the ground or after burying themselves. There was practically no end to their devices and the Huns had a new one every other week or so. They perhaps wasted themselves on new devices. A good many of them turned out to be duds. And a good many of their usually successful missiles turned out to be duds. They were undoubtedly beginning to feel the strain—mental and in their materials. So that if you had to be in these beastly places it was probably better to be in our trenches than theirs. Our war material was pretty good!

===Chinese===
此外，敌人的许多关照会落到蒂金斯这一段防线上。炮兵会继续时不时地扔进一发半发。他们不会干得太勤，因为那会招来我方炮兵的关照，未免太破费。或轻或重的高爆弹（H.E.）会砸到防线上：德国人所谓 *Minenwerfer*（迫击炮）的玩意儿，会抛出我方人员所谓的「香肠」。这些东西飞过天空时看得见，你就布置瞭望哨，来得及报警钻掩体。所以德国人已经相当弃用了这类家伙，多半因为费炸药又不很见效。也就是说，它们坑挖得漂亮，毙的人却寥寥。

飞机带着它们那撒布子弹的讨厌漏斗——看上去就是这么回事——偶尔会贴着战壕低掠一两回，但不很常见。这种营生同样太破费：它们照例只限于在头顶上慢悠悠盘旋，一面扔东西，任榴霰弹在四围炸开——把弹片溅泼到战壕上。飞猪、空中鱼雷，还有别的飘浮的投射物——些漂亮、锃亮、带鳍的银色玩意儿——会穿过空中，或触地即炸，或钻进土里再炸。他们的花样几乎无穷无尽，德国佬每隔一两个星期就翻新一样。他们或许把自己耗在新花样上了。不少花样到头来是哑弹。他们那些素来灵光的炮弹也有不少到头来是哑弹。他们无疑开始感到吃劲了——精神上和器材上都是。所以，倘若你注定要蹲在这些鬼地方，蹲在我们的战壕里多半比蹲在他们的强。我们的军工物资相当过硬！

===Original===
This was the war of attrition.… A mug’s game! A mug’s game as far as killing men was concerned, but not an uninteresting occupation if you considered it as a struggle of various minds spread all over the broad landscape in the sunlight. They did not kill many men and they expended an infinite number of missiles and a vast amount of thought. If you took six million men armed with loaded canes and stockings containing bricks or knives and set them against another six million men similarly armed, at the end of three hours four million on the one side and the entire six million on the other would be dead. So, as far as killing went, it really was a mug’s game. That was what happened if you let yourself get into the hands of the applied scientist. For all these things were the products not of the soldier but of hirsute, bespectacled creatures who peered through magnifying glasses. Or of course, on our side, they would be shaven-cheeked and less abstracted. They were efficient as slaughterers in that they enabled the millions of men to be moved. When you had only knives you could not move very fast. On the other hand, your knife killed at every stroke: you would set a million men firing at each other with rifles from eighteen hundred yards. But few rifles ever registered a hit. So the invention was relatively inefficient. And it dragged things out!

===Chinese===
这就是消耗战。……傻子玩的把戏！就杀人而论是傻子玩的把戏，可你若把它看作铺展在阳光下辽阔大地上的种种头脑的较量，倒也不是桩乏味的营生。他们杀不了多少人，却耗掉数不清的炮弹和天量的心血。你若叫六百万拿着灌铅手杖、拎着塞了砖头或刀子的长袜的人，去对阵同样武装的另外六百万，三个钟头下来，一边死四百万，另一边六百万死绝。所以，就杀人而论，这确乎是傻子玩的把戏。这就是把自己交到应用科学家手里的下场。因为这一切玩意儿都不是军人的产物，而是那些毛茸茸、戴眼镜、眯眼贴着放大镜瞧东西的生物的产物。当然，在我们这一边，他们刮净了脸蛋，也没那么心不在焉。他们作为屠宰者是称职的，因为他们让几百万人挪得动窝。只有刀子的时候你可挪不快。话又说回来，你的刀子刀刀致命：你可以设下一百万人端着步枪在一千八百码外对射。可几杆枪里才有一响打中。所以这项发明相对地没效率。还把事情拖得老长！

===Original===
And suddenly it had become boring.

They were probably going to spend a whole day during which the Germans would strain themselves, their intelligences flickering across the world, to kill a couple of Tietjens’ men, and Tietjens would exercise all his care in the effort not to have even one casualty. And at the end of the day they would all be very tired and the poor b——y men would have to set to work to repair the trenches in earnest. That was the ordinary day’s work.

He was going about it.… He had got A Company Commander to come up and talk to him about his fatigues. To the right of Headquarters the trenches appeared to have suffered less than to the left and it was possible to move quite a number of men without risk. A Company Commander was an astonishingly thin, bald man of fifty. He was so bald that his tin hat slid about all over his skull. He had been a small shipowner and must have married very late in life, for he spoke of having two children one of five, one of seven. A pigeon pair. His business was now making fifty thousand a year for him. It pleased Tietjens to think that his children would be well provided for if he were killed. A nice, silent, capable man who usually looked into the distance rather abstractedly when he talked. He was killed two months’ later, cleanly, by a bullet.

He was impatient that things had not got a move on. What had become of the big Hun strafe?

===Chinese===
而突然之间，这一切变得乏味了。

他们大概要这样耗掉一整天：德国人绷紧了神经、智慧在世界上空明明灭灭地闪，想弄死蒂金斯手下的一两个弟兄；蒂金斯则使出全副本事，力求一个伤亡都不出。到天黑，双方都疲惫不堪，可怜的、该——的弟兄们还得认认真真动工修壕。这就是一天的日常功课。

他正着手办这件事。……他把A连连长叫上来，商谈作业队的安排。营部以右的战壕受损似乎比以左轻，可以不担风险地调动相当数量的人手。A连连长是个瘦得惊人、五十岁的秃顶男人。他秃得厉害，钢盔在整个脑壳上打滑。他做过小船东，必定成婚很晚，因为他说起有两个孩子，一个五岁，一个七岁。一儿一女。他的生意如今一年给他挣五万镑。想到他若阵亡，孩子们的生活会有着落，蒂金斯觉得欣慰。一个和气、沉默、能干的人，说话时通常颇为恍惚地望着远方。两个月后，他阵亡了，干干净净，一粒子弹。

他不耐烦这局面还没有个动静。德国佬那场大炮击到底怎么了？

===Original===
Tietjens said:

“You remember the Hun company-sergeant-major that surrendered to your crowd the night before last? The fellow who said he was going to open a little sweet-stuff shop in the Tottenham Court Road with the company money he had stolen?… Or perhaps you did not hear?”

The remembrance of that shifty looking N.C.O. in blue-grey that was rather smart for a man coming in during a big fight stirred up intensely disagreeable feelings from the bottom of Tietjens’ mind. It was detestable to him to be in control of the person of another human being—as detestable as it would have been to be himself a prisoner… that thing that he dreaded most in the world. It was indeed almost more detestable, since to be taken prisoner was at least a thing outside your own volition, whereas to control a prisoner, even under the compulsion of discipline on yourself, implies a certain freewill of your own. And this had been an especially loathsome affair. Even normally, though it was irrational enough, prisoners affected him with the sense that they were unclean. As if they were maggots. It was not sensible; but he knew that if he had had to touch a prisoner he would have felt nausea. It was no doubt the product of his passionate Tory sense of freedom. What distinguished man from the brutes was his freedom. When then a man was deprived of freedom he became like a brute. To exist in his society was to live with brutes: like Gulliver amongst the Houynhms!

===Chinese===
蒂金斯说：

「你还记得前天夜里向你们那拨人投降的德国佬连队军士长吗？那个说要用偷来的公款去托特纳姆宫路（Tottenham Court Road）开一家小糖果铺的家伙？……兴许你没听说？」

对那个贼眉鼠眼、一身蓝灰色军服的军士（N.C.O.）的记忆——恶战正酣时跑过来投降的人，那身军装未免太齐整——从蒂金斯心底搅起一阵强烈的憎恶。掌控另一个人的肉身，在他看来是可憎的——几乎同他自己沦为俘虏一样可憎……俘虏，那是他在这个世界上最怕的东西。细究起来还更可憎，因为沦为俘虏至少是身不由己，而看管俘虏，哪怕你自己受着军纪的强制，也含着你自己的一份自由意志。而这又是一桩格外令人作呕的事。即便在平常，虽说毫无道理，俘虏总给他一种不洁之感。仿佛他们是蛆。这没有道理；可他知道，倘若他不得不碰一个俘虏，他会作呕。这无疑是他那炽烈的托利式自由意识的产物。人之所以异于禽兽，在于他的自由。那么，人被夺去自由，便堕同禽兽。存在于这种人的社会中，就是与禽兽为伍：就像格列佛身处慧骃（Houynhms）中间！

===Original===
And this unclean fellow had been a deserter in addition!

He had been brought in to the H.Q. dugout at three in the morning after the strafe had completely died out. It appeared that he had come over, ostensibly in the ordinary course of the attack. But he had lain all night in a shell hole, creeping in to our lines only when things were quiet. Previously to starting he had crammed his pockets with all the company money and even the papers that he could lay his hands on. He had been brought to H.Q. at that disagreeable hour because of the money and the papers, A Company judging that such things ought to be put in the hands at least of the Adjutant as quickly as possible.

===Chinese===
而这个不洁的家伙还是个逃兵！

他是在凌晨三点、炮击彻底消停之后被带进营部掩蔽部的。看情形，他是开过来的，表面上属于那次进攻的正常流程。可他在弹坑里趴了一整夜，等到四下安静才爬进我方防线。动身之前，他把连里的公款、连同抓得着手的公文，塞满了口袋。在那个别扭的钟点被送到营部，正因为钱和公文——A连认为，这类东西至少该尽快交到副官手里。

===Original===
The C.O., Mckechnie, the Intelligence Officer and the doctor had all, in addition to Tietjens himself, just settled in there, and the air of the smallish place was already fetid and reeking with service rum and whiskey. The appearance of the German had caused Tietjens almost to vomit, and he was already in a state of enervation from having had to bring the battalion in. His temples were racked with a sort of neuralgia that he believed to be caused by eyestrain.

Normally, the questioning of prisoners before they reached Division was strongly discountenanced, but a deserter excites more interest than an ordinary prisoner, and the C.O. who was by then in a state of hilarious mutiny absolutely ordered Tietjens to get all he could out of the prisoner. Tietjens knew a little German: the Intelligence Officer who knew that language well had been killed. Dunne, replacing him, had no German.

The shifty, upright, thin, dark fellow with even unusually uneasy eyes, had answered questions readily enough: Yes, the Huns were fed-up with the war; discipline had become so difficult to maintain that one of his reasons for deserting had been sheer weariness over the effort to keep the men under him in order. They had no food. It was impossible to get the men, in an advance, past any kind of food dumps. He was continually being unjustly reprimanded for his want of success, and standing there he cursed his late officers! Nevertheless, when the C.O. made Tietjens ask him some questions about an Austrian gun that the Germans had lately introduced to that front and that threw a self-burying shell containing an incredible quantity of H.E., the fellow had clicked his heels together and had answered:

===Chinese===
指挥官（C.O.）、麦克凯克尼、情报军官和军医，连蒂金斯本人在内，都刚在那儿落了脚，那间不大的地方空气已经腐浊，弥漫着配给朗姆酒和威士忌的气味。德国佬一露面，蒂金斯几乎吐出来，而他为带营进来本已是一副脱力的状态。他的太阳穴被一种神经痛折磨着，他相信那是用眼过度所致。

按规矩，战俘在押到师部之前先行审问是严遭申斥的，可逃兵比普通战俘更撩人兴趣，而那时的指挥官正处在一种乐呵呵的犯上状态里，索性厉声命令蒂金斯从俘虏嘴里榨出一切。蒂金斯懂一点德语：精通德语的那位情报军官已经阵亡。接替他的邓恩不懂德语。

那个贼眉鼠眼、身板笔直、又瘦又黑、一双眼睛出奇不安的家伙，答问倒相当爽快：是的，德国佬受够了这场战争；军纪已经难以为继，他开小差的原因之一，就是管束手下那些人实在管得精疲力竭。他们没有吃的。进攻的时候，想让队伍经过任何食品堆栈而不停下，根本不可能。他因为无功而屡遭不公的训斥，站在那儿，他把他的前长官们骂了个遍！然而，当指挥官让蒂金斯问起一门奥地利火炮——德国人新近调到那段前线、能抛自埋炮弹、装药量大得不可思议的那种——那家伙咔地一声并拢脚跟，答道：

===Original===
“*Nein, Herr Offizier, das waere Landesverratung!*”… to answer that would be to betray one’s country. His psychology had been difficult to grasp. He had explained as well as he could, using a few words of English, the papers that he had brought over. They were mostly exhortations to the German soldiers, circulars containing news of disasters to and the demoralisation of the Allied troops; there were also a few returns of no great interest—mostly statistics of influenza cases. But when Tietjens had held before the fellow’s eyes a typewritten page with a heading that he had now forgotten, the Sergeant had exclaimed: “*Ach, nicht das!*”… and had made as if to snatch the paper from Tietjens’ fingers. Then he had desisted, realising that he was risking his life, no doubt. But he had become as pale as death, and had refused to translate the phrases that Tietjens did not understand; and indeed Tietjens understood practically none of the words, which were all technical.

He knew the paper contained some sort of movement orders; but he was by that time heartily sick of the affair and he knew that that was just the sort of paper that the staff did not wish men in the line to meddle with. So he dropped the matter, and the Colonel and the Pals being by that time tired of listening and not grasping what was happening, Tietjens had sent the fellow at the double back to Brigade under the charge of the Intelligence Officer and a heavier escort than was usual.

===Chinese===
「*Nein, Herr Offizier, das waere Landesverratung!*」（不，长官先生，那就是叛国！）……回答那个，就是背叛自己的国家。他的心理难以捉摸。他借着几个英文词，尽力解释了他带过来的那些公文。多半是给德军士兵的劝谕传单，通报协约军惨败与军心瓦解的消息；还有几份没什么意思的报表——多半是流感病例统计。可当蒂金斯把一页打字稿举到那家伙眼前——抬头是什么，他如今已经忘了——那军士喊了出来：「*Ach, nicht das!*」（啊，别是那个！）……并做出要从蒂金斯指间抢夺那页纸的架势。随即他缩了手，无疑是意识到那是在拿命冒险。可他变得面如死灰，并且拒绝翻译蒂金斯看不懂的那些字句；说实在的，那些字句蒂金斯几乎一个词也不懂，全是术语。

他知道那页纸上是某种调动命令；可他那时已经对这桩事烦透了，而且他知道，这正是参谋们不愿让前线的人染指的那种纸。于是他把事情撂下了，而上校和友伴营的弟兄们那时也听累了、摸不清究竟出了什么事，蒂金斯便让情报军官押着那家伙，带上比寻常加重的卫兵，一路跑步送回旅部。

===Original===
What remained to Tietjens of the affair was the expression that the fellow had used when asked what he was going to do with the Company money he had stolen. He was going to open a little sweet shop in the Tottenham Court Road. He had, of course, been a waiter: in old Compton Street. Tietjens wondered vaguely what would become of him. What did they do with deserters? Perhaps they interned them: perhaps they made them N.C.O.’s in prisoners’ units. He could never go back to Germany.… That remained to him—and the horror and loathing he had felt at the episode: as if it had caused him personal deterioration. He had put the matter out of his mind.

It occurred to him now that, very likely, the urgent announcements from Staff of all sorts had been inspired by that very paper! The paper that loathsome fellow had tried to grab at. He remembered that he had been feeling so sick that he hadn’t bothered to have the man handcuffed.… It raised a number of questions: Does a man desert and at the same time refuse to betray his country? Well, he might. There was no end to the contradictions in men’s characters. Look at the C.O. An efficient officer and a muddled ass in one: even in soldiering matters!

On the other hand, the whole thing might be a plant of the Huns. The paper—the movement order—might have been meant to reach our Army Headquarters. On the face of it, important movement orders do not lie about in Company offices. Not usually. The Huns might be trying to call our attention to this part of the line whilst their real attack might be coming somewhere else. That again was unlikely because that particular part of the line was so weak owing to poor General Puffles’ unpopularity with the great ones at home that the Huns would be mad if they attacked anywhere else. And the French were hurrying up straight to that spot in terrific force. He might then be a hero!… But he didn’t look like a hero!

===Chinese===
这桩事留给蒂金斯的，是那家伙被问到打算怎么处置偷来的连队公款时所用的那句话。他要去托特纳姆宫路开一家小糖果铺。他从前，不消说，是个跑堂的：在老康普顿街。蒂金斯迷迷糊糊地想，此人往后会有什么下场。他们对逃兵是怎么处置的？也许拘押起来：也许叫他们在战俘营里当军士。他是回不去德国了。……留给他的就是这些——还有他在那一幕里感到的恐惧与憎恶：仿佛那件事使他自己也堕落了似的。他把这事从心里撂开了。

他现在想到，参谋部各方面那些紧急通报，很可能正是那页纸招出来的！那页令人作呕的家伙想要抢走的纸。他记得，自己当时觉得那么恶心，以至懒得叫人给那家伙上镣铐。……这就引出一串问题：一个人会一面开小差、一面拒绝背叛自己的祖国吗？咳，也可能。人的性格里的矛盾没有个完。看看指挥官：干练的军官和昏聩的蠢驴合于一身：连军务上也如此！

反过来，整桩事也可能是德国佬设的局。那页纸——那道调动命令——也许本来就是打算送到我方集团军司令部去的。明摆着，要紧的调动命令不会乱丢在连部办公室里。通常不会。德国佬或许想把我们注意力引到这段防线上，而真正的进攻落在别处。可这又不象，因为恰是那段防线最薄弱——都怪可怜的帕夫尔斯将军在国内大人物那儿不吃香——德国佬要是改打别处才是疯了。何况法军正以雷霆之势直扑那一点。那么，他倒可能是位英雄！……可他那长相不像英雄！

===Original===
This sort of complication was wearisome nowadays, though once it would have delighted him to dwell on it and work it out with nice figures and calculations of stresses. Now his only emotion about the matter was that, thank God, it was none of his job. The Huns didn’t appear to be coming.

He found himself regretting that the strafe was not coming after all. That was incredible. How could he regret not being put into immediate danger of death?

===Chinese===
这类缠夹不清的难题如今叫他疲于应付，搁在从前，他会兴致勃勃地推敲它，用精确的数字和应力演算把它推演出来。此刻他对这件事唯一的情绪是：谢天谢地，这不归他管。德国佬看样子不来了。

他发现自己竟在惋惜那场炮击终究不来。这不可置信。他怎么会惋惜自己没被立即置于死地？

===Original===
Long, thin, scrawny and mournful, with his tin hat now tilted forwards over his nose, the O.C. A Company gazed into futurity and remarked:

“I’m sorry the Huns aren’t coming!”

He was sorry the Huns were not coming. Because if they came they might as well come according to the information supplied by that prisoner. He had captured that fellow. He might as well therefore get the credit. It might get him remembered if he put in for leave. He wanted leave. He wanted to see his children. He had not seen them for two years now. Children of five and seven change a good deal in two years. He grumbled on. Without any shame at the revelation of his intimate motives. The quite ordinary man! But he was perfectly to be respected. He had a rather grating chest voice. It occurred to Tietjens that that man would never see his children.

===Chinese===
A连连长细长、干瘦、愁眉苦脸，钢盔这会儿冲前扣到鼻梁上，凝望着未来，说：

「德国佬不来，我还遗憾呢！」

他是真遗憾德国佬不来。因为倘若来，倒不妨就照那俘虏提供的情报来。那家伙是他抓的。功劳索性也该归他。他若申请休假，这或许能让人记起他。他要休假。他要见他的孩子。他已经两年没见他们了。五岁和七岁的孩子，两年里变化很大。他絮絮地抱怨下去。把自己这点私心抖搂出来，没有一丝羞愧。一个顶平常的人！可又完全值得尊敬。他有一副有点沙哑的胸音。蒂金斯心里掠过一个念头：那个人永远见不着他的孩子了。

===Original===
He wished these intimations would not come to him. He found himself at times looking at the faces of several men and thinking that this or that man would shortly be killed. He wished he could get rid of the habit. It seemed indecent. As a rule he was right. But then, almost every man you looked at there was certain to get killed.… Himself excepted. He himself was going to be wounded in the soft place behind the right collarbone.

He regretted that the strafe was not that morning coming! Because if they came they might as well come according to the information supplied by the prisoner he had examined in the stinking dugout. His unit had captured the fellow. He would now be signing its H.Q. chits as Acting O.C. Ninth Glamorganshires. So he, Tietjens, had captured that fellow. And his perspicacity in having him sent immediately back to brigade with his precious paper might get him, Tietjens, remembered favourably at Brigade H.Q. Then they would leave him in temporary command of his battalion. And if they did that he might do well enough to get a battalion of his own!

He astounded himself.… His mentality was that of O.C. A Company!

===Chinese===
他巴不得这些预感别缠上他。他发觉自己时不时盯着几张脸看，心里想这个或那个人不久就会阵亡。他希望能改掉这个习惯。那样似乎不体面。而他的预感照例是对的。不过话说回来，那儿你望见的几乎每个人都注定要阵亡。……唯他除外。他自己呢，将负伤，伤在右锁骨后头的软处。

他遗憾那场炮击那天早晨不来！因为倘若来，倒不妨就照他在那个臭烘烘的掩蔽部里审过的俘虏所供的情报来。俘虏是他的部队抓的。往后他就要以第九格拉摩根郡团代理指挥官（Acting O.C.）的身份签营部的条子了。所以说，他蒂金斯抓了那家伙。而他那当机立断把人连人带那页要命的纸即刻送回旅部的先见之明，兴许能让蒂金斯在旅部留下个好念想。那他们就会让他继续暂时统带这个营。若真如此，他兴许干得够漂亮，捞一个自己的营！

他把自己惊着了。……他这心思，竟同A连连长一个样！

===Original===
He said:

“It was damn smart of you to see that fellow was of importance and have him sent at the double to me.” O.C. A Coy. grew red over all his grim face. So, one day, he, Tietjens, might flush with pleasure at the words of some squit with a red band round his hat!

He said:

“Even if the Germans don’t come it might have been helpful. It might have been even more helpful. It might have been the means of keeping them back.” Because of course if the Germans knew that we had got hold of their Movement Order they might change their plans. That would inconvenience them. It was not likely. There was perhaps not time for the news that we knew to have got through to their Important Ones. But it was possible. Such things had happened.

===Chinese===
他说：

「你能看出那家伙要紧、派人跑步把他送到我这儿来，真他娘的机灵。」A连连长铁板的整张脸涨红了。原来，有朝一日，他蒂金斯也会因为哪个帽箍红圈的毛头参谋一句夸奖而红光满面！

他说：

「就算德国人不来，这事也可能帮了忙。甚至可能帮了大忙。可能正是叫他们缩回去的那一手。」因为，当然了，倘若德国人知道我们拿到了他们的调动命令，兴许会改计划。那会叫他们不便。可能性不大。我们知情这一消息，或许来不及传到他们那些大人物耳朵里。但也不是不可能。这种事是有过的。

===Original===
Aranjuez and the Lance-Corporal stood still and so silent in the sunlight that they resembled fragments of the reddish trench. The red gravel of the trenches began here, however, to be smirched with more agricultural marl. Later the trenches became pure alluvial soil and then ran down more smartly into stuff so wet that it was like a quicksand. A bog. It was there he had tried revetting with a syphon-drain. The thought of that extreme of his line reminded him. He said:

“You know all about keeping in communication with immediately neighbouring units?”

The grim fellow said:

“Only what they taught in the training camps at the beginning of the war, sir. When I joined up. It was fairly thorough but it’s all forgotten now.”

===Chinese===
阿兰胡埃斯和那名下士立在日光里，静得纹丝不动，活像淡红色战壕的碎块。只是到此为止，战壕的红砾石开始沾上更多田间的泥灰；再往前，战壕变成纯粹的冲积土，随后更利落地陷进湿得像流沙的底子里去。一片泥沼。他就是在那儿试过用虹吸排水法加固壕壁。想起他防线的那个极点，提醒了他这桩事。他说：

「同紧邻单位保持通讯联络，你全在行吗？」

那铁板脸的家伙说：

「只有开战那阵子训练营里教的那些，长官。我入伍那会儿。教得还算全，如今全忘光了。」

===Original===
Tietjens said to Aranjuez:

“You’re Signalling Officer. What do you know about keeping in communication with units on your right and left?”

Aranjuez, blushing and stammering, knew all about buzzers and signals. Tietjens said:

“That’s only for trenches, all that. But, in motion. At your O.T.C. Didn’t they practice you in keeping communication between troops in motion?”

===Chinese===
蒂金斯对阿兰胡埃斯说：

「你是信号军官。同左右翼单位保持通讯联络，你知道多少？」

阿兰胡埃斯红着脸、结结巴巴，把蜂音器和信号讲得头头是道。蒂金斯说：

「那一套全只适用于战壕。可在运动中呢。在你们的军官训练团（O.T.C.），他们没操练过你们在运动中的部队之间保持联络吗？」

===Original===
They hadn’t at the O.T.C.… At first it had been in the programme. But it had always been crowded out by some stunt. Rifle-grenade drill. Bomb-throwing. Stokes-gun drill. Any sort of machine drill as long as it was not moving bodies of men over difficult country—sand-hills, say—and hammering into them that they must keep in touch unit with unit or drop connecting files if a unit itself divided up.

It was perhaps the dominant idea of Tietjens, perhaps the main idea that he got out of warfare—that at all costs you must keep in touch with your neighbouring troops. When, later, he had to command the escorts over immense bodies of German prisoners on the march it several times occurred to him to drop so many connecting files for the benefit of the men or N.C.O.’s—or even the officers, of his escort who had fallen out through sheer fatigue or disease, that he would arrive in a new camp at the day’s end with hardly any escort left at all—say thirty for three thousand prisoners. The business of an escort being to prevent the escape of prisoners it might have been thought better to retain the connecting files for that purpose. But, on the other hand, he never lost a prisoner except by German bombs, and he never lost any of his stragglers at all.

===Chinese===
军官训练团没操练过。……起初是列在计划里的。可总是被什么花活挤掉。枪榴弹操练。投弹。斯托克斯炮操练。但凡器械操练都行，唯独不练怎么把成队的兵拉过复杂地形——比如说沙丘——并且把一条道理敲进他们脑子：必须单位与单位保持接触，不然就得在本单位自身分散开时沿途留下联络哨。

这或许就是蒂金斯的主导观念，或许是他从战争里得来的头一条道理——不惜一切代价，必须同左右友邻部队保持接触。到后来，当他不得不指挥押送队伍、押着大群大群的德国俘虏行军时，有好几回他想给掉队的人留这么多联络哨——为那些纯粹因为力竭或疾病掉队的押送兵、军士——甚至军官——以至一天行军抵达新营地时，押送的人几乎一个不剩——比如三千俘虏剩三十个。押送的本职既然是防止俘虏脱逃，照理该把联络哨留作此用才好。可另一方面，他从没丢过一个俘虏——除了叫德国炸弹炸死的——而掉队的人，他更是一个也没丢过。

===Original===
… He said to O.C. A Company:

“Please look after this matter in your Company. I shall arrange as soon as I can to transfer you to the outside right of the unit. If the men are doing nothing lecture them, please, yourself on this subject and talk very seriously to all lance-corporals, section leaders and oldest privates of platoons. And be good enough to get into communication at once with the Company Commander of the Wiltshires immediately on our right. In one of two ways the war is over. The war of trenches. Either the Germans will immediately drive us into the North Sea or we shall drive them back. They will then be in a state of demoralisation and we shall need to move fast. Lieutenant Aranjuez, you will arrange to be present when Captain Gibbs talks to his Company and you will repeat what he says in the other Companies.”

He was talking quickly and distinctly, as he did when he was well, and he was talking stiltedly on purpose. He could not obviously call an officers’ conference with a German attack possibly impending; but he was pretty certain that something of what he said would penetrate to nearly every ear of the Battalion if he said it before a Company Commander, a Signalling Lieutenant and an Orderly-room Lance-Corporal. It would go through that the Old Man was dotty on this joke, and Sergeants would see that some attention was paid to the matter. So would the officers. It was all that could be done at the moment.

He walked behind Gibbs along the trench which at this point was perfectly intact and satisfactory, the red gravel gradually giving place to marle. He remarked to the good fellow that in that way they would do something to checkmate the blasted civilians whose meddling with the processes of war had put them where they were. Gibbs agreed gloomily that civilian interference had lost the war. They so hated the regular army that whenever a civilian saw a trace of regular training remaining in this mud-fighting that they liked us to indulge in, he wrote a hundred letters under different names to the papers, and the War Secretary at once took steps to retain that hundred votes; Gibbs had been reading a home-newspaper that morning.

===Chinese===
……他对A连连长说：

「这件事请在贵连抓一抓。我会尽快设法把贵连调到本营最右翼的外侧。弟兄们若闲着，请你亲自就这个题目给他们讲课，并且同各排的下士、班长和资历最老的列兵郑重地谈一谈。还请即刻同紧挨我们右翼的威尔特郡团那位连长取得联络。堑壕战这场战争，不这样了结、便那样了结。要么德国人马上把我们赶进北海，要么我们把他们赶回去。到那时他们将陷于崩溃，我们就得快速机动。阿兰胡埃斯中尉，吉布斯上尉对本连讲话时，你要到场，之后到其他各连复述他的话。」

他说得又快又清楚——他身子爽利时便是如此——并且故意说得拿腔拿调。德军进攻眼看可能来，他显然召集不了军官会议；可他相当有把握：只要当着一位连长、一位信号中尉和一名文书室下士把这番话说出来，这话便会钻进全营几乎每一只耳朵。这话会传遍全营：老头子在这桩事上犯了痴；军士们自会看着把这当回事。军官们也会。眼下能做到的，就只有这些。

他跟在吉布斯身后沿战壕走；这一段战壕完好无损、令人满意，红砾石渐渐让位给泥灰。他对这位好好先生说，这样他们便能多少将死那些混账平民——正是他们对战争进程的插手，才把大家摁到眼下这步田地。吉布斯阴郁地附和：平民的干涉把战争输掉了。他们恨正规军恨到这个地步：哪个平民只要瞧见一点正规训练的残迹还留在这场他们乐意让我们沉溺的泥浆战里，他就化一百个名字给报纸写一百封信，陆军大臣便立刻设法保住那一百张选票；吉布斯那天早晨刚读了国内报纸。

===Original===
Tietjens surprised himself by saying:

“Oh, we’ll beat them yet!” It was an expression of impracticable optimism. He sought to justify his words by saying that their Army Commanders having put up such a damn good fight in spite of the most criminal form of civilian interference had begun to put a stopper on their games. Campion’s coming was a proof that soldiers were going to be allowed to have some say in the conduct of the war. It meant the single command.… Gibbs expressed a muted satisfaction. If the French took over those lines as they certainly would if they had the Single Command he would no doubt be able to go home and see his children. All their divisions would have to be taken out of the lines to be reorganised and brought up to strength.

Tietjens said:

“As to what we were talking about.… Supposing you detailed outside section leaders and another file to keep in touch with the Wiltshires and they did the same. Supposing that for purposes of recognition they wore handkerchiefs round their right and left arms respectively.… It has been done.…”

===Chinese===
蒂金斯说出下面这句话时，把自己都吓了一跳：

「哦，我们终究会打赢他们！」这是一种不切实际的乐观。他试图给自己的话找依据：他们的集团军司令们顶着罪大恶极的平民干涉还打得这么漂亮，已开始封堵那些人的把戏。坎皮恩的到来便是一个凭据：军人就要获准在战争指导上说上些话了。这意味着统一指挥。……吉布斯表示了一种不响亮的满意。倘若法国人接管这些防线——只要统一指挥成立，他们一定会——他无疑就能回国见他的孩子了。他们所有的师都得撤出防线，重新整编、补充足额。

蒂金斯说：

「至于我们方才谈的。……假定你派出外围的各班长加一名步枪兵去同威尔特郡团保持接触，他们也照样办。再假定为了识别，他们分别在左、右臂上系手帕。……这法子有人用过。……」

===Original===
“The Huns,” Captain Gibbs said grimly, “would probably pick them off specially. They’d probably pick off specially anyone who had any sort of badge. So you would be worse off.”

They were going at his request to look at a section of his trench. Orderly Room had ordered him to make arrangements for machine-gun performances there. He couldn’t. It didn’t exist. Nothing existed. He supposed that to have been the new Austrian gun. New probably, but why Austrian? The Austrians did not usually interest themselves much in High Explosives. This one, whatever it was, threw something that buried itself and then blew up half the universe. With astonishingly little noise and commotion. Just lifted up. Like a hippopotamus. He, Gibbs, had hardly noticed anything as you would have if it had been say a mine. When they came and told him that a mine had gone off there he would not believe them.… But you could see for yourself that it looked exactly as if a mine had been chucking things about. A small mine. But still a mine.…

In the shelter of the broken end of the trench a fatigue of six men worked with pick and shovel, patiently, two at a time. They threw up mud and stones and patted them and, stepping down into the thus created vacancy, threw up more mud and stones. Water oozed about, uncertain where to go. There must be a spring there. That hillside was honeycombed with springs.…

===Chinese===
「德国佬，」吉布斯上尉狠狠地说，「多半会专门挑他们打。但凡带点什么记号的人，他们多半都专门挑出来打。那你反倒更吃亏。」

他们是应他的要求，去看他战壕的一段。文书室曾命令他在那儿安排机枪发扬火力的事。他安排不了。那段壕不存在了。什么都不存在了。他估摸那准是那门新式奥地利炮。多半是新的，可为什么是奥地利炮？奥地利人通常对高爆弹不甚上心。这一门，不管它是什么，抛出的东西先自己钻进土里，然后炸翻半个乾坤。声响和骚动却小得惊人。就那么往上一拱。像一头河马。他，吉布斯，几乎什么都没觉察出来——若换作，比如说，坑道炸药，你总归会觉察的。人家来告诉他那儿炸了坑道炸药时，他还不肯信。……可你亲眼一看便知，那模样分明是坑道炸药抛腾过东西。一个小炸药。可到底还是炸药。……

在战壕断裂口一端的遮蔽下，一支六人作业队抡着镐和锹，耐心地干着，一次两人。他们把泥和石头抛上来、拍实，然后跨进由此腾出的空当，再抛上更多的泥和石头。水四处渗淌，拿不准往哪里去。那儿底下必有泉眼。那面山坡让泉眼蛀成了蜂窝。……

===Original===
You would certainly have said there had been a mine there. If we had been advancing it would have been a small mine left by the Huns to cheer us up. But we had retreated on to ground we had always held. So it couldn’t have been a mine.

Also it kicked the ground forward and backward and relatively little laterally, so that the deep hole it had created more resembled the entry into a rudimentary shaft than the usually circular shell hole. A mound existed between Tietjens and B Company trench, considerably higher than you could see over. A vast mound; a miniature Primrose Hill. But much bigger than anything they had seen created by flying pigs or other aerial missiles as yet. Anyhow the mound was high enough to give Tietjens a chance to get round it in cover and shuffle down into B Company’s line. He said to Gibbs:

“We shall have to see about that machine gun place. Don’t come any further with me. Make those fellows keep their heads down and send them back if the Huns seem like sending over any more dirt.”

===Chinese===
你准会说那儿炸过坑道炸药。若是我们进攻，那便是德国佬留下的、给我们助兴的小炸药。可我们是退到自始至终握在手里的地盘上来的。所以不可能是炸药。

再说，它把地面朝前后掀，朝两侧掀得相对少，于是它掏出的那个深坑，与其说像寻常滚圆的弹坑，不如说像一口粗具规模的矿井入口。在蒂金斯与B连战壕之间隆起一座土丘，高得远过人头顶。一座大土丘；一座小号的樱草山（Primrose Hill，伦敦山冈）。可比他们迄今见过飞猪或其他空中投射物堆出来的任何东西都大得多。好在土丘够高，足以让蒂金斯借着掩蔽绕过它，蹭下坡溜进B连的防线。他对吉布斯说：

「那个机枪位置我们得想想法子。别再陪我往前走了。叫那几个家伙把头压低，德国佬要是像要再扔泥土过来，就把他们撤回去。」

### VI / 第六章

===Original===
Tietjens reclined on the reverse slope of the considerable mound. In the sunlight. He had to be alone. To reflect on his sentimental situation and his machine guns. He had been kept so out of the affairs of the unit that he had suddenly remembered that he knew nothing whatever about his machine guns, or even about the fellow who had to look after him. A new fellow called Cobbe, who looked rather vacant, with an immense sunburnt nose and an open mouth. Not, on the face of him, alert enough for his job. But you never knew.

He was hungry. He had eaten practically nothing since seven the night before, and had been on his feet the greater part of the time.

He sent Lance-Corporal Duckett to A Company dugout, to ask if they could favour him with a sandwich and some coffee with rum in it: he sent Second-Lieutenant Aranjuez to B Company to tell them that he was coming to take a look round on their men and quarters. B Company Commander for the moment was a very young boy just out from an O.T.C. It was annoying that he had an outside Company. But Constantine, the former Commander, had been killed the night before last. He was, in fact, said to be the gentleman whose remains hung in the barbed wire which was what made Tietjens doubtful whether it could be he. He should not have been so far to the left if he had been bringing his Company in. Anyhow, there had been no one to replace him but this boy—Bennett. A good boy. So shy that he could hardly give a word of command on parade, but yet with all his wits about him. And blessed with an uncommonly experienced Company Sergeant-Major. One of the original old Glamorganshires. Well, beggars could not be choosers! The Company had reported that morning five cases of the influenza that was said to be ravaging the outside world. Here then was another thing for which they had to thank the outside world—this band of ragtime solitaries! They let the outside world severely alone; they were, truly, hermits. Then the outside world did this to them. Why not leave them to their monastic engrossedness?

Even the rotten and detestable Huns had it! They were said by the Divisional news-sheets to have it so badly that whole Divisions were incapable of effective action. That might be a lie, invented for the purpose of heartening us; but it was probably true. The German men were apparently beastly underfed, and, at that, only on substitute-foods of relatively small percentage of nutritive value. The papers brought over by that N.C.O. had certainly spoken urgently of the necessity of taking every precaution against the spread of this flail. Another circular violently and lachrymosely assured the troops that they were as well fed as the civilian populations and the Corps of Officers. Apparently there had been some sort of scandal. A circular of which he had not had time to read the whole ended up with an assertion something like: “Thus the honour of the Corps of Officers has been triumphantly vindicated.”

It was a ghastly thought, that of that whole vast territory that confronted them, filled with millions of half-empty stomachs that bred disorders in the miserable brains. Those fellows must be the most miserable human beings that had ever existed. God knows, the life of our own Tommies must be Hell. But those fellows.… It would not bear thinking of.

===Chinese===
蒂金斯斜倚在那座颇大的土丘背坡上。晒着太阳。他得独处。好琢磨琢磨他的感情处境和他的机枪。他一直被摈在本营事务之外，摈到这个地步：他猛然想起，他对自己的机枪竟一无所知，甚至连那个该照料他的人也不了解。一个叫科布（Cobbe）的新来的家伙，看上去相当茫然，晒得黢黑的大鼻子，张着一张嘴。就外表看，不够机灵，配不上他的差事。不过这可说不准。

他饿了。从头天晚上七点起他几乎什么都没吃，而且大部分时间都站着。

他派达克特下士去 A 连掩蔽部，问他们能否赏他一份三明治和一杯掺朗姆酒的咖啡：又派阿兰胡埃斯少尉去 B 连，通知他们他要过去查看人员与驻地。B 连现任连长是个刚出军官训练团（O.T.C.）的少年。让他管一个外围连，着实恼人。可原连长康斯坦丁前晚阵亡了。据说，挂在铁丝网上的遗骸正是那位先生——这也正是蒂金斯犯疑、未必是他的缘故。他若真是把连队带回来，不该跑到那么靠左的地方去。无论如何，能顶替他的只有这孩子——贝内特（Bennett）。好孩子。害臊得阅兵时几乎喊不出口令，脑子却一点不缺。还托庇于一位经验出奇老到的连军士长。老格拉摩根郡团（Glamorganshires）出身的一个。唉，要饭的哪挑得起饭！连里那天早晨上报了五例流感——据说外面那个世界正被流感蹂躏。这又是他们得谢外面世界的一桩——他们这帮拉格泰姆的隐士！他们对外面世界敬而远之；他们实实在在是隐修士。外面世界却对他们来这一手。何不随他们去守他们的修道苦功呢？

连腐烂透顶、可憎可恶的德国佬都染上了它！师部简报上说他们病得整师整师失去战斗力。那可能是瞎编出来给我们鼓劲的；但多半是真的。德国兵显然饿得半死，饶是如此，吃的还只是营养价值低得可怜的代用食品。那个军士（N.C.O.）带过来的文件确实言辞急切，说务须严防这记连枷瘟神蔓延。另一份通报声泪俱下地拼命向部队保证，他们吃得和老百姓、和军官团一样好。看来是出过什么丑闻。还有一份通报他没来得及读完，结尾大约是这样一句断言：「军官团的名誉就此大获昭雪。」

一想到他们当面的那一整片广袤地域，塞满几百万个半空的肠胃、在可怜的脑髓里孕育出种种错乱，就令人毛骨悚然。那些家伙准是人类有史以来最可怜的人。天知道，我们自己汤米的日子也准是地狱。可那些家伙。……简直不敢想下去。

===Original===
And it was curious to consider how the hatred that one felt for the inhabitants of those regions seemed to skip in a wide trajectory over the embattled ground. It was the civilian populations and their rulers that one hated with real hatred. Now the swine were starving the poor devils in the trenches!

They were detestable. The German fighters and their Intelligence and staffs were merely boring and grotesque. Unending nuisances. For he was confoundedly irritated to think of the mess they had made of his nice clean trenches. It was like when you go out for an hour and leave your dog in the drawing-room. You come back and find that it has torn to pieces all your sofa-cushions. You would like to knock its head off.… So you would like to knock the German soldiers’ heads off. But you did not wish them much real harm. Nothing like having to live in that hell on perpetually half empty, windy stomachs with the nightmares they set up! Naturally influenza was decimating them.

Anyhow, Germans were the sort of people that influenza *would* bowl over. They were bores because they came forever true to type. You read their confounded circulars and they made you grin whilst a little puking. They were like continual caricatures of themselves and they were continually hysterical.… Hypochondriacal.… Corps of Officers.… Proud German Army.… His Glorious Majesty.… Mighty Deeds.… Not much of the Ragtime Army about that, and that was welling out continuously all the time.… Hypochondria!

A ragtime army was not likely to have influenza so badly. It felt neither its moral nor its physical pulse.… Still, here was influenza in B Company.
