They must have got it from the Huns the night before last. B Company had had them jump in on top of them; then and there had been hand-to-hand fighting. It was a nuisance. B Company was a nuisance. It had naturally been stuck into the dampest and lowest part of their line. Their company dugout was reported to be like a well with a dripping roof. It would take B Company to be afflicted with such quarters.… It was difficult to see what to do—not to drain their quarters, but to exorcise their ill-luck. Still, it would have to be done. He was going into their quarters to make a strafe, but he sent Aranjuez to announce his coming so as to give the decent young Company Commander a chance to redd up his house.…

===Chinese===
细想之下颇为奇怪：人对那些地区居民的仇恨，仿佛划出一道大大的弧线，越过了交战的地面。人们真正恨的，是那里的平民百姓和他们的统治者。如今这些猪猡又让战壕里的可怜鬼挨饿！

他们可憎可恶。德国的战斗人员和他们的情报机构、参谋机关不过是又无聊又滑稽。没完没了的讨厌鬼。因为一想到他们把他干净漂亮的堑壕糟蹋成那副德行，他就火冒三丈。就像你出门一个钟头，把狗留在客厅里。回来发现它把你的沙发垫子撕了个稀烂。你恨不得敲掉它的脑袋。……所以你也想敲掉德国兵的脑袋。可你并不真希望他们遭大难。真要害他们，怎么也比不上叫他们自己住在那座地狱里——永远揣着半空直冒风的肠胃，外加这些肠胃惹出的噩梦——来得狠！流感自然把他们成片撂倒。

再说，德国人正是流感*一准*能放倒的那号人。他们招人烦，因为他们永远一副嘴脸、从不走样。你读他们那些该死的通报，读得你一边龇牙咧嘴一边有点想吐。他们像一幅幅连绵不断的自我讽刺画，而且动不动就歇斯底里。……疑心病。……军官团。……骄傲的德意志陆军。……他光荣的陛下。……丰功伟绩。……这里头可没多少拉格泰姆军队的味儿，而这类东西无时无刻不在往外涌。……疑心病！

拉格泰姆的军队不至于把流感染得这么凶。它既不号自己的道德脉，也不号自己的身体脉。……话虽如此，B 连还是有了流感。准是前天夜里从德国佬那儿招来的。德国佬当时跳进 B 连的壕里压到他们头上，当场就拼了白刃。真是麻烦。B 连真是个麻烦。它不出所料被塞进了全营防线上最潮最低的地段。据说他们的连掩蔽部像口滴水的井。也就 B 连摊得上这种住处，倒霉催的。……难的是想不出怎么办——不是给他们驻地排水，而是给他们驱邪祛晦气。可这事总得办。他要到他们驻地训一顿话，不过先派阿兰胡埃斯去通报驾到，好让那位正派的少年连长有机会收拾收拾屋子。……

===Original===
The beastly Huns! They stood between him and Valentine Wannop. If they would go home he could be sitting talking to her for whole afternoons. That was what a young woman was for. You seduced a young woman in order to be able to finish your talks with her. You could not do that without living with her. You could not live with her without seducing her; but that was the byproduct. The point is that you can’t otherwise talk. You can’t finish talks at street corners; in museums; even in drawing-rooms. You mayn’t be in the mood when she is in the mood—for the intimate conversation that means the final communion of your souls. You have to wait together—for a week, for a year, for a lifetime, before the final intimate conversation may be attained… and exhausted. So that.…

That in effect was love. It struck him as astonishing. The word was so little in his vocabulary.… Love, ambition, the desire for wealth. They were things he had never known of as existing—as capable of existing within him. He had been the Younger Son, loafing, contemptuous, capable, idly contemplating life, but ready to take up the position of the Head of the Family if Death so arranged matters. He had been a sort of eternal Second-in-Command.

Now: what the Hell was he? A sort of Hamlet of the Trenches? No, by God he was not.… He was perfectly ready for action. Ready to command a battalion. He was presumably a lover. They did things like commanding battalions. And worse!

He ought to write her a letter. What in the world would she think of this gentleman who had once made improper proposals to her; balked; said “So long!” or perhaps not even “So long!” And then walked off. With never a letter! Not even a picture postcard! For two years! A sort of a Hamlet all right! Or a swine!

===Chinese===
万恶的德国佬！他们横在他和瓦伦丁·万诺普之间。他们若是肯回家，他就可以整个下午整个下午地坐着跟她谈话。年轻女子就是干这个用的。你诱拐一个年轻女子，为的是能把和她的谈话谈完。不跟她同居，你就谈不完。不同居就办不到；可诱拐只是副产品。要害在于：不这样你们就没法谈话。街头拐角谈不成；博物馆里谈不成；连客厅里也谈不成。她来了兴致你没兴致——那种促膝的密谈，意味着两颗灵魂最后的交融。你们得一起等——等一个星期，等一年，等一辈子，等那场最后的密谈终于实现……并且谈尽。所以说。……

这实际上就是爱。他为此愕然。这个词在他的词汇表里是如此生僻。……爱，野心，发财的欲望。这些事物他从来不晓得存在——不晓得能在自己心里存在。他一直是次子，游手好闲，睥睨一切，干练，闲看人生，但死神若如此安排，随时准备接任家族之长的位置。他一直是某种终身制的二把手。

如今：他到底算个什么东西？某种战壕里的哈姆雷特？不，老天作证，他不是。……他完全准备就绪，随时能上阵。随时能指挥一个营。他大概算个情人。情人这种人就是干指挥营这种事的。还有更出格的！

他该给她写封信。这位先生曾对她有过非分之求；临阵退缩；说了声「回见！」——兴许连「回见！」都没说。然后一走了之。一封信也没有！连张画片明信片都没有！一晃两年！十足是某种哈姆雷特！要不就是头猪！

===Original===
Well, then, he ought to write her a letter. He ought to say: “This is to tell you that I propose to live with you as soon as this show is over. You will be prepared immediately on cessation of active hostilities to put yourself at my disposal; Please. Signed, Xtopher Tietjens, Acting O.C. 9th Glams.” A proper military communication. She would be pleased to see that he was commanding a battalion. Or perhaps she would not be pleased. She was a Pro-German. She loved these tiresome fellows who tore his, Tietjens’, sofa-cushions to pieces.

That was not fair. She was a Pacifist. She thought these proceedings pestilential and purposeless. Well, there were times when they appeared purposeless enough. Look at what had happened to his neat gravel walks. And to the marle too. Though that served the purpose of letting him sit sheltered. In the sunlight. With any number of larks. Someone once wrote:

> “A myriad larks in unison sang o’er her, soaring out of sight!”

That was imbecile really. Larks cannot sing in unison. They make a heartless noise like that produced by the rubbing of two corks one on the other.… There came into his mind an image. Years ago: years and years ago: probably after having watched that gunner torment the fat Hun, because it had been below Max Redoubt.… The sun was now for certain shining on Bemerton! Well, he could never be a country parson. He was going to live with Valentine Wannop!… he had been coming down the reverse side of the range, feeling good. Probably because he had got out of that O.P. which the German guns had been trying to find. He went down with long strides, the tops of thistles brushing his hips. Obviously the thistles contained things that attracted flies. They are apt to after a famous victory. So myriads of swallows pursued him, swirling round and round him, their wings touching; for a matter of twenty yards all round and their wings brushing him and the tops of the thistles. And as the blue sky was reflected in the blue of their backs—for their backs were below his eyes—he had felt like a Greek God striding through the sea.…

===Chinese===
好吧，那么他该给她写封信。他该写：「兹通知阁下：本演出一结束，本人即拟与阁下同居。务请于敌对行动实际终止之顷刻，听候本人调用；切切。署名，克托弗·蒂金斯，第九格拉摩根营代理指挥官（Acting O.C. 9th Glams）。」一份像模像样的公函。她看到他指挥一个营，会高兴的。也说不定不高兴。她亲德。她爱这些讨厌鬼——撕他蒂金斯沙发垫子的这些家伙。

这么说她不公平。她是和平主义者。她认为这些名堂瘟气十足、毫无意义。唉，有时候它们确实显得够没意义的。瞧瞧他那些齐整的砾石小径落了个什么下场。还有泥灰土也完了。不过那倒成全了他，让他能坐在遮蔽之下。晒着太阳。头顶数不清的云雀。有人写过：

> 「万千云雀齐声在她头顶歌唱，越唱越高，直到望不见的天外！」

这实在蠢透了。云雀不可能齐声歌唱。它们发出的是一种没心没肺的聒噪，像两只软木塞互相摩擦出来的声音。……一幅图景浮上他心头。多年以前：很多很多年以前：多半是看着那个炮兵逗弄胖德国佬之后，因为那地方在马克斯多面堡下面。……此刻的阳光准正照着贝默顿！唉，他是当不成乡村牧师了。他要和瓦伦丁·万诺普同居了！……他记得自己正从靶场的背坡往下走，心情舒畅。多半是因为离开了那座德国炮兵一直想找到的观测所（O.P.）。他迈开大步往下走，蓟草的顶子扫着他的胯。显然蓟草里藏着招苍蝇的东西。大捷之后它们向来如此。于是成千上万的燕子追着他，绕着他一圈圈盘旋，翅膀挨着翅膀；方圆二十来码之内，翅膀擦着他，擦着蓟草尖。而蓝天映在它们背上的蓝里——因为它们的背在他视线以下——他觉得自己像一位希腊天神，正大步走过海洋。……

===Original===
The larks were less inspiring. Really, they were abusing the German guns. Imbecilely and continuously, they were screaming imprecations and threats. They had been relatively sparse until just now. Now that the shells were coming back from a mile or so off the sky was thick with larks. A myriad—two myriad—corks at once. Not in unison. Sang o’er him, soaring out of sight!… You might almost say that it was a sign that the Germans were going to shell you again. Wonderful “hinstinck” set by the Almighty in their little bosoms! It was perhaps also accurate. No doubt the shells as they approached more and more shook the earth and disturbed the little bosoms on their nests. So they got up and shouted; perhaps warning each other; perhaps mere defiance of the artillery.

He was going to write to Valentine Wannop. It was a clumsy swine’s trick not to have written to her before. He had proposed to seduce her; hadn’t done it and had gone off without a word.… Considering himself rather a swell, too!

He said:
“Did you get a bit to eat, Corporal?”

The Corporal balanced himself before Tietjens on the slope of the mound. He blushed, rubbing his right sole on his left instep, holding in his right hand a small tin can and a cup, in his left an immaculate towel containing a small cube.

===Chinese===
云雀就不那么提气了。说真的，它们是在骂德国炮。它们蠢不可及、没完没了地尖啸着咒骂和威胁。方才它们还稀稀拉拉。这会儿炮弹从一英里开外回来了，天上便密密麻麻全是云雀。一万只——两万只——软木塞同时作响。并不齐声。在他头顶歌唱，直唱到望不见的天外！……你几乎可以说，这是德国人又要炮轰你的信号。全能的主在它们小小的胸腔里安下的奇妙「本—能」（hinstinck）！这大概也准确。毫无疑问，炮弹越飞越近，震得大地发抖，搅扰了巢里那小小的胸脯。于是它们腾身而起，大声喊叫；也许是互相报警；也许纯粹是对炮兵示威。

他要给瓦伦丁·万诺普写信。先前一直不写，是笨猪一样的行径。他曾求与她苟且；没干成，一言不发就走了。……还自命不凡得很！

他说：
「下士，你自己弄到一口吃的没有？」

下士在土丘坡上、蒂金斯面前站定，身子摆得端端正正。他红着脸，右脚掌在左脚背上蹭来蹭去，右手拎着一个小铁皮罐和一只杯子，左手是一方干干净净的毛巾，包着个小方块。

===Original===
Tietjens debated whether he should first drink of the coffee and army rum to increase his zest for the sandwiches, or whether he should first eat the sandwiches and so acquire more thirst for the coffee.… It would be reprehensible to write to Valentine Wannop. The act of the cold-blooded seducer. Reprehensible!… It depended on what was in the sandwiches. It would be agreeable to fill the void below and inwards from his breastbone. But whether do it first with a solid or warm moisture?

The Lance-Corporal was deft.… He set the coffee tin, cup and towel on a flat stone that stuck out of that heap; the towel unfolded, served as a tablecloth; there appeared three heaps of ethereal sandwiches. He said he had eaten half a tin of warm mutton and haricot beans, whilst he was cutting the sandwiches. The meat in the sandwiches consisted of foie gras, that pile: bully beef reduced to a paste with butter that was margarine, anchovy paste out of a tin and minced onion out of pickles; the third pile was bully beef *nature*, seasoned with Worcester sauce.… All the materials he had at disposal!

Tietjens smiled on the boy at his work. He said this must be a regular chef. The boy said:

“Not a chef, yet, sir!” He had a camp stool hung on his trenching tool behind his hip. He had been chief assistant to one of the chief cooks in the Savoy. He had been going to go to Paris. “What you call a marmiton, sir!” he said. With his trenching tool he was scooping out a level place in front of the flat rock. He set the camp stool on the flattened platform.

Tietjens said:
“You used to wear a white cap and white overalls?”

===Chinese===
蒂金斯盘算着：是先喝咖啡兑军中朗姆，好增进对三明治的胃口，还是先吃三明治，好生出对咖啡更大的渴。……给瓦伦丁·万诺普写信是要不得的。冷血诱拐者的行径。要不得！……这得看三明治里夹的是什么。把胸骨下方、朝里那片虚空填满，会是件惬意的事。可先用来填的是固体，还是热乎的流质？

下士手脚麻利。……他把咖啡罐、杯子和毛巾搁在一块从土堆里凸出来的平石上；毛巾一展开便是桌布；上面出现三小堆仙品似的三明治。他说他切三明治的当口，自己已吃了半罐热羊肉烧白豆。三明治里的馅，这一堆是鹅肝酱；那一堆是罐头咸牛肉搅成的酱，掺着人造黄油，还有听装鳀鱼酱和腌菜里剁出来的洋葱末；第三堆是原味罐头咸牛肉，佐以伍斯特辣酱。……他手头能支配的全套材料也就这些了！

蒂金斯含笑看着忙活的年轻人。他说这准是个正经厨子了。年轻人说：

「还不敢当厨子呢，长官！」他的工兵铲上、胯后挂着一张折叠凳。他原是萨伏伊饭店（Savoy）一位大厨的头号助理。本来还打算去巴黎。「就是您说的那种 marmiton（厨工学徒），长官！」他说。他用工兵铲在平石前面铲出一片平地，把折叠凳安在那小平台上。

蒂金斯说：
「你从前是不是戴白帽子、穿白罩衣？」

===Original===
He liked to think of the blond boy resembling Valentine Wannop dressed all in slim white. The Lance-Corporal said:

“It’s different now, sir!” He stood at Tietjens’ side, always caressing his instep. He regarded cooking as an Art. He would have preferred to be a painter, but Mother hadn’t enough money. The source of supply dried up during the War.… If the C.O. would say a word for him after the War.… He understood it was going to be difficult to get jobs after the War. All the blighters who had got out of serving, all the R.A.S.C., all the Lines of Communication men would get first chance. As the saying was, the further from the Line the better the pay. And the chance, too!

Tietjens said:
“Certainly I shall recommend you. You’ll get a job all right. I shall never forget your sandwiches.” He would never forget the keen, clean flavour of the sandwiches or the warm generosity of the sweet, be-rummed coffee! In the blue air of that April hillside. All the objects on that white towel were defined: with iridescent edges. The boy’s face, too! Perhaps not physically iridescent. His breath, too, was very easy. Pure air! He was going to write to Valentine Wannop: “Hold yourself at my disposal. Please. Signed.…” Reprehensible! Worse than reprehensible! You do not seduce the child of your father’s oldest friend. He said:

“I shall find it difficult enough to get a job after the War!”

===Chinese===
他乐于想象这金发小子像瓦伦丁·万诺普，通身纤瘦的白。下士说：

「如今不一样啦，长官！」他站在蒂金斯身旁，两只脚背仍旧互相摩挲着。他把烹调当作一门艺术。他本来更想当画家，可妈妈钱不够。战争一打响，财源就枯竭了。……要是战后指挥官能替他美言一句。……他听说战后找工作会很难。那些躲了兵役的混蛋们、皇家陆军勤务队（R.A.S.C.）的全体、兵站线上的全体，都会先轮到机会。俗话说得好：离前线越远，饷钱越优。机会也越优！

蒂金斯说：
「我当然会替你写推荐。你准能找到工作。你的三明治我一辈子忘不了。」这三明治清冽干净的风味，这掺了朗姆的甜咖啡那股热腾腾的慷慨，他永远不会忘！在四月山坡的蔚蓝空气里。白毛巾上的一切物什都轮廓分明：镶着虹彩的边。小伙子的脸也是！或许不是物理意义上的虹彩。他的呼吸也舒展。纯净的空气！他要给瓦伦丁·万诺普写信：「务请听候本人调用。切切。署名。……」要不得！比要不得还要不得！你不能诱拐你父亲至交的孩子。他说：

「战后我要找个工作，怕也够难的！」

===Original===
Not only to seduce the young woman, but to invite her to live a remarkably precarious life with him. It isn’t done!

The Lance-Corporal said:
“Oh, sir; no, sir!… You’re Mr. Tietjens, of Groby!”

He had often been to Groby of a Sunday afternoon. His mother was a Middlesbrough woman. Southbank, rather. He had been to the Grammar School and was going to Durham University when… Supplies stopped. On the eight nine fourteen.…

They oughtn’t to put North Riding, Yorkshire, boys in Welsh-traditioned units. It was wrong. But for that he would not have run against this boy of disagreeable reminiscences.

===Chinese===
不但要诱拐那年轻女子，还要请她跟他去过一种朝不保夕到家的日子。这可干不得！

下士说：
「哎呀长官，不会的，长官！……您是格罗比的蒂金斯先生呀！」

他常在星期天下午到格罗比去。他母亲是米德尔斯伯勒人。确切说，是索斯班克人。他上过文法中学，正要去达勒姆大学，这时……接济断了。一九一四年八九月间。……

他们不该把约克郡北赖丁的小伙子塞进威尔士传统的部队。这不对。可若不是这样，他也就不会撞上这个满腹不祥回忆的小子了。

===Original===
“They say,” the boy said, “that the well at Groby is three hundred and twenty feet deep, and the cedar at the corner of the house a hundred and sixty. The depth of the well twice the height of the tree!” He had often dropped stones down the well and listened: they made an astonishingly loud noise. Long: like echoes gone mad! His mother knew the cook at Groby. Mrs. Harmsworth. He had often seen… he rubbed his ankles more furiously, in a paroxysm… Mr. Tietjens, the father, and him, and Mr. Mark and Mr. John and Miss Eleanor. He once handed Miss Eleanor her riding crop when she dropped it.…

Tietjens was never going to live at Groby. No more feudal atmosphere! He was going to live, he figured, in a four-room attic-flat, on the top of one of the Inns of Court. With Valentine Wannop. *Because* of Valentine Wannop!

He said to the boy:
“Those German shells seem to be coming back. Go and request Captain Gibbs as soon as they get near to take his fatigues under cover until they have passed.”

He wanted to be alone with Heaven.… He drank his last cup of warm, sweetened coffee, laced with rum.… He drew a deep breath. Fancy drawing a deep breath of satisfaction after a deep draft of warm coffee, sweetened with condensed milk and laced with rum!… Reprehensible! Gastronomically reprehensible!… What would they say at the Club?… Well, he was never going to be at the Club! The Club claret was to be regretted! Admirable claret. And the cold sideboard!

===Chinese===
「人家说，」小伙子说，「格罗比那口井有三百二十英尺深，房角那棵雪松一百六十英尺高。井深正好是树高的两倍！」他常往井里扔石头听声：那声音响得吓人。悠悠长长：像发疯的回声！他母亲认得格罗比的厨子。哈姆斯沃思太太。他常常见到……他一阵癫狂，发疯似的搓起脚脖子来……蒂金斯老先生，还有他，还有马克先生、约翰先生和埃莉诺小姐。有一回埃莉诺小姐的马鞭掉了，还是他捡起来递给她的。……

蒂金斯再也不打算住格罗比了。再不要什么封建气氛！他打算住——他盘算着——一套四居室的顶楼公寓，就在律师学院（Inns of Court）某一座的顶上。和瓦伦丁·万诺普一起。就*因为*瓦伦丁·万诺普！

他对小伙子说：
「德国炮弹好像又回来了。去请吉布斯上尉，炮弹一挨近，就让他的公差们进掩体避一避，等炮火过去再出来。」

他想独自与上苍待一会儿。……他喝下最后一杯温热的甜咖啡，兑了朗姆的。……他深深吸了一口气。想想看，一大杯加了炼乳的甜咖啡、兑着朗姆下了肚，接着竟心满意足地深吸一口气！……要不得！口腹之欲上要不得！……俱乐部里的人会怎么说？……得了，他反正再不进俱乐部了！只有俱乐部的红酒可惜！顶好的红酒。还有那冷食柜！

===Original===
But, for the matter of that, fancy drawing deep breaths of satisfaction over the mere fact of lying—in command of a battalion!—on a slope, in the clear air, with twenty thousand—two myriad!—corks making noises overhead and the German guns directing their projectiles so that they were slowly approaching! Fancy!

They were, presumably, trying out their new Austrian gun. Methodically, with an infinite thoroughness. If, that is to say, there really was a new Austrian gun. Perhaps there wasn’t. Division had been in a great state of excitement over such a weapon. It stood in Orders that everyone was to try to obtain every kind of information about it, and it was said to throw a projectile of a remarkable, High Explosive efficiency. So Gibbs had jumped to the conclusion that the thing that had knocked to pieces his projected machine-gun emplacement, had been the new gun. In that case they were trying it out very thoroughly.

The actual report of the gun or guns—they fired every three minutes, so that might mean that there was only one and that it took about three minutes to reload—was very loud and rather high in tone. He had not yet heard the actual noise made by the projectile, but the reports from a distance had been singularly dulled. When, presumably, the projectile had effected its landing, it bored extraordinarily into the ground and then exploded with a time-fuse. Very likely it would not be very dangerous to life, but, if they had enough of the guns and the H.E. to plaster the things all along the Line, and if the projectiles worked as efficiently as they had done on poor Gibb’s trench, there would be an end of trench warfare on the Allied side. But, of course, they probably had not either enough guns or enough High Explosive and the thing would very likely act less efficiently in other sorts of soils. They were very likely trying that out. Or, if they were firing with only one gun they might be trying how many rounds could be fired before the gun became ineffective. Or they might be trying only the attrition game: smashing up the trenches which was always useful and then sniping the men who tried to repair them. You could bag a few men in that way, now and then. Or, naturally, with planes.… There was no end to these tiresome alternatives! Presumably, again, our planes might spot that gun or battery. Then it would stop!

===Chinese===
可话又说回来，想想看：不过是躺着——指挥着一个营呢！——在山坡上，在澄净的空气里，头顶两万只——两万个！——软木塞聒噪着，而德国炮兵正校着他们的炮弹、慢慢朝这边逼近，就为这么点事便心满意足地深吸一口气！想想看！

他们多半是在试那门新奥地利炮。有条不紊，细致入微。倘若真有一门新奥地利炮的话。也许并没有。师部为这种武器闹腾得厉害。命令里写着，人人都要设法搜罗它的各种情报；据说它发射的弹丸高爆效能惊人。于是吉布斯一口咬定，把他拟建的机枪掩体轰散架的就是那门新炮。要是那样，他们可试得真够彻底的。

那门炮或那几门炮的实际声响——每三分钟放一发，可见也许只有一门，装弹大约要三分钟——非常响，音调颇高。炮弹实际的啸声他还没听见过，但远处的爆音沉闷得出奇。等炮弹落了地——想必如此——它异常深地往地里钻，然后由定时引信引爆。它对生命多半不会太危险，可他们若是有足够的炮和足够的高爆弹（H.E.），沿全线铺满这玩意儿，而弹丸又都像在可怜的吉布斯壕上那样奏效，协约国这边的堑壕战就该寿终正寝了。当然，他们多半既没有那么多炮，也没有那么多高爆药，而且这东西换到别的土质里很可能就没那么灵了。他们多半也在试这个。又或者，若只用了门单炮，他们可能在试这门炮打多少发之后报废。再或者，他们玩的纯粹是消耗战：把堑壕砸烂——这永远有用——再冷枪狙击那些想修壕的人。这样隔三差五总能捞几个。再不然，自然就是用飞机。……这些讨厌的选项没个穷尽！再者，想必我们的飞机也可能发现那门炮或那个炮组。那它就消停了！

===Original===
Reprehensible!… He snorted! If you don’t obey the rules of your club you get hoofed out, and that’s that! If you retire from the post of Second-in-Command of Groby, you don’t have to… oh, attend battalion parades! He had refused to take any money from Brother Mark on the ground of a fantastic quarrel. But he had not any quarrel with Brother Mark. The sardonic pair of them were just matching obstinacies. On the other hand you had to set to the tenantry an example of chastity, sobriety, probity, or you could not take their beastly money. You provided them with the best Canadian seed corn; with agricultural experiments suited to their soils; you sat on the head of your agent; you kept their buildings in repair; you apprenticed their sons; you looked after their daughters when they got into trouble and after their bastards, your own or another man’s. But you must reside on the estate. *You must reside on the estate.* The money that comes out of those poor devil’s pockets must go back into the land so that the estate and all on it, down to the licensed beggars, may grow richer and richer and richer. So he had invented his fantastic quarrel with Brother Mark: because he was going to take Valentine to live with him. You could not have a Valentine Wannop having with you in a Groby the infinite and necessary communings. You could have a painted doxy from the servants’ hall, quarrelling with the other maids, who would want her job, and scandalising the parsons for miles round. In their sardonic way the tenants appreciated that: it was in the tradition and all over the Riding they did it themselves. But not a lady: the daughter of your father’s best friend! They wanted Quality women to *be* Quality and they themselves would go to ruin, spend their dung-and-seed-money on whores and wreck the fortunes of the Estate, sooner than that you should indulge in infinite conversations.… So he hadn’t taken a penny of their money from his brother, and he wouldn’t take a penny when he in turn became Groby. Fortunately, there was the heir.… Otherwise he could not have gone with that girl!

===Chinese===
要不得！……他嗤了一声！不守俱乐部的规矩，就得被轰出去，就这么回事！你既然辞了格罗比二把手的职，就不必……哦，就不必参加营级阅兵了！他曾以一场异想天开的争吵为名，拒绝从马克兄弟手里拿一分钱。可他和马克兄弟并无争吵。他们那对冷嘲客不过是卯上了犟劲。另一方面，你得给佃户们做出贞洁、清醒、正直的榜样，否则你拿他们那点讨厌钱拿得不安生。你要给他们提供最好的加拿大种玉米；提供合他们土质的农业试验；你要坐镇在你地产代理人的头上；你要让他们的房舍修葺不辍；你要把他们的儿子送去学徒；他们的女儿出了事你要照应，他们的私生子——你自己的或别人种的——你也要照应。但你必须住在庄园里。*你必须住在庄园里。*从那些可怜虫口袋里掏出来的钱，必须回到土地里去，好让庄园和庄园上的一切，直到那些领了执照的叫花子，都变得越来越富、越来越富、越来越富。于是他发明了与马克兄弟的那场异想天开的争吵：因为他要带瓦伦丁去同居。在格罗比，你可没法同一个瓦伦丁·万诺普进行那无穷而必需的心灵交通。你可以从下房里弄来个涂脂抹粉的浪蹄子，跟想抢她差事的女仆们吵翻天，把方圆几英里的牧师们都吓坏。佃户们以他们冷嘲的方式欣赏这个：这是老传统，整个北赖丁的庄主们自己都这么干。可不能是一位淑女：你父亲至交的女儿！他们要上等女人*就得上等女人*，宁可自己败家，把买粪肥种子的钱扔进窑子，把家业搅得倾家荡产，也不能容你沉溺于无穷无尽的谈话。……所以他没从兄弟手里拿他们一分钱，等他日后轮上格罗比，也一分不拿。所幸还有继承人。……不然他可没法跟那姑娘走！

===Original===
Two pangs went through him. His son had never written to him: the girl might have married a War Office clerk! On the rebound! That was what it would be: a civilian War Office clerk would be the most exact contrast to himself!… But the son’s letters would have been stopped by the mother. That was what they did to people who were where *he* was. As the C.O. had said! And Valentine Wannop, who had listened to his conversation, would never want to mingle intimately in another’s! Their communion was immutable and not to be shaken!

So he was going to write to her: freckled, downright, standing square on feet rather widely planted apart, just ready to say: “Oh, *chuck* it, Edith Ethel!”… She made the sunlight!

Or no: by Heavens, he could not write to her! If he stopped one or went dotty.… Wouldn’t it make it infinitely worse for her to know that his love for her had been profound and immutable? It would make it far worse, for by now the edges of passion had probably worn less painful. Or there was the chance of it!… But impenitently he would go on willing her to submit to his will: through mounds thrown up by Austrian projectiles and across the seas. They would do what they wanted and take what they got for it!

He reclined, on his right shoulder, feeling like some immense and absurd statue: a collection of meal-sacks done in mud: with grotesque shorts revealing his muddy knees.… The figure on one of Michelangelo’s Medici tombs. Or perhaps his *Adam*… He felt the earth move a little beneath him. The last projectile must have been pretty near. He would not have noticed the sound, it had become such a regular sequence. But he noticed the quiver in the earth.…

Reprehensible! He said. For God’s sake *let* us be reprehensible! And have done with it! We aren’t Hun strategists forever balancing pros and cons of militant morality!

===Chinese===
两阵刺痛穿身而过。他的儿子从没给他写过信：那姑娘呢，说不定已经嫁了个陆军部文员！赌气反弹嫁的！准是这么回事：一个平民的陆军部文员，正是和他本人最精确的对立！……可儿子的信准是被当妈的截了。他们就是这么对付身处*他*这种境地的人的。指挥官就说过！而瓦伦丁·万诺普，既然听过他的谈话，就绝不会想再亲密地掺和进别人的谈话！他们的交融不可变更、不可动摇！

于是他要给她写信：满脸雀斑，直来直去，两脚分得略宽、四平八稳地站着，正要开口说：「哦，*少来*了，伊迪丝·埃塞尔！」……她是制造阳光的人！

要不，还是不：苍天在上，他不能给她写信！万一他挨了一下，或者疯了。……让她知道他对她的爱一直深沉而不渝，岂不是让她痛上加痛、痛到没边？那只会糟得多，因为到如今，激情的锋刃磨到这会儿，多半已不那么疼了。至少有这个指望！……可他仍要执迷不悟地盼她顺从他的意志：隔着奥地利炮弹掀起的土丘，隔着大海重洋。他们要怎么干就怎么干，后果自负！

他侧过右肩躺着，自觉像一尊庞大而荒谬的雕像：一堆泥塑的粗粉麻袋：滑稽的短裤露出两条泥膝盖。……米开朗琪罗美第奇墓上那些人像之一。或者也许是他的《亚当》……他感到身下的大地微微一动。最后那发炮弹准是落得相当近了。声响他是不会留意的，它已成了那样规律的序列。可他留意到了大地那一颤。……

要不得！他说。看在上帝份上，让咱们*要不得*一回吧！一了百了！咱们又不是德国佬的谋略家，没完没了地掂量斗争道德的正反两面！

===Original===
He took, with his left hand the cup from the rock. Little Aranjuez came round the mound. Tietjens threw the cup downhill at a large bit of rock. He said to Aranjuez’s wistful, enquiring eyes:

“So that no toast more ignoble may ever be drunk out of it!”

The boy gasped and blushed:

“Then you’ve got someone that you love, sir!” he said in his tone of hero-worship. “Is she like Nancy, in Bailleul?”

===Chinese===
他左手从石头上拿起杯子。小阿兰胡埃斯从土丘那边绕过来。蒂金斯把杯子朝坡下一块大石头掷去。他对着阿兰胡埃斯那双恋恋不舍、探询的眼睛说：

「免得往后有更低贱的祝酒，再用它喝过！」

小伙子倒抽一口气，红了脸：

「那么您有了心爱的人了，长官！」他用他那崇拜英雄的腔调说。「她像不像南希，巴约勒的那位？」

===Original===
Tietjens said:
“No, not like Nancy.… Or, perhaps, yes, a little like Nancy!” He did not want to hurt the boy’s feelings by the suggestion that anyone unlike Nancy could be loved. He felt a premonition that that child was going to be hurt. Or, perhaps, it was only that he was already so suffering.

The boy said:
“Then you’ll get her, sir. You’ll certainly get her!”

“Yes, I shall probably get her!” Tietjens said.

===Chinese===
蒂金斯说：
「不像，不像南希。……要不，也许，像，有一点像南希！」他不想伤小伙子的心——暗示有谁不像南希也值得爱，会伤他的心。他有一种预感：这孩子要受伤。又或者，不过是因为他自己已经痛成这样。

小伙子说：
「那您一定会得到她，长官。您准能得到她！」

「是啊，多半能到手！」蒂金斯说。

===Original===
The Lance-Corporal came, too, round the mound. He said that A Company were all under cover. They went all together round the heap in the direction of B Company’s trench down into which they slid. It descended sharply. It was certainly wet. It ended practically in a little swamp. The next battalion had even some yards of sandbag parapet before entering the slope again with its trench. This was Flanders. Duck country. The bit of swamp would make personal keeping in communication difficult. Where Tietjens had put in his tile-syphons a great deal of water had exuded. The young O.C. Company said that they had had to bale the trench out, until they had made a little drain down into the bog. They baled out with shovels. Two of the shovels still stood against the brushwood revetments of the parapet.

“Well, you should not leave your shovels about!” Tietjens shouted. He was feeling considerable satisfaction at the working of his syphon. In the meantime we had begun a considerable artillery demonstration. It became overwhelming. There was some sort of Bloody Mary somewhere a few yards off or so it seemed. She pooped off. The planes had perhaps reported the position of the Austrian gun. Or we might be strafing their trenches to make them shut up that weapon. It was like being a dwarf at a conversation, a conflict—of mastodons. There was so much noise it seemed to grow dark. It was a mental darkness. You could not think. A Dark Age! The earth moved.

He was looking at Aranjuez from a considerable height. He was enjoying a considerable view. Aranjuez’s face had a wrapt expression—like that of a man composing poetry. Long dollops of liquid mud surrounded them in the air. Like black pancakes being tossed. He thought: “Thank God I did not write to her. We are being blown up!” The earth turned like a weary hippopotamus. It settled down slowly over the face of Lance-Corporal Duckett who lay on his side, and went on in a slow wave.

It was slow, slow, slow… like a slowed down movie. The earth manoeuvred for an infinite time. He remained suspended in space. As if he were suspended as he had wanted to be in front of that cockscomb in whitewash. Coincidence!

The earth sucked slowly and composedly at his feet.

===Chinese===
下士也从土丘那边绕了过来。他说 A 连的人全进了掩体。他们一齐绕过土堆，朝 B 连的堑壕走去，滑进壕里。壕下降得陡。的确潮湿。尽头几乎就是一小片沼泽。邻营的堑壕进坡之前，甚至还有几码沙袋胸墙。这就是弗兰德斯。水鸭子之乡。那片小沼泽会妨碍人员彼此联络。蒂金斯埋了瓦管虹吸管的地方，渗出了大量的水。年轻的连长说，他们不得不拿堑壕往外舀水，后来才修了一条小排水沟通进泥沼。舀水用的是铁锹。有两把还立在胸墙的柴束护壁上。

「喂，你们不该把铁锹乱扔乱放！」蒂金斯喊道。他对自己的虹吸管干得那样漂亮，颇为得意。与此同时，我方开始了一场规模可观的炮兵示威。进而排山倒海。几码开外某处仿佛架着一门血腥玛丽。她砰砰地放。也许飞机报出了那门奥地利炮的位置。要不然，就是我们正在炮击他们的堑壕，好让他们闭了那件武器的嘴。这就像一个侏儒旁听乳齿象们谈话——交战。吵得人眼前发黑。那是精神上的黑暗。你没法思考。一个黑暗时代！大地动了。

他正从很高的地方看着阿兰胡埃斯。他正享受一片辽阔的视野。阿兰胡埃斯的脸上有种出神的表情——像一个人正在腹中作诗。一坨坨长长的烂泥围在他们四周的空中。像抛起来的黑煎饼。他想：「谢天谢地，我没给她写信。我们正在被炸上天！」大地像一头疲惫的河马翻了个身。它慢慢压到达克特下士的脸上——他侧身躺着——又化作一道缓慢的浪，继续向前。

慢，慢，慢……像放慢了的银幕。大地机动了无穷无尽的时间。他悬在半空。仿佛他真悬在了那片白灰鸡冠跟前——正是他从前渴望悬着的地方。巧合！

大地从容不迫、慢慢地吸吮他的双脚。

===Original===
It assimilated his calves, his thighs. It imprisoned him above the waist. His arms being free, he resembled a man in a life-buoy. The earth moved him slowly. It was solidish.

Below him, down a mound, the face of little Aranjuez, brown, with immense black eyes in bluish whites, looked at him. Out of viscous mud. A head on a charger! He could see the imploring lips form the words: “Save me, Captain!” He said: “I’ve got to save myself first!” He could not hear his own words. The noise was incredible.

A man stood over him. He appeared immensely tall because Tietjens’ face was on a level with his belt. But he was a small Cockney Tommy really. Name of Cockshott. He pulled at Tietjens’ two arms. Tietjens tried to kick with his feet. Then he realised it was better not to kick with his feet. He was pulled out. Satisfactorily. There had been two men at it. A second, a Corporal had come. They were all three of them grinning. He slid down with the sliding earth towards Aranjuez. He smiled at the pallid face. He slipped a lot. He felt a frightful burning on his neck, below and behind the ear. His hand came down from feeling the place. The finger tips had no end of mud and a little pinkishness on them. A pimple had perhaps burst. He had at least two men not killed. He signed agitatedly to the Tommies. He made gestures of digging. They were to get shovels.

He stood over Aranjuez, on the edge of liquid mud. Perhaps he would sink in. He did not sink in. Not above his boot tops. He felt his feet to be enormous and sustaining. He knew what had happened. Aranjuez was sunk in the issuing hole of the spring that made that bog. It was like being on Exmoor. He bent down over an ineffable, small face. He bent down lower and his hands entered the slime. He had to get on his hands and knees.

Fury entered his mind. He had been sniped at. Before he had had that pain he had heard, he realised, an intimate drone under the hellish tumult. There was reason for furious haste. Or, no.… They were low. In a wide hole. There was no reason for furious haste. Especially on your hands and knees.

===Chinese===
它吞下了他的小腿、他的大腿。它把他圈禁在腰以上。两条胳膊还自由着，他像套在救生圈里的人。大地缓缓地挪动他。它算得上结实。

在他下方，一道土坡之下，小阿兰胡埃斯的脸望着他，棕褐色的脸，蓝白的眼白上一双极大的黑眼睛。从黏泥里冒出来。大银盘上的一颗头颅！他看得见那哀求的唇形，拼出的话是：「救救我，上尉！」他说：「我得先自救！」他听不见自己的话。那噪音不可思议。

一个人站在他身旁居高临下。他显得高不可测，因为蒂金斯的脸正齐着他的腰带。其实是个小个子伦敦东区佬汤米。名叫科克肖特。他拽着蒂金斯的两条胳膊。蒂金斯想用脚蹬。随即明白还是不蹬的好。他被拽了出来。顺顺当当。干这活的有两个人。第二个是个下士。三个人全咧着嘴笑。他随着滑塌的土朝阿兰胡埃斯滑下去。他对那张灰白的脸笑了笑。他连滑了好几跤。他觉得脖子上火烧火燎，在耳朵下面、后面。他的手从那地方摸下来。指尖上糊了没完没了的泥，还有一点粉红。也许破了颗粉刺。他至少保住了两个人没死。他急切地朝汤米们打手势。他做出挖土的动作。他们得去拿铁锹。

他站在阿兰胡埃斯上方，在流泥的边上。说不定会陷下去。他没有陷下去。最深没过靴口。他觉得双脚大得出奇，托得住他。他明白出了什么事。阿兰胡埃斯陷在泉眼里，正是那眼泉积成了这片泥沼。就像身在埃克斯穆尔高地。他朝一张说不出的、小小的脸俯下身去。他俯得更低，双手插进泥浆。他不得不趴下，手脚着地。

狂怒涌进他脑中。他挨了冷枪。在感到那阵灼痛之前，他意识到，他曾在那地狱般的大嚣之下听见一声贴耳的嗡鸣。有理由狂速行事。可又不然。……他们位置低。在一个大坑里。没有理由狂速行事。尤其在你手脚着地的时候。

===Original===
His hands were under the slime, and his forearms. He battled his hands down greasy cloth; under greasy cloth. *Slimy*, not greasy! He pushed outwards. The boy’s hands and arms appeared. It was going to be easier. His face was now quite close to the boy’s, but it was impossible to hear what he said. Possibly he was unconscious. Tietjens said: “Thank God for my enormous physical strength!” It was the first time that he had ever had to be thankful for great physical strength. He lifted the boy’s arms over his own shoulders so that his hands might clasp themselves behind his neck. They were slimy and disagreeable. He was short in the wind. He heaved back. The boy came up a little. He was certainly fainting. He gave no assistance. The slime was filthy. It was a condemnation of a civilisation that he, Tietjens, possessed of enormous physical strength, should never have needed to use it before. He looked like a collection of meal-sacks; but, at least, he could tear a pack of cards in half. If only his lungs weren’t…

Cockshott, the Tommie, and the Corporal were beside him. Grinning. With the two shovels that ought not to have stood against the parapet of their trench. He was intensely irritated. He had tried to indicate with his signs that it was Lance-Corporal Duckett that they were to dig out. It was probably no longer Lance-Corporal Duckett. It was probably by now “it.” The body! He had probably lost a man, after all!

Cockshott and the Corporal pulled Aranjuez out of the slime. He came out reluctantly, like a lugworm out of sand. He could not stand. His legs gave way. He drooped like a flower done in slime. His lips moved, but you could not hear him. Tietjens took him from the two men who supported him between the arms and laid him a little way up the mound. He shouted in the ear of the Corporal: “Duckett! Go and dig out Duckett! At the double!”

He knelt and felt along the boy’s back. His spine might have been damaged. The boy did not wince. His spine might be damaged all the same. He could not be left there. Bearers could be sent with a stretcher if one was to be found. But they might be sniped coming. Probably, he, Tietjens, could carry that boy: if his lungs held out. If not, he could drag him. He felt tender, like a mother, and enormous. It might be better to leave the boy there. There was no knowing. He said: “Are you wounded?” The guns had mostly stopped. Tietjens could not see any blood flowing. The boy whispered: “No, sir!” He was, then, probably just faint. Shell shock, very likely. There was no knowing what shell shock was or what it did to you. Or the mere vapour of the projectile.

He could not stop there.

===Chinese===
他的手在泥浆之下，连同前臂。他的双手顺着油布往下摸，摸进油布底下。是*滑腻*的泥，不是油！他往外推。小伙子的手和胳膊露出来了。事情会好办些了。他的脸此刻凑得极近，可小伙子说什么根本听不见。也许他已经昏了过去。蒂金斯说：「感谢上帝，给了我这一身蛮力！」这是他平生头一回不得不感谢自己气力过人。他把小伙子的双臂搭上自己的肩，好让那双手在他脖后扣拢。那双手黏糊糊的，很不舒服。他气短了。他往后拽。小伙子上来了一点。他肯定正在晕过去。他一点也帮不上忙。泥浆污秽不堪。这是对一个文明的控诉：他蒂金斯空有这一身蛮力，从前竟然一次也不曾需要用过。他看着像一堆粗粉麻袋；可好歹，他能把一副纸牌撕成两半。要是他的肺不……就好了

科克肖特——那个汤米——和下士到了他身旁。咧着嘴笑。扛着那两把本不该立在胸墙上的铁锹。他恼火透了。他打手势本想指明，要他们挖出来的是达克特下士。现在多半已不是达克特下士了。这会儿多半已是「那东西」了。尸体！他说不定终究还是折了一个人！

科克肖特和下士把阿兰胡埃斯从泥浆里拽出来。他老大不情愿地出来，像沙蚕从沙里被拔出来。他站不住。两条腿直打软。他蔫得像一朵在泥浆里泡蔫的花。他嘴唇动着，可你听不见。蒂金斯从那两个架着他胳膊的人手里接过他，把他安放在土坡上方一点的地方。他冲着下士的耳朵喊：「达克特！去把达克特挖出来！跑步去！」

他跪下，顺着小伙子的背摸过去。脊椎也许伤了。小伙子没有缩。脊椎也可能照样伤了。不能把他撂在这儿。要是找得到担架，可以派担架兵来。可他们来时可能挨冷枪。多半他蒂金斯自己能扛动这孩子：只要他的肺顶得住。顶不住，就拖。他心里柔情涌动，像个母亲，又自觉庞大无比。把小伙子留在那儿说不定更好。没个准。他说：「你受伤没有？」炮大半停了。蒂金斯看不见哪儿流血。小伙子低声说：「没有，长官！」那么，他多半只是晕了过去。炮弹休克，很可能。谁也说不清炮弹休克是什么，它把你怎么样。再不就是那颗炮弹的毒雾。

他不能停在那儿。

===Original===
He took the boy under his arm as you might do a roll of blankets. If he took him on his shoulders he might get high enough to be sniped. He did not go very fast, his legs were so heavy. He bundled down several steps in the direction of the spring in which the boy had been. There was more water. The spring was filling up that hollow. He could not have left the boy there. You could only imagine that his body had corked up the spring-hole before. This had been like being at home where they had springs like that. On the moors, digging out badgers. Digging earth drains, rather. Badgers have dry lairs. On the moors above Groby. April sunlight. Lots of sunlight and skylarks.

He was mounting the mound. For some feet there was no other way. They had been in the shaft made by that projectile. He inclined to the left. To the right would take them quicker to the trench, but he wanted to get the mound between them and the sniper. His breathing was tremendous. There was more light falling on them.

Exactly!… *Snap! Snap! Snap!*… Clear sounds from a quarter of a mile away.… Bullets whined. Overhead. Long sounds, going away. Not snipers. The men of a battalion. A chance! *Snap! Snap! Snap!* Bullets whined overhead. Men of a battalion get excited when shooting at anything running. They fire high. Trigger pressure. *He* was now a fat, running object. Did they fire with a sense of hatred or fun! Hatred probably. Huns have not much sense of fun.

His breathing was unbearable. Both his legs were like painful bolsters. He would be on the relatively level in two steps if he made them.… Well, make them!… He was on the level. He had been climbing: up clods. He *had* to take an immense breath. The ground under his left foot gave way. He had been holding Aranjuez in front of his own body as much as he could, under his right arm. As his left foot sank in, the boy’s body came right on top of him. Naturally this stiffish earth in huge clods had fissures in it. Apertures. It was not like regular digging.

The boy kicked, screamed, tore himself loose.… Well, if he wanted to go! The scream was like a horse’s in a stable on fire. Bullets had gone overhead. The boy rushed off, his hands to his face. He disappeared round the mound. It was a conical mound. He, Tietjens, could now crawl on his belly. It was satisfactory.

===Chinese===
他把小伙子夹在腋下，像夹一卷毯子。要是扛上肩，人便高出去一截，容易挨冷枪。他走得不算快，两条腿沉得很。他朝小伙子陷过的那眼泉的方向，连跌带撞下了几级。水更多了。那眼泉正把洼地灌满。真不能把小伙子留在那儿。只能这么想：先前是他的身子塞住了泉眼。这就像回到了家乡，家乡就有这样的泉。在荒原上，掏獾洞。不，更像是挖排水暗沟。獾的窝是干的。在格罗比高地的荒原上。四月的阳光。大把大把的阳光和云雀。

他在往土丘上爬。有那么几步路，别无他途。他们先前待的那坑，是炮弹打出来的竖井。他往左边偏。走右边能更快到堑壕，可他要让土丘挡在他们和那个狙击手之间。他的喘息惊天动地。照到他们身上的光越来越多了。

果然！……*啪！啪！啪！*……四分之一英里外传来的清脆声响。……子弹呼啸。掠过头顶。长长的声音，远去。不是狙击手。是一个营的兵。赶巧了！*啪！啪！啪！*子弹在头顶呼啸。一个营的兵朝任何跑动的东西开枪时都会兴奋。他们打高了。扳机上使的劲。*他*如今成了个跑动的大胖子。他们是带着仇恨还是带着好玩开的枪！多半是仇恨。德国佬没什么好玩之心。

他的喘息叫人受不了。两条腿像两只疼痛的长枕头。再迈两步，他就能站上比较平的地方，只要迈出去。……那就迈吧！……他站上了平地。他一直在攀爬：踩着大土块往上。他*不得不*深深吸一大口气。他左脚下的地面塌了。他一直尽可能把阿兰胡埃斯挡在自己身前，夹在右腋下。左脚一陷，小伙子的身子整个压到了他身上。自然，这种硬邦邦的大块泥土里满是裂缝。窟窿。这可不同于规规矩矩的挖土。

小伙子连踢带踹，尖叫着挣脱了。……好吧，他要是想跑就跑！那尖叫像马厩失火里的马。子弹已从头顶过去了。小伙子冲了出去，双手捂着脸。他绕过土丘不见了。那是座圆锥形的土丘。他，蒂金斯，现在可以匍匐前进了。这倒叫人满意。

===Original===
He crawled. Shuffling himself along with his hips and elbows. There was probably a textbook way of crawling. He did not know it. The clods of earth appeared friendly. For bottom soil thrown to the top they did not feel or smell so very sour. Still, it would take a long time to get them into cultivation or under grass. Probably, agriculturally speaking that country would be in pretty poor condition for a long time.…

He felt pleased with his body. It had had no exercise to speak of for two months—as second-in-command. He could not have expected to be in even the condition he was in. But the mind had probably had a good deal to do with that! He had, no doubt, been in a devil of a funk. It was only reasonable. It was disagreeable to think of those Hun devils hunting down the unfortunate. A disagreeable business. Still, we did the same.… That boy must have been in a devil of a funk. Suddenly. He had held his hands in front of his face. Afraid to see. Well, you couldn’t blame him. They ought not to send out schoolgirls. He was like a girl. Still, he ought to have stayed to see that he, Tietjens, was not pipped. He might have thought he was hit from the way his left leg had gone down. He would have to be strafed. Gently.

Cockshott and the Corporal were on their hands and knees digging with the short-handled shovels that are known as trenching-tools. They were on the rear side of the mound.

“We’ve found ’im, sir,” the Corporal said. “Regular buried. Just seed is foot. Dursen’t use a shovel. Might cut ’im in arf!”

Tietjens said:
“You’re probably right. Give me the shovel!”

===Chinese===
他爬着。靠胯骨和胳膊肘蹭着往前。爬大概是有教范姿势的。他不会。那些大土块显得很友好。作为翻上来的底土，摸着闻着都不算太酸。不过，要把它们养熟成耕地或种上草皮，还得好长时间。多半，从农业上讲，这个国家会贫瘠潦倒好一阵子。……

他对自己的身子板很满意。当二把手的两个月里，它几乎没受过像样的锻炼。他本来连眼下的状态都不能指望。可这多半也有心神的功劳！他无疑怕得要死过一阵。这也合情理。想到那些德国佬魔鬼追猎倒霉蛋，就不痛快。一桩不痛快的营生。不过，我们也干一样的。……那孩子准也是怕得要死。猝不及防的。他曾把双手捂在脸前。不敢看。唉，怪不得他。他们不该把女学生派出来。他就像个姑娘。不过，他到底该留下来看着点，别让他蒂金斯挨了枪子。看他左腿陷下去的样子，他可能以为他中了弹。回头得训他一顿。轻轻地训。

科克肖特和下士手脚着地，正用那种短柄铲挖着——就是所谓的工兵铲。他们在土丘的后坡。

「找着他了，长官，」下士说。「埋得严实。就瞅见个脚。不敢使铲子。怕把他拦腰铲成两半儿！」

蒂金斯说：
「你说得多半对。铲子给我！」

===Original===
Cockshott was a draper’s assistant, the Corporal a milkman. Very likely they were not good with shovels.

He had had the advantage of a boyhood crowded with digging of all sorts. Duckett was buried horizontally, running into the side of the conical mound. His feet at least stuck out like that, but you could not tell how the body was disposed. It might turn to either side or upwards. He said:

“Go on with your tools above! But give me room.”

The toes being to the sky, the trunk could hardly bend downwards. He stood below the feet and aimed terrific blows with the shovel eighteen inches below. He liked digging. This earth was luckily dryish. It ran down the hill conveniently. This man had been buried probably ten minutes. It seemed longer but it was probably less. He ought to have a chance. Probably earth was less suffocating than water. He said to the Corporal:

===Chinese===
科克肖特是布店伙计，下士是送奶工。八成使不好铁锹。

他却占了一样便宜：童年的时光塞满了各式各样的挖掘。达克特是横着被埋的，直插进圆锥土丘的侧壁。至少他的双脚就那么戳在外面，可身子究竟是怎么个方向，说不清。也许朝左歪、朝右歪，也许朝上。他说：

「你们在上头接着干！但给我腾出地方。」

脚趾朝天，躯干便弯不下去多少。他站在双脚下方，照着十八英寸以下的地方，一锹一锹猛砍。他喜欢挖土。这土幸而偏干。顺着坡往下淌，正好省事。这人被埋大约十分钟了。感觉上更久，多半还不到。他该有生机。土多半比水不那么闷死人。他对下士说：

===Original===
“Do you know how to apply artificial respiration?”

“To the drowned?”

Cockshott said:
“I do, sir. I was swimming champion of Islington baths!” A rather remarkable man, Cockshott. His father had knocked up the arm of a man who tried to shoot Mr. Gladstone in 1866 or thereabouts.

A lot of earth falling away, obligingly, after one withdrawal of the shovel Lance-Corporal Duckett’s thin legs appeared to the fork, the knees drooping.

===Chinese===
「你会做人工呼吸吗？」

「给淹的？」

科克肖特说：
「我会，长官。我是伊斯灵顿浴场的游泳冠军！」科克肖特这人相当了不起。他父亲曾一拳磕开过一条胳膊——那人的，1866 年前后想枪击格莱斯顿先生的那个。

铁锹抽出一回，大片泥土殷勤地跟着塌落，达克特下士两条瘦腿露了出来，直到腿根，膝盖耷拉着。

===Original===
Cockshott said:
“’E ain’t rubbin’ ’is ankles this journey!”

The Corporal said:
“Company C’mander is killed, sir. Bullet clean through the ’ed!”

It annoyed Tietjens that here was another head wound. He could not apparently get away from them. It was silly to be annoyed, because in trenches a majority of wounds had to be head wounds. But Providence might just as well be a little more imaginative. To oblige one. It annoyed him, too, to think that he had strafed that boy just before he was killed. For leaving his shovels about. A strafe leaves a disagreeable impression on young boys for quite half an hour. It was probably the last incident in his life. So he died depressed.… Might God be making it up to him!

He said to the Corporal:

===Chinese===
科克肖特说：
「这回他可蹭不成脚脖子喽！」

下士说：
「连长阵亡了，长官。子弹正正穿了脑袋！」

又是一个头部创伤，蒂金斯恼火。他显然躲不开它们。恼火是没道理的，因为在堑壕里，创伤大半就得是头部创伤。可天意也不妨多来点想象力。讨个人喜欢嘛。还有一桩也让他恼火：就在那孩子阵亡前，他还训了他。为乱放铁锹的事。一顿训斥在少年心口上要压上足足半个钟头的疙瘩。那多半是他一生中最后一件事了。他就这么郁郁而终。……愿上帝补偿他！

他对下士说：

===Original===
“Let me come.” Duckett’s left hand and wrist had appeared, the hand drooping and improbably clean, level with the thigh. It gave the line of the body; you could clear away beside him.

“’E wasn’t on’y twenty-two,” the Corporal said.

Cockshott said: “Same age as me. Very particular ’e was about your rifle pull-throughs.”

A minute later they pulled Duckett out, by the legs. A stone might have been resting on his face, in that case his face would have been damaged. It wasn’t, though you had had to chance it. It was black but asleep.… As if Valentine Wannop had been reposing in an ash-bin. Tietjens left Cockshott applying artificial respiration very methodically and efficiently to the prostrate form.

It was to him a certain satisfaction that, at any rate, in that minute affair he hadn’t lost one of the men but only an officer. As satisfaction it was not militarily correct, though as it harmed no one there was no harm in it. But for his men he always felt a certain greater responsibility; they seemed to him to be there infinitely less of their own volition. It was akin to the feeling that made him regard cruelty to an animal as a more loathsome crime than cruelty to a human being, other than a child. It was no doubt irrational.

===Chinese===
「让我来。」达克特的左手和手腕露出来了，手耷拉着，干净得出奇，与大腿齐平。这给出了身体的方向；可以顺着旁边清土了。

「他才二十二呀，」下士说。

科克肖特说：「跟我同岁。您那些步枪的通条，他可上心得很。」

一分钟后，他们拽着双腿把达克特拉了出来。本来也许有块石头压在脸上，那样脸就毁了。幸好没有，尽管这只能碰运气。他一脸黑，却像睡着。……仿佛瓦伦丁·万诺普曾在灰堆里安睡过一场。蒂金斯留下科克肖特，让他对那具直挺的身体极有条理、极有效率地做人工呼吸。

有一件事多少让他满意：在那桩小小的战事里，他没折一个士兵，只折了一个军官。这种满意在军事上并不正确，不过既然无害于人，也就无妨。可他对自己的士兵总多担着一分责任；在他看来，他们在场，几乎全然不是出于自愿。这近似于那种心情——正是它使他把虐待动物看作比虐待人（孩子除外）更可憎的罪。这无疑是不理性的。

===Original===
Leaning, in the communication trench, against the corrugated iron that boasted a great whitewashed A, in a very clean thin Burberry boasting half a bushel of badges of rank—worsted crowns and things!—and in a small tin hat that looked elegant, was a slight figure. How the *devil* can you make a tin hat look elegant! It carried a hunting switch and wore spurs. An Inspecting General. The General said benevolently:
“Who are you?” and then with irritation: “Where the devil is the officer commanding this Battalion? Why can’t he be found?” He added: “You’re disgustingly dirty. Like a blackamoor. I suppose you’ve an explanation.”

Tietjens was being spoken to by General Campion. In a hell of a temper. He stood to attention like a scarecrow.

He said:
“I am in command of this Battalion, sir. I am Tietjens, second-in-command. Now in command temporarily. I could not be found because I was buried. Temporarily.”

===Chinese===
交通壕里，一道瘦削的身影倚在波纹铁板上，那铁板自夸着一个刷白的大大的 A；来人穿一件非常干净的单薄博柏利雨衣，自夸着半蒲式耳的军衔徽章——绒线皇冠之类！——戴一顶小钢盔，看着竟很雅致。钢盔怎么能弄出雅致*劲儿*来，见鬼！他手里拿着一根猎鞭，靴上戴着马刺。一位视察将军。将军和颜悦色地说：
「你是谁？」随即恼怒起来：「指挥这个营的军官到底在哪儿？怎么找不着他？」他又补一句：「你脏得叫人恶心。像个黑摩尔人。我料你有话解释。」

跟他说话的是坎皮恩将军。正一肚子邪火。他立正站着，像一尊稻草人。

他说：
「我现在指挥这个营，长官。我是蒂金斯，二把手。目前临时指挥。刚才找不到我，是因为我被埋了。暂时性的。」

===Original===
The General said:
“You.… Good God!” and fell back a step, his jaw dropping. He said: “I’ve just come from London!” And then: “By God, you don’t stop in command of a Battalion of mine a second after I take over!” He said: “They said this was the smartest battalion in my unit!” and snorted with passion. He added: “Neither my galloper nor Levin can find you or get you found. And there you come strolling along with your hands in your pockets!”

In the complete stillness, for, the guns having stopped, the skylarks, too, were taking a spell, Tietjens could hear his heart beat, little dry scraping sounds out of his lungs. The heavy beats were very accelerated. It gave an effect of terror. He said to himself:
“What the devil has his having been in London to do with it?” And then: “He wants to marry Sylvia! I’ll bet he wants to marry Sylvia!” That was what his having been to London had to do with it. It was an obsession with him: the first thing he said when surprised and passionate.

They always arranged these periods of complete silence for the visits of Inspecting Generals. Perhaps the Great General Staffs of both sides arrange that for each other. More probably our guns had split themselves in the successful attempt to let the Huns know that we wanted them to shut up—that we were firing with what Papists call a special intention. That would be as effective as a telephone message. The Huns would know there was something up. Never put the other side in a temper when you can help it.

He said:

===Chinese===
将军说：
「你。……老天爷！」倒退一步，下巴都耷拉下来。他说：「我刚从伦敦回来！」接着说：「上帝作证，我一接任，你就一秒钟也别想再指挥我的营！」他说：「他们还说这是我部队里最利落的营！」怒不可遏地哼了一声。又补一句：「我的传令官也好，列文也好，都找不着你，也支使不动人找你。你倒好，揣着手闲逛过来！」

四下里一片死寂——炮停了，云雀也歇了口气——蒂金斯听得见自己的心跳，听见肺里发出细小而干涩的刮擦声。沉重的搏跳急促得很。听着叫人一阵恐怖。他对自己说：
「他到伦敦去过，跟这有什么相干？」接着：「他想娶西尔维娅！我打赌他想娶西尔维娅！」他去过伦敦，指的就是这个。这是他的一块心病：他一吃惊、一动怒，头一句就是这个。

他们总要为视察将军们安排这种万籁俱寂的时段。说不定两边的大总参谋部就是这么彼此互相安排的。多半是我方炮兵豁出去嗓子，总算让德国佬明白我们要他们闭嘴——我们正怀着天主教徒所谓的「特定意向」开炮。这跟打电话一样管用。德国佬就知道有事要发生了。能不把对面惹毛，就千万别惹。

他说：

===Original===
“I’ve just had a scratch, sir. I was feeling in my pockets for my field-dressing.”

The General said:
“A fellow like you has no right to be where he can be wounded. Your place is the lines of communication. I was mad when I sent you here. I shall send you back.”

He added:
“You can fall out. I want neither your assistance nor your information. They said there was a damn smart officer in command here. I wanted to see him.… Of the name of… Of the name of… It does not matter. Fall out.…”

Tietjens went heavily along the trench. It came into his head to say to himself:

===Chinese===
「我刚挂了点小彩，长官。我正往口袋里摸绷带包呢。」

将军说：
「你这种人没资格待在会挂彩的地方。你该待的地方是兵站线。我把你派到这儿来是发了疯。我要把你送回去。」

他又说：
「你退下吧。你的协助和你的情报我都不要。他们还说这儿指挥的是个鬼精明的军官。我本来要见见他。……姓什么来着……姓什么来着……无所谓了。退下。……」

蒂金斯拖着沉重的步子沿堑壕走去。他脑子里冒出一句自语：

===Original===
“It *is* a land of Hope and Glory!” Then he exclaimed: “By God! I’ll take the thing before the Commander-in-Chief. I’ll take the thing before the King in Council if necessary. By God I will!” The old fellow had no business to speak to him like that. It was importing personal enmity into service matters. He stood still reflecting on the terms of his letter to Brigade. The Adjutant Notting came along the trench. He said:
“General Campion wants to see you, sir. He takes over this Army on Monday.” He added: “You’ve been in a nasty place, sir. Not hurt, I trust!” It was a most unusual piece of loquacity for Notting.

Tietjens said to himself:
“Then I’ve got five days in command of this unit. He can’t kick me out before he’s in command.” The Huns would be through them before then. Five days’ fighting! Thank God!

He said:
“Thanks. I’ve seen him. No, I’m all right. Beastly dirty!”

===Chinese===
「这*真是*一片希望与光荣的土地！」随即他嚷出声：「上帝作证！这事我要闹到总司令面前。必要的话，闹到御前枢密院去。上帝作证，我一定办到！」老家伙没资格那样跟他说话。这是把私仇掺进公务。他站住，琢磨着给旅部那封信的措辞。副官诺丁沿堑壕走过来。他说：
「坎皮恩将军要见您，长官。他星期一接掌这个集团军。」他又说：「您刚才待的地方够呛，长官。没伤着吧，我希望！」对诺丁来说，这是破天荒的一回多话。

蒂金斯心里说：
「那我还有五天指挥这支部队。他没接掌指挥权，就踢不走我。」五天之内德国佬就会打穿他们。五天的仗！感谢上帝！

他说：
「谢谢。我见过他了。没事，我好着呢。就是脏得邪乎！」

===Original===
Notting’s beady eyes had a tinge of agony in them. He said:
“When they said you had stopped one, sir, I thought I should go mad. We *can’t* get through the work!”

Tietjens was wondering whether he should write his letter to Brigade before or after the old fellow took over. Notting was saying:
“The doctor says Aranjuez will get through all right.”

It would be better, if he were going to base his appeal on the grounds of personal prejudice.

“Of course he will lose his eye. In fact it… it is not practically there. But he’ll get through.”

===Chinese===
诺丁那双圆眼睛里含着一丝痛苦。他说：
「他们说您中了一下的时候，长官，我以为我要疯了。公务我们*实在*忙不过来！」

蒂金斯正在琢磨，给旅部的信该在老家伙接任前写还是接任后写。诺丁正说：
「军医说阿兰胡埃斯能挺过来，没事。」

这样更好——倘若他打算把申诉立在个人偏见的理由上的话。

「当然，他会失去那只眼睛。其实它……它实际上已经不在了。可他会挺过来的。」

## Part III / 第三部

### I / 第一章

===Original===

Coming into the Square was like being suddenly dead, it was so silent and so still to one so lately jostled by the innumerable crowd and deafened by unceasing shouts. The shouting had continued for so long that it had assumed the appearance of being a solid and unvarying thing: like life. So the silence appeared like Death; and now she had death in her heart. She was going to confront a madman in a stripped house. And the empty house stood in an empty square all of whose houses were so eighteenth century and silver-grey and rigid and serene that they ought all to be empty too and contain dead, mad men. And was this the errand? For today when all the world was mad with joy? To become bear-ward to a man who had got rid of all his furniture and did not know the porter—mad without joy!

It turned out to be worse than she expected. She had expected to turn the handle of a door of a tall empty room; in a space made dim with shutters she would see him, looking suspiciously round over his shoulder, a grey badger or a bear taken at its dim occupations. And in uniform. But she was not given time even to be ready. In the last moment she was to steel herself incredibly. She was to become the cold nurse of a shell-shock case.

But there was not any last moment. He charged upon her. There in the open. More like a lion. He came, grey all over, his grey hair—or the grey patches of his hair—shining, charging down the steps, having slammed the hall door. And lopsided. He was carrying under his arm a diminutive piece of furniture. A cabinet.

It was so quick. It was like having a fit. The houses tottered. He regarded her. He had presumably checked violently in his clumsy stride. She hadn’t seen because of the tottering of the houses. His stone-blue eyes came fishily into place in his wooden countenance—pink and white. *Too* pink where it was pink and too white where it was white. Too much so for health. He was in grey homespuns. He should not wear homespuns or grey. It increased his bulk. He could be made to look… Oh, a fine figure of a man, let us say!

===Chinese===

走进广场，就像骤然间死了过去——对才被人山人海推搡过、被不停的呼喊震聋了耳朵的人来说，这里实在太静、太寂了。呼喊持续了那么久，久到俨然成了一件坚实不变的东西：像生命本身。于是这寂静便像死亡；而此刻她心里正揣着死亡。她要走进一幢被搬空了的宅子，去面对一个疯子。空宅立在空荡荡的广场上，四周的房舍全都那样十八世纪、银灰、刚硬、安详，倒仿佛理应也全都空着、全都装着死了的疯子。难道这就是她的差事？在普天同疯同乐的今天？给一个把家具卖了个精光、连门房都认不出的男人当熊倌——疯，却不带欢乐的疯！

结果比她预想的更糟。她原本以为会是这样：拧开一间高旷空屋的门把手；在百叶窗滤暗的光线里看见他，狐疑地回头张望，一只灰獾，或一头熊，正埋头干它幽暗里的营生。而且穿着军装。可人家连让她定下心来的工夫都没给。到最后关头，她还得不可思议地硬起心肠。她本要做弹震症病例的冷静看护。

可根本没有什么最后关头。他朝她直冲过来。就在露天里。更像一头狮子。他来了，浑身灰的，他的灰头发——或者说头发里的灰斑——发着亮，砰地摔上厅门，一路冲下台阶。而且身子歪向一边。他腋下夹着一件小巧的家具。一只橱柜。

这一切快得很。像癫痫发作。房屋都在摇晃。他端详她。想必他是在自己那笨拙的大步里猛地刹住的。她没看见，因为房屋在摇晃。他那石板蓝的眼睛，鱼一般呆滞地各就各位，嵌在木然的脸上——粉是粉，白是白。粉的地方*太*粉，白的地方太白。就健康而言，太过分了。他穿一身灰色手织粗呢。他不该穿粗呢，也不该穿灰的。那显得他块头更大。他本可以拾掇成……哦，不妨就说，堂堂一表男子汉！

===Original===

What was he doing? Fumbling in the pocket of his clumsy trousers. He exclaimed—she shook at the sound of his slightly grating, slightly gasping voice:—

“I’m going to sell this thing.… Stay here.” He had produced a latchkey. He was panting fiercely beside her. Up the steps. He was beside her. Beside her. Beside her. It was infinitely sad to be beside this madman. It was infinitely glad. Because if he had been sane she would not have been beside him. She could be beside him for long spaces of time if he were mad. Perhaps he did not recognise her! She might be beside him for long spaces of time with him not recognising her. Like tending your baby!

He was stabbing furiously at the latchhole with his little key. He *would*: that was normal. He was a stab-the-keyhole sort of clumsy man. She would not want that altered. But she would see about his clothes. She said: “I am deliberately preparing to live with him for a long time!” Think of that! She said to him:

“Did you send for me?”

He had the door open: he said, panting—His *poor* lungs!:

“No.” Then: “Go in!” and then: “I was just going.…”

She was in his house. Like a child.… He had not sent for her.… Like a child faltering on the sill of a vast black cave.

===Chinese===

他在干什么？在笨拙的裤袋里摸索。他开了口——听见他那微微沙哑、微微气喘的嗓音，她浑身一颤：——

「我要去把这个卖掉。……你在这儿等着。」他掏出了一把弹簧锁钥匙。他在她身边猛烈地喘着气。上台阶。他在她身边。在身边。在身边。在这个疯子身边，是无限的悲哀。也是无限的欢喜。因为倘若他神志清明，她就不会在他身边。他若是疯的，她便可以长长久久待在他身边。说不定他认不出她呢！他认不出她，她也能长长久久待在他身边。就像照看自己的婴孩！

他正拿那把小钥匙狠命往锁眼里戳。他就是这样的人：这才正常。他属于那种笨手笨脚对着锁眼乱戳的男人。这一点她不愿被改变。可他的衣裳她要管一管。她说：「我正在深思熟虑地准备跟他长久过下去！」想想这个！她对他说：

「是你叫人找我的吗？」

他已把门打开：他说，喘着气——他那*可怜*的肺！：

「不是。」接着：「进去！」再接着：「我正要出去。……」

她进了他的宅子。像个孩子。……他没有叫人来找她。……像个孩子，在一座巨大黑洞洞的洞穴门槛上畏畏缩缩。

===Original===

It *was* black. Stone flags. Pompeian red walls scarred pale-pink where fixed hall-furniture had been removed. Was it *here* she was going to live?

He said, panting, from behind her back:

“Wait here!” A little more light fell into the hall. That was because he was gone from the doorway.

He was charging down the steps. His boots were immense. He lolloped all over on one side because of the piece of furniture he had under his arm. He was grotesque, really. But joy radiated from his homespuns when you walked beside him. It welled out; it enveloped you.… Like the warmth from an electric heater, only that did not make you want to cry and say your prayers—the haughty oaf.

===Chinese===

里面真黑。石板地面。庞培红（Pompeian red）的墙上留着一道道浅粉的疤，那是固定的厅堂家具被拆走的地方。她要住的就是这里吗？

他说，喘着气，在她背后：

「在这儿等着！」厅里落进多一点儿的光。那是因为他已经不在门口了。

他正冲下台阶。他的靴子大得吓人。因为腋下夹着那件家具，他整个人朝一边一拐一拐。他实在怪诞，真的。可你走在他身边时，欢喜从他那一身粗呢里放射出来。它涌出来；它把你裹住。……像电暖气散出的暖，只是那暖不会叫你想哭、想祷告——这个傲慢的呆子。

===Original===

No, but he was not haughty. Gauche, then! No, but he was not gauche.… She could not run after him. He was a bright patch, with his pink ears and silver hair. Gallumphing along the rails in front of the eighteenth century houses. *He* was eighteenth century all right.… But then the eighteenth century never went mad. The only century that never went mad. Until the French Revolution: and that was either not mad or not eighteenth century.

She stepped irresolutely into the shadows; she returned irresolutely to the light.… A long hollow sound existed: the sea saying: *Ow, Ow, Ow* along miles and miles. It was the armistice. It was Armistice Day. She had forgotten it. She was to be cloistered on Armistice Day! Ah, not cloistered! Not cloistered there. My beloved is mine and I am his! But she might as well close the door!

She closed the door as delicately as if she were kissing him on the lips. It was a symbol. It was Armistice Day. She ought to go away; instead she had shut the door on… Not on Armistice Day! What was it like to be… changed!

No! She ought not to go away! She ought not to go away! She ought *not*! He had told her to wait. She was not cloistered. This was the most exciting spot on the earth. It was not her fate to live nunlike. She was going to pass her day beside a madman; her night; too.… Armistice Night! That night would be remembered down unnumbered generations. Whilst one lived that had seen it the question would be asked: What did you do on Armistice Night? My beloved is mine and I am his!

===Chinese===

不，他并不傲慢。那么，是笨拙！不，他也不笨拙。……她没法追上去。他是明亮的一点，粉红的耳朵，银灰的头发。在十八世纪宅第前的围栏边一路笨拙欢快地大步颠行。*他*十足是十八世纪的。……可十八世纪从来不曾发疯。唯一不曾发疯的世纪。直到法国大革命为止：而那场革命要么不算疯，要么已不算十八世纪。

她迟疑不定地跨进暗影里；又迟疑不定地回到光亮处。……一种悠长空洞的声响存在着：大海在说：*噢，噢，噢*，绵延数英里又数英里。那是停战。那天是停战日。她把它忘了。她竟要在停战日过与世隔绝的日子！啊，不算隔绝！在那儿不算。良人属于我，我也属于我的良人！可她还是把门关上的好！

她关上门，动作轻柔，仿佛在亲吻他的嘴唇。这是一个象征。这天是停战日。她本该走开；她却把门关上，关在门外的是……不是把停战日关在外面！被……改变，那是怎样一种滋味！

不！她不该走开！她不该走开！她*不*该！他叫她等着来着。她不是被隔绝。这是全世界最叫人激动的地方。修女似的过日子，不是她的命。她要在疯子身边度过她的白天；还有她的夜晚。……停战之夜！那一夜将被世世代代、无数代人铭记。只要还有一个亲历者活着，人们就会问：停战之夜你干了什么？良人属于我，我也属于他！

===Original===

The great stone stairs were carpetless: to mount them would be like taking part in a procession. The hall came in straight from the front door. You had to turn a corner to the right before you came to the entrance of a room. A queer arrangement. Perhaps the eighteenth century was afraid of draughts and did not like the dining-room door near the front entrance.… My beloved is… Why does one go repeating that ridiculous thing. Besides it’s from the Song of Solomon, isn’t it? The Canticle of Canticles! Then to quote it is blasphemy when one is… No, the essence of prayer is volition, so the essence of blasphemy is volition. She did not want to quote the thing. It was jumped out of her by sheer nerves. She was afraid. She was waiting for a madman in an empty house. Noises whispered up the empty stairway!

She was like Fatima. Pushing open the door of the empty room. He might come back to murder her. A madness caused by sex obsessions is not infrequently homicidal.… What did you do on Armistice Night? “I was murdered in an empty house!” For, no doubt he would let her live till midnight.

But perhaps he had not got sex-obsessions. She had not the shadow of a proof that he had; rather that he hadn’t! Certainly, rather that he hadn’t. Always the gentleman.

===Chinese===

宽大的石楼梯上没有铺地毯：走上去就像参加一场列队仪式。门厅从前门笔直通进来。你得向右拐个弯，才到一间屋子的入口。一个古怪的格局。也许十八世纪怕穿堂风，不喜欢餐厅的门挨着正门。……良人属于我……人为什么总这样重复那句可笑的话。再说那出自《雅歌》，不是吗？《歌中之歌》！那么人正处于……这时引用它，便是渎神了。不，祷告的本质在于意志，所以渎神的本质也在于意志。她并不想引用那句话。它是被纯粹的神经从她身上惊出来的。她害怕。她正在一幢空宅里等一个疯子。种种声响在空楼梯上一路耳语上来！

她像法蒂玛（Fatima，「蓝胡子」式故事中窥探禁室的妻子）。推开空屋的门。他说不定会回来杀了她。由性执念引起的疯病，往往不乏杀人的。……停战之夜你干了什么？「我在一幢空宅里被人谋杀了！」因为，毫无疑问，他会容她活到午夜。

可他也许并没有性执念。他有没有，她连一丝一毫的证据也没有；倒不如说他没有！肯定不如说他没有。永远是个正人君子。

===Original===

They had left the telephone! The windows were duly shuttered but in the dim light from between cracks the nickel gleamed on white marble. The mantelshelf. Pure Parian marble, the shelf supported by rams’ heads. Singularly chaste. The ceilings and rectilinear mouldings in an intricate symmetry. Chaste, too. Eighteenth century. But the eighteenth century was not chaste.… *He* was eighteenth century.

She ought to telephone to her mother to inform that Eminence in untidy black with violet tabs here and there of the grave step that her daughter was…

What was her daughter going to do?

She ought to rush out of the empty house. She ought to be trembling with fear at the thought that he was coming home very likely to murder her. But she wasn’t. What was she? Trembling with ecstasy? Probably. At the thought that he was coming. If he murdered her.… Can’t be helped! She was trembling with ecstasy all the same. She must telephone to her mother. Her mother might want to know where she was. But her mother never *did* want to know where she was. She had her head too screwed on to get into mischief!… Think of *that*!

===Chinese===

他们把电话留下了！窗都照章上了百叶，但从板缝透进的微光里，镍件在白色大理石上发亮。壁炉台。纯白的帕罗斯大理石（Parian），台面由几个公羊头托着。出奇地素净。天花板和直线形的线脚，构成繁复的对称。也是素净的。十八世纪。可十八世纪并不贞洁。……*他*才是十八世纪。

她该给母亲打个电话，把这一重大步骤告知那位一身不整洁的黑衣、东一处西一处缀着紫罗兰色饰带的「殿下」：她的女儿就要……

她的女儿要干什么呢？

她本该冲出这幢空宅。一想到他回家来很可能要杀她，她本该吓得发抖。可她没有。她这是怎么了？欢喜得发抖？多半是。一想到他要回来。倘若他杀了她。……那也没办法！她照样欢喜得发抖。她必须给母亲打电话。母亲也许想知道她在哪儿。可母亲从来*不*想知道她在哪儿。她的脑袋瓜螺丝拧得太紧，闯不出祸来！……想想*这个*！

===Original===

Still, on such a day her mother might like to. They ought to exchange gladnesses that her brother was safe for good now. And others, too. Normally her mother was irritated when she rang up. She would be at her work. It was amazing to see her at work. Perhaps she never would again. Such untidiness of papers. In a little room. Quite a little room. She never would work in a big room because a big room tempted her to walk about and she could not afford the time to walk about.

She was writing at two books at once now. A novel.… Valentine did not know what it was about. Her mother never let them know what her novels were about till they were finished. And a woman’s history of the War. A history by a woman for women. And there she would be sitting at a large table that hardly left room for more than getting round it. Grey, large, generous-featured and tired she would be poking over one set of papers on one side of the table or just getting up from over the novel, her loose pince-nez falling off; pushing round the table between its edge and the wall to peer at the sheets of the woman’s history that were spread all over that region. She would work for ten minutes or twenty-five or an hour at the one and then for an hour and a half or half an hour or three-quarters at the other. What a muddle her dear old head must be in!

With a little trepidation she took the telephone. It had got to be done. She could not live with Christopher Tietjens without first telling her mother. Her mother ought to be given the chance of dissuading. They say you ought to give a lover a chance of a final scene before leaving him or her for good. Still more your mother. That was jannock.

===Chinese===

不过，在这样的日子，母亲也许乐意的。弟弟从此永远平安了，她们该互道欢喜。还有别的人也都平安。平日里她一打电话，母亲就烦躁。她准是在工作。看她工作是一桩奇观。也许再也见不着了。那样凌乱的稿纸。在一间小屋里。相当小的一间屋。她从不在大屋里工作，因为大屋诱人踱步，而她踱不起那个工夫。

她如今同时在写两本书。一部小说。……瓦伦丁不知道写的是什么。母亲从不让他们提前知道她的小说写什么，总要等写完了才说。还有一部女性的战争史。一部女人写给女人看的历史。她就这样坐在一张大桌旁，桌子大得只留下绕着走的余地。头发灰白、身量高大、五官宽厚而倦怠的她，不是伏在桌子一侧的一叠稿纸上拨弄，就是刚从小说那摊子上抬起身，松垮的夹鼻眼镜往下滑；再侧着身子在桌沿与墙之间挤过去，查看摊满那半边的战争史稿纸。她会在这本上干十分钟，或二十五分钟，或一个钟头，再到那本上干一个半钟头，或半个钟头，或三刻钟。她那颗可爱的老脑袋，准是一团乱麻！

她略带惶恐地拿起电话。这事非做不可。不先告诉母亲，她就不能同克里斯托弗·蒂金斯一起生活。应当给母亲一个劝阻的机会。人们说，你要永远离开一位恋人，总该给他或她一场最后告别戏的机会。对母亲就更该如此。这才叫公道（jannock，英格兰北方土语：公道、正派）。

===Original===

It broke the word of promise to the ear, the telephone!… Was it blasphemy to quote Shakespeare when one was going to.… Perhaps bad taste. Shakespeare, however, was not spotless. So they said.… Waiting! Waiting! How much of one’s life wasn’t spent waiting, with one’s weight boring one’s heels into the ground.… But *this* thing was dead. No roar came from its mouth and when you jabbed the little gadget at the side up and down no bell tinked.… It had probably been disconnected. They had perhaps cut him off for not paying. Or he had cut it off so that she might not scream for the police through it whilst he was strangling her. Anyhow they were cut off. They would be cut off from the world on Armistice Night.… Well, they would probably be cut off for good!

What nonsense. He had not known that she was coming. He had not asked her to come.

So, slowly, slowly she went up the great stone staircase, the noises all a-whispering up before her… “So, slowly, slowly she went up and slowly looked about her. Henceforth take warning by the fall.…” Well, she did not need to take warning: she was not going to fall in the way Barbara Allen did. Contrariwise!

===Chinese===

这电话，把对耳朵许下的诺言撕毁了！……人正处于……这时引用莎士比亚，算不算渎神？也许只是趣味欠雅。不过莎士比亚也不是一尘不染的。人家是这么说的。……等！等！一个人的生涯，有多少不是耗在等待里，体重把脚跟生生磨进地里。……可*这*东西是死的。它嘴里发不出吼声，你把旁边那个小机关上上下下捅了又捅，也没有铃响。……多半是拆了线。他们也许因为他欠费，把线掐了。要不就是他自己剪了线，好叫他掐她脖子的时候，她不能对着电话喊警察。反正他们被切断了。停战之夜，他们将与全世界隔绝。……嗯，他们多半要永远隔绝了！

多荒唐。他并不知道她要来。他没有请她来。

于是，慢慢地，慢慢地，她登上宽大的石楼梯，种种声响在她前面一路耳语着上去……「就这样，慢慢地，慢慢地，她走了上去，缓缓环顾四周。后人当以这场倾覆为鉴。」（《芭芭拉·艾伦》民谣）哼，她不需要引以为戒：她不会像芭芭拉·艾伦那样倾覆。恰恰相反！

===Original===

He had not sent for her. He had not asked Edith Ethel to ring her up. Then presumably she felt humiliated. But she did not feel humiliated! It was in effect fairly natural. He *was* quite noticeably mad, rushing out, lopsided, with bits of furniture under his arm and no hat on his noticeable hair. Noticeable! That was what he was. He would never pass in a crowd!… He *had* got rid of all his furniture as Edith Ethel had alleged. Very likely he had not recognised the porter, too. She, Valentine Wannop, had seen him going to sell his furniture. Madly! Running to do it. You do not run when you are selling furniture if you are sane. Perhaps Edith Ethel had seen him running along with a table on his head. And she was by no means certain that he had recognised her, Valentine Wannop!

So Edith Ethel might have been almost justified in ringing her up. Normally it would have been an offence, considering the terms on which they had parted. Considering that Edith Ethel had accused her of having had a child by this very man! It was pretty strong, even if she had seen him running about the Square with furniture, and even if there had been no one else who could help.… But she ought to have sent her miserable rat of a husband. There was no excuse!

Still, there had been nothing else for her, Valentine to do. So there was no call for her to feel humiliated. Even if she had not felt for this man as she did she would have come, and, if he had been very bad, would have stayed.

He had not sent for her! this man who had once proposed love to her and then had gone away without a word and who had never so much as sent her a picture-postcard! Gauche! Haughty! Was there any other word for him? There could not be. Then she ought to feel humiliated. But she did not.

===Chinese===

他没有叫人来找她。他没有托伊迪丝·埃塞尔打电话给她。如此说来，她想必该觉得受辱。可她并不觉得受辱！这实在再自然不过。他*明明白白地*疯了，冲出门去，身子歪斜，腋下夹着几件家具，一头惹眼的头发上连帽子也没戴。惹眼！他就是这样的人。他在人群里永远藏不住！……他果然像伊迪丝·埃塞尔说的那样，把家具都处理掉了。多半他也没认出门房来着。她，瓦伦丁·万诺普，亲眼看见他跑去卖他的家具。疯了似的！跑着去办这件事。一个神志清明的人，卖家具的时候是不会跑的。说不定伊迪丝·埃塞尔看见过他顶着一张桌子沿广场跑。而且她一点也拿不准，他到底认没认出她，瓦伦丁·万诺普！

这么一说，伊迪丝·埃塞尔打电话给她，几乎情有可原了。换了平常，那就是冒犯——想想她们不欢而散时的那些情形。想想伊迪丝·埃塞尔曾指责她给这个男人生过一个孩子！这就相当过分了，即便她看见过他夹着家具在广场上乱跑，即便再没有别人能帮忙。……可她也该把她那只可怜的耗子似的丈夫派来。这里没有借口可讲！

话虽如此，她，瓦伦丁，当时也别无选择。所以谈不上受辱。即便她对这个人没有如今这份感情，她也会来；倘若他病得很重，她还会留下。

他没有叫人来找她！这个人，曾经向她求过爱，随后一言不发地走掉，后来连一张图画明信片都没给她寄过！笨拙！傲慢！还有别的词形容他吗？不可能有了。那么她该觉得受辱。可她并不。

===Original===

She felt frightened, creeping up the great staircase, and entering a great room. A very great room. All white; again with stains on the walls from which things had been removed. From over the way the houses confronted her, eighteen-centuryishly. But with a touch of gaiety from their red chimney pots… And now she was spying: with her heart in her mouth. She was terribly frightened. This room was inhabited. As if set down in a field, the room being so large, there camped.… A camp-bed for the use of officers, G.S. one, as the saying is. And implements of green canvas, supported on crossed whitewood staves: a chair, a bucket with a rope handle, a washing-basin, a table. The bed was covered over with a flea-bag of brown wool. She was terribly frightened. The further she penetrated the house the more she was at his mercy. She ought to have stayed downstairs. She was spying on him.

These things looked terribly sordid and forlorn. Why did he place them in the centre of the room? Why not against a wall? It is usual to stand the head of a bed against a wall when there is no support for the pillows. Then the pillows do not slip off. She would change.… No, she would not. He had put the bed in the centre of the room because he did not want it to touch walls that had been brushed by the dress of.… You must not think bad things about that woman!

They did not look sordid and forlorn. They looked frugal. And glorious! She bent down and drawing down the flea-bag at the top, kissed the pillows. She would get him linen pillows. You would be able to get linen now. The war was over. All along that immense line men could stand up!

===Chinese===

她心怀恐惧，蹑手蹑脚登上大楼梯，走进一个大房间。非常大的房间。一片白；墙上又是拆走东西留下的疤。对面的房舍与她相向而立，一派十八世纪的做派。但烟囱帽是红的，添了一分欢快……此刻她这是在窥探：心都提到了嗓子眼。她怕得厉害。这房间是有人住的。房间这么大，仿佛置身旷野，当中扎着营。……一张军官用行军床，G.S. 通用制式（General Service）的，俗话说得好。还有一套绿帆布家什，架在交叉的白木杆上：一把椅子，一只拴着绳提手的吊桶，一只洗脸盆，一张桌子。床上罩着一条棕色羊毛的旧铺盖（flea-bag，士兵对铺盖的谑称）。她怕得厉害。她往宅子深处走得越远，就越任他摆布。她本该待在楼下。她这是在窥探他。

这些东西看上去寒酸透顶、凄惶透顶。他为什么把它们摆在房间正中央？为什么不靠墙？枕头无处倚靠的时候，床照例是床头贴墙放的。枕头就不会滑落。她要给他挪一挪。……不，她不挪。他把床摆在房间中央，是因为他不愿让它挨着曾被那女人的裙裾拂过的墙壁。……你可不许对那个女人起坏念头！

它们看上去并不寒酸凄惶。它们显得清俭。而且光荣！她弯下腰，把床头那头的铺盖掀开一点，吻了吻那对枕头。她要给他置办亚麻枕套。如今买得到亚麻布了。战争结束了。沿着那条漫无尽头的大战线，人都能站起来了！

===Original===

At the head of the room was a dais. A box of square boarding, like the model-throne artists have in studios. Surely she did not receive her guests on a dais: like Royalty. She was capable… *You must not.…* It was perhaps for a piano. Perhaps she gave concerts. It was used as a library now. A row of calf-bound books stood against the wall on the back edge of the platform. She approached them to see what books he had selected. They must be the books he had read in France. If she could know what books he had read in France she would know what some of his thoughts there had been. She knew he slept between very cheap cotton sheets.

Frugal and glorious. That was he! And he had designed this room to love her in. It was the room she would have asked.… The furnishing… Alcestis never had.… For she, Valentine Wannop, was of frugal mind, too. And his worshipper. Having reflected glory.… Damn it, she was getting soppy. But it was curious how their tastes marched together. He had been neither haughty nor gauche. He had paid her the real compliment. He had said: “Her mind so marches with mine that she will understand.”

The books were indeed a job lot. Their tops ran along against the wall like an ill-arranged range of hills; one was a great folio in calf, the title indented deep and very dim. The others were French novels and little red military text books. She leaned over the dais to read the title of the tall book. She expected it to be Herbert’s *Poems* or his “Country Parson”.… *He* ought to be a Country Parson. He never would be now. She was depriving the church of.… Of a Higher Mathematician, really. The title of the book was *Vir. Obscur*.

===Chinese===

房间头上有一座矮台。一方木板拼的台子，像画家画室里的模型宝座。想必她总不至于在矮台上接待宾客：跟王侯似的。她倒做得出来……*你可不许。……*那台子也许是放钢琴的。也许她开过音乐会。如今它当书房用。一排犊皮封面的小书靠墙立在台子后沿。她走近去看他挑了些什么书。那些想必是他在法国读过的书。她若能知道他在法国读过什么书，就能推知他在那边有过哪些念头。她知道，他睡在很便宜的棉布被单之间。

清俭而光荣。这就是他！而这间屋子，是他设计来爱她的。这正是她会开口要的屋子。……这陈设……阿尔刻斯提斯（Alcestis，欧里庇得斯悲剧中代夫赴死的贤妻）也不曾有过。……因为她，瓦伦丁·万诺普，也是个心境清俭的人。而且是他崇拜着的人。分得他折射的荣光。……见鬼，她多愁善感起来了。可奇怪的是，他们的趣味竟这样齐头并进。他既不傲慢，也不笨拙。他给了她真正的敬意。他说过：「她的心智与我这样齐头并进，她自会明白。」

这批书确实是杂凑的一堆。书顶贴着墙沿高高低低，像一列排得凌乱的山峦；其中一部是犊皮面的对开本大书，书名压印得极深，又极模糊。其余是些法国小说和红色小开本的军事教科书。她俯身越过矮台，去读那部大书的书名。她料想那是赫伯特的《诗集》，或是他的《乡村牧师》。……*他*本该当个乡村牧师。如今永远当不成了。她正使教会失去一位……其实是一位高等数学家。那部书的书名是《无名之士》（Vir. Obscur.，拉丁文 vir obscurus）。

===Original===

Why did she take it that they were going to live together? She had no official knowledge that he wanted to. But *they* wanted to **talk**. You can’t talk unless you live together. Her eye, travelling downwards along the dais caught words on paper. They threw themselves up at her from among a disorder of half a dozen typed pages; they were in big, firm, pencilled letters. They stood out because they were pencilled; they were:

*A man could stand up on a bleedin’ ’ill!*

Her heart stopped. She must be out of condition. She could not stand very well, but there was nothing to lean on to. She had—she didn’t know she had—read also the typed words:

> “Mrs. Tietjens is leaving the model cabinet by Barker of Bath which she believes you claim.…”

===Chinese===

她凭什么认定他们就要共同生活了？他有没有这个意思，她并无正式的凭据。可*他们*要**说话**。不住在一起就没法说话。她的目光沿着矮台向下扫，撞上了纸上的字。那字从五六页凌乱的打字稿里自己跳到她眼前；是粗壮有力的铅笔字。因为是铅笔写的，所以格外醒目；写的是：

*人总算能在那该——的山头上站起来啦！*

她的心停跳了。她准是体力不济。她几乎站不住，可又没有可以倚靠的东西。她已经——她并不知道已经——读了那些打出来的字：

> 「兹悉：蒂金斯夫人现将巴斯的巴克（Barker of Bath，著名家具行）所制那件模型橱柜转让他人，她相信阁下声言对该橱柜拥有权利。……」

===Original===

She looked desperately away from the letter. She did not want to read the letter. She could not move away. She believed she was dying. Joy never kills.… But it.… “*fait peur.*” “Makes afraid.” Afraid! Afraid! Afraid! There was nothing now between them. It was as if they were already in each others’ arms. For surely the rest of the letter must say that Mrs. Tietjens had removed the furniture. And his comment—amazingly echoing the words she had just thought—was that he could stand up. But it wasn’t in the least amazing. My beloved is mine.… Their thoughts marched together; not in the least amazing. They could now stand on a hill together. Or get into a little hole. For good. And talk. Forever. She must not read the rest of the letter. She must not be certain. If she were certain she would have no hope of preserving her.… Of remaining.… Afraid and unable to move. She would be forced to read the letter because she was unable to move. Then she would be lost. She looked beseechingly out of the window at the house-fronts over the way. They were friendly. They would help her. Eighteenth century. Cynical, but not malignant. She sprang right off her feet. She could move then. She hadn’t had a fit.

Idiot. It was only the telephone. It went on and on. *Drrinn; drinnnn; d.r.R.I.n.n.* It came from just under her feet. No, from under the dais. The receiver was on the dais. She hadn’t consciously noticed it because she had believed the telephone was dead. Who notices a dead telephone?

She said—It was as if she were talking into his ear, he so pervaded her—she said:

“Who are you?”

===Chinese===

她绝望地把目光从信上挪开。她不想读这封信。她又挪不动地方。她觉得自己快要死了。欢喜从来不杀人。……可它……「*fait peur*」（法语：「叫人害怕」）。「叫人害怕」。害怕！害怕！害怕！如今他们之间再没有隔阂了。仿佛他们已经彼此拥在怀里。因为信的其余部分，必定是说蒂金斯夫人已把家具搬走了。而他的批注——竟与她方才心里的字句遥相呼应——是：人能站起来了。可这毫不奇怪。良人属于我。……他们的心意齐头并进；毫不奇怪。他们如今可以一同站上山头了。或者钻进一个小洞穴。从此不出来。说话。永远。她切不可读信的其余部分。她切不可确定无疑。她若确定了，就再无希望保全她的……保全她的完好。……害怕，动弹不得。她将被迫读那封信，因为她动弹不得。那她就完了。她恳求似的望向窗外，望着对面的屋宇。它们是友善的。它们会帮她。十八世纪。愤世，却不恶毒。她整个人跳了起来。这么说，她能动。她没犯病。

傻瓜。不过是电话罢了。它响个不停。*叮铃；铃铃铃铃；叮、铃、铃、铃。*声音就在她脚底下。不，在矮台底下。听筒搁在矮台上。她先前没有留意它，因为她一直当那电话是死的。谁会留意一部死电话？

她说——仿佛正贴着他的耳朵说话，他如此弥漫了她全身——她说：

「你是谁？」

===Original===

One ought not to answer all telephone calls, but one does so mechanically. She ought not to have answered this. She was in a compromising position. Her voice might be recognised. Let it be recognised. She desired to be known to be in a compromising position! What did you do on Armistice Day!

A voice, heavy and old said:

“You *are* there, Valentine.…”

She cried out:

“Oh, poor *mother*.… But he’s not here.” She added “He’s not been here with me. I’m still only waiting.” She added again: “The house is empty!” She seemed to be stealthy, the house whispering round her. She seemed to be whispering to her mother to save her and not wanting the house to hear her. The house was eighteenth century. Cynical. But not malignant. It wanted her undoing but it knew that women liked being… ruined.

===Chinese===

电话不该每一通都接，可人总是机械地接了。这一通她本不该接。她的处境说不清道不明。她的嗓音可能被听出来。听出来好了。她巴不得让人知道，她正处在一个说不清道不明的境地！停战日你干了什么！

一个沉重而苍老的声音说：

「你*果然*在那儿，瓦伦丁。……」

她叫出声来：

「哦，可怜的*妈妈*。……可他不在这儿。」她补一句：「他没同我一起在这儿待过。我还只是在等着。」她又补一句：「房子是空的！」她像是行迹诡秘，房子在她四周耳语。她像是在悄悄求母亲救她，又不想让房子听见。这房子是十八世纪的。愤世。但不恶毒。它巴不得她身败名裂，可它知道，女人们喜欢被……毁了名节。

===Original===

Her mother said, after a long time:

“Have you *got* to do this thing?… My little Valentine… My little Valentine!” She wasn’t sobbing.

Valentine said:

“Yes, I’ve got to do it!” She sobbed. Suddenly she stopped sobbing.

She said quickly:

“Listen mother. I’ve had no conversation with him. I don’t know even whether he’s sane. He appears to be mad.” She wanted to give her mother hope. Quickly. She had been speaking quickly to get hope to her mother as quickly as possible. But she added: “I believe that I shall die if I cannot live with him.”

===Chinese===

隔了许久，母亲说：

「你*非*做这件事不可吗？……我的小瓦伦丁……我的小瓦伦丁！」她没有啜泣。

瓦伦丁说：

「是的，我非做不可！」她啜泣起来。忽然她又止住了。

她飞快地说：

「听着，妈妈。我跟他还一句话都没说上。我甚至不知道他神志清不清明。他看着像疯了。」她想给母亲留一点希望。快快地。她方才讲得那么快，就是为了尽快把希望递给母亲。可她又添了一句：「我相信，若不能跟他一起生活，我会死的。」

===Original===

She said that slowly. She wanted to be like a little child trying to get truth home to its mother.

She said:

“I have waited too long. All these years.” She did not know that she had such desolate tones in her voice. She could see her mother looking into the distance with every statement that came to her, thinking. Old and grey. And majestic and kind.… Her mother’s voice came:

“I have sometimes suspected.… My poor child.… It has been for a long time?” They were both silent. Thinking. Her mother said:

“There isn’t any practical way out?” She pondered for a long time. “I take it you have thought it all out. I know you have a good head and you are good.” A rustling sound. “But I am not level with these times. I should be glad if there were a way out. I should be glad if you could wait for each other. Or perhaps find a legal.…”

===Chinese===

这句话她说得很慢。她想像个小孩，努力把真相送进母亲心里。

她说：

「我等得太久了。这么多年。」她不知道自己的嗓音里竟有这样荒凉的调子。她能看见，母亲随着传过去的每一句话遥望远方，思索着。苍老而灰暗。庄严而仁慈。……母亲的声音传来：

「我有时也起过疑心。……我可怜的孩子。……已经很久了吗？」两人都沉默了。各自思索。母亲说：

「就没有一条实际的出路吗？」她沉思良久。「我料想你已经把一切都想透了。我知道你有个好脑袋，人又善良。」一阵窸窣声。「可我跟不上这个时代了。倘若有一条出路，我会高兴。倘若你们能彼此等待，我会高兴。或者，或许能寻出一条合法的……」

===Original===

Valentine said:

“Oh, mother, don’t cry!”.… “Oh mother I can’t.…”… “Oh, I will come.… Mother I will come back to you if you order it.” With each phrase her body was thrown about as if by a wave. She thought they only did that on the stage. Her eyes said to her:

> … “Dear Sir,
> “Our Client, Mrs. Christopher Tietjens of Groby-in-Cleveland.…”

===Chinese===

瓦伦丁说：

「哦，妈妈，别哭！」……「哦妈妈，我不能……」……「哦，我会回来……妈妈，只要你吩咐，我就回到你身边。」每说一句，她的身子就像被一个浪头打晃一下。她原以为这种事只有舞台上才有。她的眼睛对她说：

> ……「敬启者：
> 「我们的当事人，克利夫兰郡格罗比的克里斯托弗·蒂金斯夫人……」
===Original===

They said:

> “After the occurrence at the Base-Camp at.…”

They said:

> “Thinks it useless.…”

===Chinese===

它们说：

> 「自……后方营地发生之事……

它们说：

> 「认为无补于事。……」

===Original===

She was agonised for her mother’s voice. The telephone hummed in E flat. It tried B. Then it went back to E flat. Her eyes said:

“Proposes when occasion offers to remove to Groby…” in fat, blue typescript. She cried agonisedly:

“Mother. Order me to come back or it will be too late…”

She had looked down, unthinkingly… as one does when standing at the telephone. If she looked down again and read to the end of the sentence that contained the words: “It is useless,” it would be too late! She would know that his wife had given him up!

Her mother’s voice came, turned by the means of its conveyance into the voice of a machine of Destiny.

“No I can’t. I am thinking.”

===Chinese===

她为母亲的声音痛苦不堪。电话嗡嗡作响，是降E调。它试了试B调。然后又回到降E调。她的眼睛说：

「一俟时机成熟，拟迁往格罗比……」——胖胖的蓝色打字字样。她痛苦万分地喊道：

「妈妈。吩咐我回来吧，不然就太迟了……」

她已经低头看过一眼，未假思索……人站在电话前都会这样。她若再低头一次，把那句含着「无补于事」字样的句子读完，就太迟了！她就会知道，他的妻子已经放弃他了！

母亲的声音传来，被传导的器械转成一部命运机器的声音。

「不，我不能。我在思索。」

===Original===

Valentine placed her foot on the dais at which she stood. When she looked down it covered the letter. She thanked God. Her mother’s voice said:

“I cannot order you to come back if it would kill you not to be with him.” Valentine could feel her late-Victorian advanced mind, desperately seeking for the right plea—for any plea that would let her do without seeming to employ maternal authority. She began to talk like a book: an august Victorian book; Morley’s *Life of Gladstone*. That was reasonable: she wrote books like that.

She said they were both good creatures of good stock. If their consciences let them commit themselves to a certain course of action they were probably in the right. But she begged them, in God’s name to assure themselves that their consciences *did* urge that course. She *had* to talk like a book!

Valentine said:

“It is nothing to do with conscience.” That seemed harsh. Her mind was troubled with a quotation. She could not find it.
